# Sūrah al-Naḥl (Chapter 16) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:1

> **The command of Allah is at hand, so do not hasten it. Glorified and Exalted is He above what they associate ˹with Him in worship˺!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Past Tense Used For A Future That Will Not Fail**

The sūrah opens with a verb in the past: atā — it has come. The subject is amr Allāh, the command or the decisive matter of Allah. Grammatically the event still lies ahead, yet it is spoken of as already accomplished. Arabic does this when certainty is absolute. Al-Ṭabarī reads the past here as taḥqīq, a way of saying the arrival is fixed and nothing will turn it back. Al-Rāzī notes the same device in other passages where the Hour is described as having drawn near. The effect on a Makkan listener was direct. They had heard warnings before and measured them by the calendar of their own impatience. By putting the verb in the past, the Qur'an removes the calendar from human hands. What Allah has decreed moves on its own schedule. The verse therefore does not argue about dates. It announces a reality and then forbids the response that turns warning into sport. Do not seek to hurry it. The prohibition touches both mockery and anxiety, both the scoffer who says bring it and the believer who wishes it would come quickly to settle accounts.

**Why The Command Covers More Than One Event**

Commentators differ on what amr means here, and the difference is instructive because both senses live in the word. Amr can mean a spoken command, as in kun fa-yakūn, and it can mean a matter, an affair that Allah brings about. Ibn Kathīr gathers reports that point in two directions. Some early authorities understood the command as the Hour itself, the Resurrection when every soul stands for reckoning. Others understood it as the worldly punishment that was already moving toward Quraysh, the defeat and breaking of their power that would become visible at Badr. The two readings support each other rather than compete. In Qur'anic usage the coming of Allah's amr often marks a turning point where denial can no longer be maintained, whether that turning point is the death of an individual, the collapse of a town, or the final gathering. Verses like 40:78, where judgment is passed when Allah's command comes, and 11:40, where the flood begins when the command arrives, show the pattern. Al-Qurṭubī observes that al-Naḥl will return to both horizons again and again: verses about the Hour, verses about destroyed peoples, verses about the moment angels take the soul. All are instances of one law: when Allah's decisive matter arrives, excuses end.

**The Mockery That Prompted The Prohibition**

Reports preserved by al-Wāḥidī and others link this opening to a specific Makkan habit. When the Prophet ﷺ recited that the Hour has drawn near, some listeners paused and waited, half expecting the sky to split that afternoon. When nothing visible happened, they turned waiting into mockery and said, if you are truthful, hasten it for us. The Qur'an records this demand in several forms: *They say, when will this promise be, if you are truthful* appears in 10:48, 21:38, 27:71, 34:29, 36:48, and the bolder challenge *If this is the truth from You, rain down stones upon us* appears in 8:32. The answer in this verse does not accept the premise. It says the command is at hand and forbids seeking haste. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the wisdom: to demand haste is to confuse delay with cancellation. A creditor who grants time has not forgiven the debt. Moreover, haste would harm the very people who ask for it. If Allah hastened evil for people as they hasten good, their term would already be ended, as 10:11 says. Every additional day is a space where a heart may turn, where a wrong may be repaired, where a witness may become a believer. The prohibition therefore carries mercy inside severity.

**Human Haste As A Described Weakness**

The Qur'an names haste as part of human makeup. *Man was created of haste* (21:37) and *man is ever hasty* in prayer for good and ill (17:11). This is not a passing remark. It explains why warnings are mocked and why relief is demanded before its time. The impatient person wants the future now, whether that future is punishment for his enemies or reward for himself. The Qur'an corrects both. To the scoffer it says, you will not hurry Allah. To the distressed believer it says, Allah's timing contains knowledge you do not have. In al-Naḥl this theme will return in verses about those who were wronged and then emigrated, who were told to be patient, and in the closing counsel to respond to harm with patience rather than excess. The opening thus sets a moral posture for the whole sūrah: do not live by the clock of impatience. Live by the clock of trust. The command is coming. Your task is not to pull it forward but to be ready when it arrives, and readiness in the Qur'an always means acknowledgment of Allah alone and deeds that match that acknowledgment.

**Glorified Above What They Associate**

The second half of the verse shifts from threat to transcendence: subḥānahu wa-taʿālā ʿammā yushrikūn. Subḥān declares Allah free of every defect and need. The root suggests swift movement away, as if the mind swims away from any unworthy thought. Taʿālā adds height: exalted far above. The phrase ʿammā yushrikūn carries two meanings that both apply. Allah is exalted above the partners themselves — the stones, idols, and imagined intercessors that cannot create, cannot hear, cannot know when they will be raised — and He is exalted above the act of association, which does not touch Him but only harms those who do it. This closing is not decoration. It explains why the demand for haste is foolish at its root. The Makkans imagined Allah as one power among others, whose threats could be tested like a rival's. If He is subḥān, beyond likeness and need, then His command does not compete for space. Nothing beside Him can delay it, prevent it, or shield anyone from it. The same seal appears again in 16:3 and elsewhere in the Qur'an, for example 10:18 and 30:40, always after a mention of creation or judgment, to remind the listener that tawḥīd is not one doctrine among many but the frame that makes sense of all the rest.

**The Name Al-Niʿam And The Place Of This Verse In The Sūrah**

Al-Qurṭubī reports that some early scholars called this chapter Sūrat al-Niʿam, the chapter of blessings, because no other Makkan sūrah counts Allah's gifts so patiently. That name helps place verse 1. Before counting blessings, the sūrah must clear the ground of mockery. A heart that treats Allah's promise as a joke will not see milk, honey, wool, and stars as signs; it will see them as mere things. So the sūrah begins with awe, then moves to gratitude. The sequence matters. The command that is at hand gives weight to every blessing that follows: cattle, horses, rain, crops, night and day, sea, mountains, landmarks, stars. Each is presented as coming from the One who is glorified above partners and whose command cannot be hurried. Linguistically, the insertion markers ˹with Him in worship˺ in the translation reflect the translators' effort to make clear that association here is in worship, not in essence. The Arabic yushrikūn already implies association in worship and lordship. Classical Arabic lexicons list sh-r-k as sharing or assigning a share, and in Qur'anic usage it almost always denotes giving to another what belongs to Allah alone in worship or obedience.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:2

> **He sends down the angels with revelation by His command to whoever He wills of His servants, ˹stating:˺ “Warn ˹humanity˺ that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Me, so be mindful of Me ˹alone˺.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From The Command That Comes To The Spirit That Descends**

Verse 1 spoke of Allah's amr arriving. Verse 2 explains how that amr reaches human beings: yunazzilu al-malā'ikata bi-l-rūḥ min amrihi. He sends down the angels with the rūḥ, the spirit, from His command. The verb yunazzilu is in the present tense, indicating a repeated, ongoing act. Revelation is not a single event locked in the past; it is a sending down that Allah does whenever He wills, to whomever He wills among His servants. The pairing of amr in both verses is deliberate. The same decisive authority that brings judgment also brings guidance, and guidance comes first. Before the command that ends history, there is the command that gives history its meaning. Al-Ṭabarī notes that rūḥ here is understood by most early commentators as revelation itself, called spirit because it gives life to hearts as spirit gives life to bodies. Al-Rāzī adds that just as a body without spirit is dead, a community without revelation is dead though it walks and trades.

**Why Revelation Is Called Rūḥ**

The Qur'an uses rūḥ for several related realities, and the commentators distinguish them carefully. Sometimes rūḥ is the angel Jibrīl himself, called al-Rūḥ al-Amīn, the Trustworthy Spirit. Sometimes it is revelation, because revelation animates. Sometimes it is the secret of life that humans know little about, as in 17:85: *They ask you about the spirit. Say, the spirit is from the command of my Lord, and you have not been given knowledge except a little* (17:85). The verse here brings two of those senses together: angels descend, and they carry rūḥ. The angel is the carrier, the rūḥ is what is carried. Al-Qurṭubī points out that calling revelation a spirit teaches something about how it works. A spirit does not merely inform; it revives. A person may know many facts and still be spiritually lifeless. When revelation enters, intentions change, fear and hope find their proper objects, and the heart begins to move toward its Creator. This is why the same root appears in 42:52: *And thus We have revealed to you a spirit from Our command. You did not know what the Book was nor what faith was, but We made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants* (42:52). The verse in al-Naḥl therefore defines the purpose of prophethood in one image: dead hearts brought to life by a descent from above.

**Angels As Bearers, Not Authors**

Yunazzilu al-malā'ikata — He sends down the angels. The plural suggests the host that accompanies revelation, though the direct bearer is Jibrīl. The Qur'an elsewhere names him explicitly: *He brings it down upon your heart by Allah's permission* (2:97). The point of mentioning angels in the plural is to show the honor and protection around the message. Revelation does not drift down like rumor; it is carried by pure, obedient beings whose only task is to execute Allah's command. Ibn Kathīr notes that this guards against the Makkan claim that the Prophet ﷺ invented the message or learned it from a human teacher. Angels are not of this earth. Their descent marks the message as coming from beyond human craft. At the same time, the phrase *to whoever He wills of His servants* removes any human claim to control who receives it. Prophethood is not earned by lineage, wealth, or eloquence. It is a selection, an iṣṭifā', and Allah knows best where to place His message, as 6:124 says. The Makkans had asked, *Why was this Qur'an not sent down upon a great man from the two towns* (43:31), meaning a rich leader of Makkah or Ṭā'if. The answer is in this verse: choice belongs to the One who sends, not to those who receive.

**The Core Message In One Sentence**

What do the angels say when they descend? An anḏirū annahu lā ilāha illā anā fa-ittaqūn — warn that there is no god worthy of worship except Me, so be mindful of Me alone. This is the summary of every messenger's call. Al-Ṭabarī cites 21:25: *We did not send any messenger before you except that We revealed to him, there is no god but Me, so worship Me* (21:25). The command anḏirū, warn, shows that revelation begins with warning before promise, because a heedless heart does not feel need for promise until it feels danger. The warning is not about tribal defeat or poverty; it is about the foundation of worship. Lā ilāha illā anā — there is no deity except Me. The pronoun anā, I, is direct, without intermediary, to stress that Allah speaks about Himself. Then comes fa-ittaqūn — so be mindful of Me, fear Me, guard yourselves by reverence for Me. Taqwā comes from w-q-y, to shield or protect. It is not mere terror; it is the carefulness of one who knows a danger is real and takes protection. Here protection is found in turning to Allah alone. The two halves of the message mirror the two halves of the shahādah: negation of all false gods, affirmation of Allah alone, then the practical consequence in conduct.

**A Verse That Defines Prophethood For The Whole Sūrah**

Placed at the beginning of Sūrat al-Niʿam, this verse tells the reader how to understand every blessing that follows. Cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, honey — all will be listed as gifts. But the greatest gift is the one that tells you who gave the gifts and what He asks in return. Without revelation, a person may enjoy blessings while forgetting the Bestower, which is the very definition of ingratitude that this sūrah condemns. With revelation, the same blessings become signs. Al-Rāzī makes this connection explicit: the physical blessings sustain the body, the blessing of revelation sustains the spirit, and the second is greater because the body perishes while the spirit returns to its Lord. The verse also establishes a criterion for evaluating any claim to guidance. Any teaching that does not center on lā ilāha illā Allah is not from the rūḥ, even if it carries spiritual language. The angels descend only with tawḥīd. That is the test.

**Lexical Note On Nazzala And Taqwā**

The verb nazzala is in the form II, which often indicates repeated or gradual descent, as opposed to anzala which can indicate a single sending down. Many commentators note that the Qur'an uses nazzala for the gradual revelation over twenty-three years, which matches the historical reality of the Prophet's experience. The command ittaqūn is plural, addressed to humanity through the messengers. The translators add ˹alone˺ in brackets to make clear that mindfulness here means mindfulness of Allah exclusively, not a general carefulness. In classical Arabic, ittaqā with direct object means to fear or be wary of that thing itself, so ittaqūn here means fear Me, not merely fear My punishment. This nuance matters because it places the motive in the person of Allah, not only in consequences. The heart that knows who Allah is will naturally guard against what displeases Him.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:3

> **He created the heavens and the earth for a purpose. Exalted is He above what they associate with Him ˹in worship˺!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation With Truth, Not As Play**

Khalqa al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa bi-l-ḥaqq — He created the heavens and the earth in truth, for a purpose. The phrase bi-l-ḥaqq appears repeatedly in the Qur'an to counter the idea that the world is accidental, purposeless, or a game. Al-Rāzī explains ḥaqq here as having three layers: existence that is real rather than imaginary, wisdom that is purposeful rather than vain, and justice that will be fulfilled rather than ignored. The heavens and earth are not scenery; they are a court where truth will be established. The verse echoes 44:38-39: *We did not create the heavens and earth and what is between them in play. We did not create them except in truth* (44:38-39). And 30:8: *Do they not reflect within themselves? Allah did not create the heavens and earth and what is between them except in truth and for an appointed term* (30:8). By placing this immediately after the mention of angels descending with revelation, the sūrah connects two creations: the physical cosmos and the guidance that explains its purpose. One without the other leaves a person with eyes but no light.

**Why The Heavens Are Mentioned Before The Earth**

The order samāwāt then arḍ is consistent across the Qur'an. It reflects both observable magnitude and Qur'anic cosmology where the heavens are the larger, encompassing structure. Linguistically, samāwāt is plural, indicating vastness and layers. The Qur'an elsewhere says *He created seven heavens in layers* (67:3). Earth is singular, the immediate dwelling. Together they denote totality — everything high and low, distant and near. Al-Qurṭubī notes that beginning with heavens establishes Allah's power before discussing human ingratitude; if He holds the immense heavens, He certainly holds the small idols people trust. The phrase also refutes two opposite errors. Against those who thought the world eternal and uncreated, it says khalaqa — He created, it had a beginning. Against those who thought it created but without purpose, it says bi-l-ḥaqq — with truth, with purpose. Both corrections are needed for tawḥīd to stand.

**The Seal That Returns: Exalted Above Association**

Taʿālā ʿammā yushrikūn — Exalted is He above what they associate with Him. This is the same seal as in verse 1, repeated here to bind creation to transcendence. If Allah alone created heavens and earth, then associating others with Him in worship contradicts the very world those others inhabit. The idols did not create a single heaven or a grain of earth, yet they are given a share of devotion that belongs to the Creator. The Qur'an makes this argument explicit in 30:40: *Allah is the One who created you, then provided for you, then will cause you to die, then give you life. Is there any of your partners who does anything of that? Glorified is He and exalted above what they associate* (30:40). The repetition of the seal in 16:1 and 16:3 forms an inclusio: the sūrah opened with judgment and transcendence, then revelation, then creation and transcendence again. The message is that judgment, revelation, and creation all point to the same conclusion — Allah alone deserves worship.

**Purpose Implies Return**

To say the heavens and earth were created bi-l-ḥaqq also implies they were created for an appointed term and for reckoning. Al-Ṭabarī links ḥaqq to the return: because creation is with truth, there must be a Day when truth is sorted from falsehood. If there were no return, the oppressor and oppressed would be equal at death, which would make creation in vain. The Qur'an denies this: *Did you think We created you in vain and that you would not be returned to Us?* (23:115). In al-Naḥl, this logic will become concrete. After listing blessings, the sūrah will say *If you tried to count Allah's blessings, you would never number them* (16:18), then ask *Can the One who creates be equal to those who do not?* (16:17). The argument moves from creation to gratitude to worship to accountability. A purposeful creation demands a purposeful human response. That response is tawḥīd and shukr, and its opposite is shirk and kufr, which in Arabic share the root sense of covering over a reality.

**The Bracketed Addition In Translation**

The translators add ˹in worship˺ in brackets to clarify yushrikūn. The verb ashraka literally means to make a partner or give a share. In Qur'anic usage it almost always means to associate others with Allah in the rights that belong to Him alone, especially worship, obedience, and reliance. The brackets are not in the Arabic but reflect interpretive clarity. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define shirk as assigning a share of lordship or worship to other than Allah. The addition helps an English reader who might think association is merely mental. It is practical: directing prayer, sacrifice, hope, or ultimate fear to other than Allah. By marking it as worship, the translation follows the understanding of Ibn ʿAbbās and others who explained yushrikūn here as worshipping idols alongside Allah while acknowledging Allah as Creator.

**How This Verse Anchors The Rest Of The Sūrah**

Verse 3 functions as a thesis for verses 5-18. Once it is established that heavens and earth are created with purpose by One who is exalted above partners, the sūrah can proceed to enumerate what that One has placed within heavens and earth for human benefit. Each blessing becomes evidence for the thesis. Rain, crops, cattle, sea, mountains — none created themselves, none are sustained by idols. Al-Rāzī counts more than twenty blessings in the next fifteen verses, each introduced as a sign. The repetition of the transcendence formula therefore does more than close a verse; it trains the reader to respond to every blessing with tanzīh, declaring Allah free of partners. In a Makkan context where idols stood inside the Kaʿbah itself, built by Ibrāhīm for Allah alone, this reminder was both theological and intensely practical: the house built for One was being used for many, and the heavens and earth that testify to One were being ignored.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:4

> **He created humans from a sperm-drop, then—behold!—they openly challenge ˹Him˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Cosmos To The Smallest Beginning**

After heavens and earth, the Qur'an turns to the human. Khalaqa al-insāna min nuṭfah — He created the human from a nuṭfah, a drop. The word nuṭfah in Arabic denotes a small amount of liquid, a drop that clings. It appears in the Qur'an to describe the initial stage of human formation, often paired with language of lowliness and weakness. The shift from vast heavens to a single drop is intentional. If the previous verse established Allah's power through magnitude, this verse establishes it through minuteness. The One who holds galaxies also originates a speaking, arguing, planning human from a fluid that cannot be seen without closeness. Al-Rāzī observes that this is a stronger proof of power than creating heavens, because heavens are great from great, while a human is great from insignificant. The same argument appears in 36:77: *Does man not see that We created him from a drop, and behold, he is a clear adversary?* (36:77). The wording there is almost identical to this verse, showing that the Qur'an returns to this image when confronting denial of resurrection.

**The Suddenness Of Fa-idhā**

Fa-idhā huwa khaṣīmun mubīn — then behold, he is an open challenger. The particle fa-idhā conveys surprise and suddenness. One moment a drop, the next a disputant who argues against his own Creator. The Qur'an uses this construction to highlight ingratitude and forgetfulness of origin. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical force: the origin is weak, the outcome is bold. The human forgets the first and exhibits the second. Khaṣīm comes from kh-ṣ-m, to contend, to be party to a dispute. Mubīn means clear, manifest, open. Together they describe not quiet doubt but open argumentation, the kind that confronts prophets and denies resurrection. Ibn Kathīr connects this to a reported occasion: a Makkan, identified in some reports as Ubayy ibn Khalaf, came with a decayed bone, crumbled it, and said, who will give life to this after it has rotted? The Qur'an answers such challenges elsewhere: *Say, He will give it life who produced it the first time* (36:79). The point is not that questioning is forbidden, but that this particular questioning is rooted in arrogance that forgets its own beginning.

**What The Drop Teaches About Resurrection**

The verse is placed in Sūrat al-Niʿam among blessings, yet it sounds like rebuke. That placement is precise. The ability to argue, to reason, to speak clearly is itself a blessing, but when turned against the Giver it becomes evidence against the arguer. Al-Ṭabarī explains that nuṭfah is mentioned to remind the human of how easily Allah can recreate. If He could create from a drop the first time, with no prior example, then recreation after death is easier. The Qur'an makes this explicit in 30:27: *It is He who originates creation, then will repeat it, and that is even easier for Him* (30:27). The Makkan deniers did not doubt that Allah created them; they doubted that He would bring them back. The verse answers by pointing to their own origin: you already were brought from nothing like this, and you already know it, because you see children born. To deny the second creation while affirming the first is inconsistent. The word insān is generic, but the lesson is personal. Each reader knows his origin was a drop; each reader knows he now argues.

**The Psychology Of The Open Challenger**

Khaṣīmun mubīn describes a character type that appears throughout al-Naḥl. Later verses will mention those who argue that Allah forced them into shirk (16:35), those who say revelation is fables of ancients (16:24), those who attribute daughters to Allah while disliking daughters for themselves (16:57-59). All are forms of khaṣm, argument that defends desire rather than seeks truth. Al-Qurṭubī notes that khaṣīm here does not mean a skilled debater in a praiseworthy sense, but a contentious opponent who uses speech to push away truth. The Qur'an distinguishes between jidāl that is good, as in *argue with them in the best way* (16:125), and jidāl that is blameworthy, which is argument for falsehood. The same tongue can do either. The drop that became a tongue is tested by how it uses speech. This is why gratitude in this sūrah includes speech: acknowledging blessings, not arguing them away. The human who was created to be ʿabd, servant, becomes khaṣīm, adversary, when he forgets.

**A Link To The Blessings That Follow**

At first glance verse 4 interrupts the flow from heavens and earth to cattle and rain. In fact it prepares for it. By reminding the human of his lowly origin and contentious nature, it makes the coming catalogue of blessings an act of forbearance. Allah continues to provide warmth, food, beauty, transport, rain, crops, and guidance to a creature who openly challenges Him. Al-Rāzī counts this as part of divine generosity: despite the human's argumentativeness, Allah does not withhold provision. Instead He lists provision in detail, as if to say, see how you are treated despite your contention. This also explains the seal in verse 7: *Surely your Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful* after mentioning cattle bearing loads. The grace is highlighted because the recipient is described in verse 4 as undeserving by argument. The sūrah thus moves from power to ingratitude to mercy, a pattern that will recur.

**Language And Translation Note**

Nuṭfah is translated as sperm-drop, though classical lexicons define it more broadly as a small drop of any fluid, including the combined fluids from both parents. The addition ˹Him˺ in brackets clarifies the object of challenge; the Arabic khaṣīmun mubīn is absolute, but context shows the challenge is directed against Allah, His messenger, and His promise of resurrection. The particle idhā after fa- gives vividness: it is as if the Qur'an says, look, suddenly. Translators render it as behold to capture that immediacy. The verse's brevity is part of its force. In seven Arabic words it moves from non-existence to existence to argument, compressing a lifetime into a single sentence, and thereby making the reader feel the speed with which favor is forgotten.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:5

> **And He created the cattle for you as a source of warmth, food, and ˹many other˺ benefits.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Blessing After The Rebuke**

After describing the human as a contentious challenger, the Qur'an immediately says: wa-l-anʿāma khalaqahā — and the cattle He created for you. The pronoun lakum, for you, is placed early to stress purpose and favor. Despite contention, provision continues. Anʿām in Qur'anic usage denotes grazing livestock: camels, cattle, sheep, and goats, as detailed in 6:142-144 where eight pairs are listed. These four kinds were the core wealth of Arabian society, providing transport, milk, meat, wool, leather, and social standing. By naming them right after the drop, the sūrah moves from origin to sustenance, showing that the One who created from a drop also sustains that creation with other creations. Al-Ṭabarī notes that khalaqahā emphasizes that even these familiar animals are not self-existing; they were created, assigned, and subjected. The human did not invent the camel's endurance or the sheep's wool.

**Warmth, Food, And More**

Lakum fīhā difʾun wa-manāfiʿu wa-minhā ta'kulūn — in them there is warmth and benefits and from them you eat. Difʾ, translated as warmth, comes from d-f-ʾ, to warm, to protect from cold. Classical commentators explain it as wool, hair, fur, and leather used for clothing, tents, and bedding. In a desert climate where nights are cold, this is not luxury but survival. Al-Qurṭubī adds that difʾ also includes the warmth of shelter made from animal products, not only clothing. Manāfiʿ, benefits, is plural and indefinite, indicating many other uses: milk, breeding, sale, dowry, blood-money, and manure for fields. The Qur'an often leaves manāfiʿ open to include what people know and what they discover later. Wa-minhā ta'kulūn — and from them you eat — is placed last though eating is immediate, to show that food is one benefit among many, not the only one. Al-Rāzī points out the order: first protection from cold, which is preservation of life, then general benefits, then specific food, moving from necessity to utility to enjoyment. The structure teaches gratitude in layers.

**A Blessing That Is Also A Sign**

In Sūrat al-Niʿam, blessings are never only blessings; they are signs pointing beyond themselves. Cattle are a sign of Allah's mercy because He created them with qualities perfectly suited to human need: camels store water and endure thirst, sheep produce wool that grows again, cattle provide strength for plowing. These qualities did not have to be together. Al-Rāzī argues that the convergence of many benefits in one animal is evidence of intentional design. If chance ruled, one might find an animal with wool but no milk, or meat but no ability to carry loads. Instead, the same animal offers several. Moreover, the verse says khalaqahā lakum — for you. This purposeful assignment implies a purposeful Assigner. The Qur'an elsewhere asks, *Did they create them or did We create them?* The answer is obvious to anyone who reflects on a newborn calf that stands within minutes, guided to its mother's milk without teaching. The blessing thus becomes an argument against the partners mentioned in verses 1 and 3. Idols did not create cattle nor do they sustain them, yet a share of gratitude is given to them.

**The Social World Of Anʿām**

For the first Makkan audience, anʿām were not abstract. They were currency, status, and survival. A man's wealth was counted in camels. A tribe's strength was measured by its herds. When the Qur'an says these were created for you, it touches the core of that economy and says, your wealth is a gift, not an achievement. This is why the sūrah later mentions that cattle bear burdens to lands you could not reach except with hardship (16:7). The same animal that gives warmth also makes trade possible, connecting distant markets. The mention of difʾ first also subtly refutes the idea that the world is purely for the strong. The weak, the cold, the hungry are considered first: warmth for those who shiver, food for those who hunger. In a society where the strong often seized grazing lands, this emphasis matters. The One who created cattle assigned them for all of you, not only for chiefs.

**Why The Verse Ends Without A Seal**

Unlike verses 1, 3, and 7, verse 5 does not end with a divine name or transcendence formula. It ends with benefits and eating, leaving the thought open. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that this openness invites the reader to continue to the next verses where beauty, transport, and mercy are added. The catalogue is cumulative. Each verse adds a layer: warmth and food, then beauty when returning and grazing, then burden-bearing, then riding and adornment, then unknown creations. The absence of a seal here is therefore rhetorical; it keeps the list moving. The reader is not allowed to stop at one blessing and say enough; he is led through an inventory that will later be summarized as uncountable (16:18). The verse also introduces a pattern that will dominate the sūrah: lakum, for you, repeated to stress that creation is oriented toward human need, not indifferent.

**Lexical Note On Difʾ And Manāfiʿ**

Difʾ is an uncommon word in modern Arabic but common in classical texts for anything that provides warmth, including clothing, tents, and even fire from dung in some interpretations. Lisān al-ʿArab cites difʾ as what protects from cold, from the verb dafaʾa, to repel cold. Manāfiʿ is the plural of manfaʿah, from n-f-ʿ, to benefit. Its indefinite form suggests abundance. The bracketed ˹many other˺ in translation reflects that manāfiʿ is general, covering benefits beyond warmth and food. The translators add it to avoid the impression that benefits are limited. In tafsīr, al-Ṭabarī lists milk, offspring, and price among manāfiʿ, while al-Qurṭubī adds use of skins for water containers and wool for furnishing. The point is that the Qur'an leaves the category open so that each generation finds its own known benefits inside it, while acknowledging that full enumeration is impossible.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:6

> **They are also pleasing to you when you bring them home and when you take them out to graze.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Beauty As A Recognized Blessing**

Verse 5 mentioned warmth, benefits, and food. Verse 6 adds jamāl — beauty. Wa-lakum fīhā jamālun ḥīna turīḥūna wa-ḥīna tasraḥūn — and for you in them is beauty when you bring them home in the evening and when you send them out to pasture in the morning. This is the only place in the Qur'an where cattle are explicitly described as a source of aesthetic pleasure. Elsewhere they are described as useful, but here they are pleasing to look at. Al-Rāzī highlights this as evidence that the Qur'an does not reduce creation to mere utility. Beauty is itself a benefit, a gift that nourishes the eye and heart. The Makkan who owned herds knew this beauty firsthand: the sight of camels returning at dusk, full, slow, dust rising, is a scene of prosperity and calm. The Qur'an validates that feeling and attributes it to Allah. Your pleasure when you see them is part of what He created for you. This reframes aesthetics as part of gratitude, not vanity.

**Evening Return And Morning Departure**

Turīḥūna comes from r-w-ḥ, to return in the evening, to bring home. Tasraḥūna comes from s-r-ḥ, to send out to pasture, to let go free in the morning. The two verbs capture a daily rhythm that structured Bedouin life: at sunrise herds leave for grazing, at sunset they return. Between those two moments the owner experiences two different beauties. In the morning, the sight is of hope and abundance as animals go out lean and eager. In the evening, the sight is of fulfillment as they return full-bellied, milk heavy. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the word order: turīḥūna before tasraḥūna, evening before morning, though chronologically morning comes first. He explains that evening return is more beautiful to the owner because it shows profit, the result of the day's grazing, and because evening is a time of gathering, rest, and counting. Al-Ṭabarī adds that evening is when neighbors see each other's herds and recognize Allah's favor, making beauty a social, visible blessing, not hidden.

**Why Beauty Matters In An Argument For Tawḥīd**

Sūrat al-Naḥl is arguing against shirk, association of partners with Allah. Why mention beauty? Because beauty reveals intention. A purely utilitarian animal could be ugly and still provide milk and meat. That cattle are also beautiful, that their movement, symmetry, and presence please the human eye, suggests that the Creator considered human delight, not only human survival. Al-Rāzī argues that jamāl cannot be explained by material necessity; it points to a Creator who is Himself beautiful and loves beauty, as in the prophetic saying *Allah is beautiful and loves beauty* (Muslim 91). The idols, by contrast, were often crude stones, unable to create beauty or to appreciate it. By directing attention to the beauty of living creatures, the verse invites comparison: which deserves your devotion, the living beautiful creation that benefits you, or the lifeless stone that benefits nothing? The aesthetic argument is gentle but firm. It does not threaten; it asks you to look.

**The Human Need For Beauty And Its Place In Gratitude**

Modern readers may pass over this verse as minor, but for its first audience it named something they felt but had not been taught to count as divine favor. In a harsh environment, beauty is not extra; it sustains morale. The Qur'an's inclusion of jamāl among blessings teaches that gratitude should include what pleases the eye, not only what fills the stomach. This has practical consequences. If beauty is a divine gift, then caring for animals so that they remain healthy and beautiful is part of gratitude, and mistreating them is ingratitude. Classical jurists used this verse to argue for good treatment of livestock, since Allah mentions their beauty as a gift to humans, implying they should be kept in a state where that beauty is preserved. The verse also subtly counters a worldview that sees religion as only about hardship. Here, pleasure in a beautiful sight at dusk is affirmed as legitimate and divinely given, provided it leads to recognition of the Giver.

**The Pairing Of Benefit And Beauty**

The sequence from verse 5 to 6 moves from necessity to delight. First difʾ, warmth, which preserves life; then manāfiʿ, general benefits; then ta'kulūn, food; then jamāl, beauty. This is a rising scale from survival to enjoyment. Al-Rāzī sees in this a hierarchy of human needs that the Qur'an respects: first the body is protected, then it is fed, then the soul is delighted. The same hierarchy appears later in the sūrah where houses are mentioned as places of rest, then garments for protection, then garments for adornment. The pattern shows that Islam does not demand denial of beauty but places it after necessity, so that beauty does not become a god. When beauty is seen as coming from Allah, it leads to gratitude; when it is seen as coming from self, it leads to pride. The verse therefore trains the eye: when you see your herds at evening, say, this is from Allah.

**Linguistic Note On Jamāl And The Daily Cycle**

Jamāl in Arabic denotes beauty that is pleasing in appearance and proportion, distinct from ḥusn which can include inner goodness, and from zīnah which often denotes added adornment. The Qur'an uses jamāl only twice, here and in 15:16 where it describes stars as beautiful. Both uses connect beauty to creation that guides or sustains. Turīḥūna and tasraḥūna are both in the form IV with plural address, indicating a habitual, collective action. The pronouns lakum and fīhā anchor the blessing: for you, in them. The benefit is not abstract; it is located in the animals themselves and directed to you. The verse's brevity — a single sentence — mirrors the simplicity of the scene it describes: evening, morning, herds, pleasure. No theological term is needed. The image does the work.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:7

> **And they carry your loads to ˹distant˺ lands which you could not otherwise reach without great hardship. Surely your Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Beasts That Make Distant Lands Near**

Wa-taḥmilu athqālakum ilā baladin lam takūnū bālighīhi illā bi-shiqqi al-anfus — and they carry your heavy loads to a land you would not reach except with great hardship to yourselves. Athqāl is plural of thiql, heavy load, baggage, goods that weigh down. The verse describes a specific function of cattle, especially camels, that made civilization possible in Arabia: long-distance transport of goods, water, and people across deserts where human strength fails. Al-Ṭabarī explains balad here as any distant town or region that a person needs to reach for trade or migration. Without these animals, the journey would require shiqq al-anfus, literally splitting of selves, an idiom for extreme effort that tears body and soul. The Qur'an uses vivid physical language to make the reader feel the alternative: without these carriers, you would have to carry the loads yourselves, and you would break. The blessing is therefore not only that loads move, but that you are spared breaking.

**Trade, Migration, And The Making Of Society**

For Makkans, this verse was not poetry but economics. Quraysh were traders. Their two annual journeys, to Yemen in winter and Syria in summer, mentioned in Sūrat Quraysh (106:2), depended entirely on camels bearing athqāl. Without camels, there is no trade; without trade, there is no Makkan prosperity. By saying these animals carry your loads to distant lands, the Qur'an reminds Quraysh that their wealth, which they attributed to their own skill and to the protection of their idols, in fact rides on the backs of animals Allah created and subjected. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse also applies to migration, hijrah, which would soon become central to the Muslim community. Those who would leave Makkah for Abyssinia and later Madinah would do so with camels carrying their families and goods. The verse thus anticipates the very experience of its listeners: the loads they would soon carry as emigrants are made possible by the same Creator they are being called to trust.

**Why The Verse Ends With Two Names**

Inna rabbakum la-ra'ūfun raḥīm — surely your Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. This is the first occurrence of divine names in the blessings catalogue, and it comes after mentioning hardship spared. Ra'ūf comes from ra'fah, a tenderness that prevents harm before it occurs. Raḥīm is mercy that brings good and forgives. The pair appears often in the Qur'an where human weakness is highlighted. Al-Rāzī explains that ra'fah is relevant here because carrying loads would otherwise cause shiqq al-anfus, a splitting of the self. To spare humans that splitting is ra'fah — gentle prevention. To then provide the animals that not only spare but also enable trade and growth is raḥmah — mercy that gives beyond mere protection. The names also answer verse 4's description of humans as contentious challengers. Even though humans challenge, the Lord remains gracious and merciful, continuing to provide carriers for their loads. The lordship, rabbakum, your Lord, is possessive and nurturing, not distant. It is the Lord who raised you from a drop who now spares you from breaking under your own baggage.

**The Concept Of Taskhīr Behind The Scene**

Though the word taskhīr, subjection, does not appear in this verse, the idea underlies it. Animals that are stronger than humans — a camel can kill a man — nonetheless allow themselves to be loaded, led, and directed. This obedience is not natural in the sense of automatic; it is a subjection placed by Allah. The Qur'an makes this explicit later: *And We have subjected them to them, so some they ride and some they eat* (36:72). Al-Qurṭubī points out that a child can lead a camel that could crush him, which shows that its docility is a divine gift, not a human achievement. The verse invites reflection on this: why does a powerful animal submit to a weaker human? The answer in the sūrah is that Allah subjected it for you. Gratitude therefore requires acknowledging the Subjugator, not merely using the subjected. When the Makkans loaded their caravans and thanked their idols for protection, they misdirected thanks. The camel's submission came from Allah, not from Hubal.

**Hardship As A Measure Of Blessing**

The phrase illā bi-shiqqi al-anfus — except with great hardship — quantifies blessing by imagining its absence. The Qur'an often does this to make ingratitude visible. It asks you to imagine life without the blessing, then to feel its weight. Without cattle carrying loads, every family would have to carry its own goods, every trader his own merchandise, every migrant his own children and water. The society that Quraysh prided itself on would collapse. By forcing that imagination, the verse makes the blessing unmistakable. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that shiqq can also be read as shaqq, with different vowel, meaning half, so the phrase could mean you would not reach the land except with half your selves gone, meaning after losing half your strength or half your number. Both readings converge on the same point: without these animals, reaching distant lands would cost you yourselves. The blessing is therefore measured in saved lives and preserved strength.

**Translation Note On Distant Lands**

The translators add ˹distant˺ in brackets before lands to capture the implication of balad here. Balad alone means town, land, country, but context indicates a distant destination that requires effort, not a neighboring village. The brackets show interpretive addition. Athqālakum, your loads, includes both physical loads and metaphorical burdens, though tafsīr focuses on physical goods. The word shiqq in classical lexicons means hardship that splits or cleaves, from the root sh-q-q, to split. Lisān al-ʿArab defines shiqq al-anfus as reaching the point where the soul is almost split from the body due to effort. By using such strong language for the alternative, the Qur'an elevates the animal from mere tool to savior from self-destruction. The verse thus completes a three-step argument: you are weak from a drop, you are contentious, yet your Lord is gracious and merciful enough to create for you carriers that prevent your breaking.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:8

> **˹He also created˺ horses, mules, and donkeys for your transportation and adornment. And He creates what you do not know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Different Kind Of Animal For A Different Kind Of Need**

Verse 5-7 spoke of anʿām, grazing livestock that provide warmth, food, beauty, and burden-bearing. Verse 8 turns to khayl, bighāl, and ḥamīr — horses, mules, and donkeys — created li-tarkabūhā wa-zīnah — so you may ride them and as adornment. The distinction is purposeful. Anʿām were primarily for sustenance and trade; these three are primarily for riding and dignity. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the Arabs differentiated between animals eaten and animals ridden, and the Qur'an respects that distinction while reminding that both categories are created. Horses in particular were symbols of nobility, speed, and martial power. To own a horse was to possess both transport and status. By saying Allah created them for riding and adornment, the verse acknowledges that human beings care about how they appear and how they move, and it affirms that this care is legitimate when attributed to its Creator. Zīnah, adornment, is not condemned here; it is listed as a divine purpose.

**Why Mules And Donkeys Are Named**

Mentioning bighāl and ḥamīr alongside khayl shows the Qur'an's attention to the full range of society. Not everyone owned a horse; horses were expensive and required skill. Donkeys were the transport of the common person, the student, the farmer, the woman carrying water. Mules, offspring of horse and donkey, were valued for strength and endurance in mountainous terrain. By naming all three, the verse includes all classes. The poor man's donkey and the chief's horse are both created by Allah for human use. Al-Qurṭubī adds that this naming also has legal implications. The fact that anʿām were mentioned separately as food, while these three are mentioned for riding and adornment without mention of eating, was used by many jurists to argue that horse, mule, and donkey meat is not intended as food. The Prophet ﷺ explicitly forbade eating domestic donkeys on the day of Khaybar, as reported in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4216 and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1801, and most schools extend the prohibition to mules. Horse meat is treated differently; some schools permit it, others discourage it because horses are more valuable alive for riding and defense.

**Adornment That Is More Than Vanity**

Wa-zīnah — and as adornment. The word zīnah in the Qur'an can mean both natural beauty and added decoration. Here it refers to the dignity and splendor that riding confers. Al-Rāzī explains that riding a horse, especially a well-kept one, gives the rider a sense of elevation, visibility, and honor. In a society where leaders were recognized by their mounts, this was not superficial. A rider could be seen, heard, and followed. The Qur'an does not dismiss this human desire to appear well; it places it within divine creation. You want to ride and look dignified — Allah created the means for that. This is consistent with the sūrah's broader method: it does not tell people to suppress natural desires but to see them as gifts that point to the Giver. Later the sūrah will mention garments for protection and garments for adornment (16:81), repeating the same pair: protection and beauty, necessity and delight. The pairing guards against two extremes: asceticism that rejects beauty and materialism that worships it.

**And He Creates What You Do Not Know**

Wa-yakhluqu mā lā taʿlamūn — and He creates what you do not know. This short clause opens the catalogue beyond the Arabian horizon. Al-Ṭabarī says it means Allah creates other animals and things beyond those named, which the listeners did not know at the time. Al-Qurṭubī expands: it includes animals in distant lands, creatures in the sea, and inventions that would appear later. Many modern commentators note that the phrase comfortably includes forms of transport and adornment that did not exist in the seventh century — ships larger than imagined, vehicles, aircraft — because the principle remains: Allah creates, whether directly as living creatures or by giving humans knowledge to make new carriers. The clause also functions as humility training. After listing familiar animals, the Qur'an reminds that creation is larger than your experience. You do not know all that Allah creates. This prepares for verses about rain, stars, and sea that also exceed immediate knowledge. It also answers the Makkan tendency to limit Allah's power to what they could see. If He creates what you do not know, then your ignorance is not a measure of His ability.

**The Bracketed Addition And Its Reason**

The translation begins with ˹He also created˺ in brackets because the Arabic verb khalaqa appears in verse 5 and is understood to continue, but verse 8's syntax begins with wa-l-khayla — and horses — with the verb implied. The brackets show that the translators supplied the verb for English clarity, not that it is missing in meaning. Classical Arabic often omits a repeated verb for brevity, relying on the previous verb to carry forward. This is called iḍmār or ḥadhf, elision for conciseness. The meaning is unanimous: horses, mules, and donkeys are also created by Allah. Recognizing this elision matters because it ties verses 5-8 into one creation passage, all governed by the same Creator. The sūrah is not listing random blessings; it is building a single argument: all these are from One.

**How This Verse Sets Up The Next**

Verse 8 ends with unknown creation, and verse 9 begins with guidance: *It is upon Allah to show the straight way*. The connection is deliberate. After establishing that Allah creates what you know and what you do not know, the sūrah says He also creates guidance and shows the way. Just as He did not leave you without transport, He did not leave you without direction. The physical carriers that prevent you from perishing in the desert have a parallel in the spiritual guidance that prevents you from perishing in confusion. Al-Rāzī makes this link explicit: horses carry bodies, guidance carries hearts, and both are created and shown by Allah. The mention of unknown creation also widens the reader's imagination before the next verses about rain, crops, and celestial bodies, which include phenomena that seventh-century Arabs knew existed but did not fully understand. By saying He creates what you do not know, the sūrah gives permission to keep discovering blessings without feeling that new discoveries threaten faith; they confirm it.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:9

> **It is upon Allah ˹alone˺ to ˹clearly˺ show the Straight Way. Other ways are deviant. Had He willed, He would have easily imposed guidance upon all of you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Physical Roads To The Straight Path**

After listing animals that carry bodies to distant lands and landmarks that show direction, the sūrah turns to the road that matters most: wa-ʿalā Allāhi qaṣdu al-sabīl — upon Allah is the direction of the way, to show the straight way. Qaṣd means straightness, directness, aiming for the target without deviation. Sabīl is way, path. The phrase indicates that guidance to the straight path is something Allah has taken upon Himself to provide. Al-Ṭabarī explains qaṣdu al-sabīl as the straight path that leads to Allah, the path of truth, as opposed to jā'ir, deviant paths that turn away. The verse comes right after horses and unknown creations to draw a parallel: just as Allah created carriers for your bodies, He has taken responsibility for guiding your hearts. Physical roads need landmarks; the spiritual road needs revelation. Both are from Him. The bracketed ˹alone˺ and ˹clearly˺ in translation reflect that the Arabic construction with ʿalā implies obligation or guarantee — it is for Allah, upon Him, to show — and that the showing is clear, not obscure.

**Other Ways Are Deviant**

Wa-minhā jā'ir — and among them, from those ways, are deviant ones. Jā'ir comes from j-w-r, to deviate, to turn aside unjustly. It describes a path that does not lead to the destination but veers off. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the pronoun minhā refers to subul, paths, implied though not explicitly stated, meaning among paths there are those that are crooked. The Qur'an often contrasts one straight way with many crooked ways, as in 6:153: *This is My straight path, so follow it, and do not follow other paths, for they will separate you from His path* (6:153). The image is of a main road with side tracks that lead into desert or danger. The sūrah will later give examples of such deviant paths: attributing partners to Allah, saying Allah willed their shirk as excuse, calling revelation fables. Each is a jā'ir path. By mentioning deviant ways immediately after affirming that Allah shows the straight way, the verse makes clear that guidance is available but not automatic; people may still choose crooked tracks. The existence of deviation does not mean guidance failed; it means humans have choice.

**Had He Willed, He Could Have Forced Guidance**

Wa-law shā'a la-hadākum ajmaʿīn — had He willed, He would have guided you all together. This clause answers a hidden objection. If Allah alone shows the way, why are not all guided? The answer is that He could have forced guidance, but He willed to allow choice. Al-Rāzī explains that hadā here means compelled guidance, making everyone follow truth without option, which Allah is able to do but does not do because the purpose of creation includes testing. The same idea appears in 6:149: *Had He willed, He would have guided you all* (6:149) and 13:31 and 32:13. Ashʿarī theologians cite this to show that guidance is by Allah's will, not by human merit alone, while Muʿtazilī thinkers emphasize that Allah wills to leave room for human earning, so that reward has meaning. Both agree on the outcome: diversity of response to guidance is part of divine wisdom, not inability. The verse therefore preserves both divine power and human responsibility. Allah is able to guide all, He has shown the straight way clearly, yet He leaves the walking to you.

**Why This Verse Is Placed Here**

The placement after transport and before rain is not random. The sūrah is building a layered argument: Allah creates carriers, He creates what you do not know, He shows the way. The physical journey and spiritual journey are paired. A traveler needs a mount and a road and direction. Verses 5-8 gave mounts, verse 15-16 will give landmarks and stars, and this verse gives direction for the heart. Without qaṣd al-sabīl, even the best mount goes astray. The Makkan audience understood travel. They knew that a caravan with strong camels but no guide perishes. The verse says, for your ultimate journey, Allah Himself is the guide who shows qaṣd, the straight aim. This also reframes the earlier prohibition against hastening Allah's command. The one who hastens judgment wants to skip the road; the one who follows qaṣd walks it patiently. The sūrah will close with *Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom* (16:125), echoing this same sabīl.

**The Meaning Of Straightness In Arabic**

Qaṣd in classical lexicons means moderation, middle, directness. It is the opposite of excessive deviation to either side. A qaṣd path is one that is direct to its goal without crookedness. Jā'ir is from the same root as jawr, injustice, because deviation from truth is injustice to oneself. The Qur'an uses qaṣd and jā'ir as moral terms, not only geographic. A straight path is just, balanced, leading to Allah; a deviant path is unjust, leading away. This moral geography is important for al-Naḥl, which repeatedly contrasts those who are grateful with those who cover favors, those who reflect with those who are proud. The straight way is the way of gratitude, which recognizes the Giver. Deviant ways are ways of ingratitude, which attribute gifts to others or deny them. The verse thus condenses the sūrah's ethical vision into one sentence: one straight way, many deviant ways, all under divine knowledge and will.

**A Verse That Guards Against Two Errors**

This verse guards against two opposite errors that still divide people. The first error is to think guidance is not from Allah, that humans find it alone by reason. The verse says wa-ʿalā Allāhi qaṣdu al-sabīl — upon Allah is the showing, not upon reason alone. The second error is to think that since Allah could guide all, those who are not guided are excused. The verse says He could have guided all, but He willed to show clearly and leave choice, so responsibility remains. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse therefore establishes both tawakkul and taklīf: reliance on Allah for guidance and accountability for following. In practical terms, the believer is taught to ask Allah for guidance constantly, as in al-Fātiḥah *Guide us to the straight path* (1:6), while also striving to walk, because the path has been shown. The balance is precise: Allah shows, you walk; He could compel, but He invites.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:10

> **He is the One Who sends down rain from the sky, from which you drink and by which plants grow for your cattle to graze.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Descent That Makes Life Possible**

Huwa al-ladhī anzala min al-samā'i mā'an — He is the One who sends down water from the sky. The pronoun huwa is emphatic: He, not any partner, not any idol, not any force of nature independent of Him. Anzala indicates sending down from above, a descent that humans cannot produce. In Arabian context, rain was not a minor blessing; it was life itself. Without it, wells dry, pastures die, cattle perish, trade stops. Al-Ṭabarī notes that samā' here means the sky or the direction above, not only clouds, to stress that the source is beyond human control. The Qur'an repeats this gift throughout, as in 15:22 and 23:18, always with the same emphasis: you do not send it down. The verse then divides the water's use into two immediate benefits: lakum minhu sharābun wa-minhu shajarun fīhi tusīmūn — from it you have drink and from it comes vegetation in which you pasture your cattle. Sharāb is drink for humans directly, shajar here means plants, trees, herbage generally, not only trees, and tusīmūn means to pasture, to let cattle graze freely. The same rain serves both you and your animals, linking human and animal dependence on one source.

**Drink And Pasture From One Source**

The pairing of sharābun and shajarun is deliberate. From one water come two kinds of life: human drink and animal feed. Al-Rāzī points out the wisdom: rain falls as one substance, yet it produces different tastes, colors, and uses. You drink it fresh, your cattle eat plants grown by it, then you drink milk from those cattle, as verse 66 will later explain. The chain is: rain → plants → cattle → milk and meat → human. One descent sustains multiple levels. This interconnectedness is a sign of planning, not chance. If rain were random, it might sustain one level but not all. Instead, it sustains the whole system that verses 5-8 just described. The cattle whose warmth, food, beauty, and transport were listed depend on this rain for their pasture. Without rain, there is no jamāl at evening return because there is no full belly. The verse thus ties the earlier catalogue back to its source. The One who created cattle also sends the rain that feeds them. Gratitude must go to the source, not to the intermediate.

**Shajar As All Vegetation**

Shajar is usually translated as trees, but commentators explain that here it means all plants, herbage, and shrubs that cattle graze, including what Arabs call hashīsh and kalā'. Al-Qurṭubī cites linguistic usage where shajar can mean any plant with a stalk, not only tall trees. The phrase fīhi tusīmūn, in which you pasture, uses sāma, to graze freely, distinct from raʿā which can mean to tend. Tusīmūn suggests letting animals roam in pasture, a scene of abundance where herbage is plentiful enough that cattle can be left to eat. This implies rain that is not scant but sufficient to produce pasture. In a desert, such pasture appears suddenly after rain, transforming barren land into green within days. Those who witnessed this transformation knew it as a resurrection in miniature, which is why the Qur'an often links rain to resurrection: *And We send down from the sky blessed water and give growth thereby to gardens and grain* (50:9) and *Look at the traces of Allah's mercy, how He gives life to the earth after its death; indeed He will give life to the dead* (30:50). The pasture that appears after rain is therefore both provision and proof.

**Why The Verse Says He Is The One**

The opening huwa al-ladhī is a formula of exclusivity used throughout this sūrah. It appears in verses 10, 11, 12, 14, and others, each time to attribute a blessing solely to Allah. This repetition is rhetorical: after establishing that Allah is exalted above partners, the sūrah now shows in detail why. Each blessing is introduced with He is the One who... to prevent misattribution. The Makkans attributed rain to stars, to idols, to chance. The Qur'an insists: He is the One who sends it. This exclusivity is the practical meaning of tawḥīd. It is not only to say Allah exists, but to say He alone does these things. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that huwa al-ladhī also carries a sense of personal care: the same He who created you from a drop is the He who sends down your drink. The continuity of subject — He created heavens, He created humans, He created cattle, He sends down rain — builds a single portrait of a Lord who creates, sustains, and guides, leaving no room for partners.

**A Verse That Prepares For Crops And Fruits**

Verse 10 mentions drink and pasture, the basics. Verse 11 will expand to crops, olives, palms, grapevines, and every fruit, showing increasing specialization. The order from general to specific mirrors human experience: first you need water to drink, then pasture for your animals, then crops for food, then fruits for enjoyment. Al-Rāzī sees in this a progression from necessity to delight, similar to verses 5-6 where warmth preceded beauty. The verse also introduces the idea that will become central in verse 11: rain as a sign for those who reflect. Even here, the sign is present: the same water that you drink also grows plants that you do not eat but your cattle do, showing a provision that considers both you and your dependents. A system that provides for multiple species from one source reflects mercy that is wider than human need alone.

**Lexical And Scientific Note**

Anzala min al-samā' is used for rain throughout the Qur'an, though modern meteorology explains rain formation through evaporation and condensation. The Qur'anic phrase does not contradict this; it speaks of ultimate source, not proximate mechanism, and classical commentators like al-Rāzī discussed winds and clouds as intermediaries while still attributing the sending to Allah. Sharāb is from sh-r-b, to drink, and includes all potable water. Tusīmūn is from s-w-m, to graze, to move freely for pasture. The form tusīmūn indicates ongoing, habitual pasturing, not a single event. The verse's simplicity — one sentence linking sky, water, drink, plants, cattle — condenses a whole water cycle into human terms. It is a reminder that the water cycle, which today is diagrammed in textbooks, was for the first audience a daily dependence and a visible mercy, and its regularity is itself a sign of a consistent Creator.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:11

> **With it He produces for you ˹various˺ crops, olives, palm trees, grapevines, and every type of fruit. Surely in this is a sign for those who reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Water To Crops To Fruits**

Yunbitu lakum bihi al-zarʿa wa-l-zaytūna wa-l-nakhīla wa-l-aʿnāba wa-min kulli al-thamarāt — with it He produces for you crops, olives, palm trees, grapevines, and from every fruit. The pronoun bihi refers back to the water in verse 10. One water, many outputs. Zarʿ is general crops, grains like wheat and barley that are staple food. Zaytūn, olives, provide oil, food, and light. Nakhīl, palm trees, provide dates, shade, building material, and fiber. Aʿnāb, grapevines, provide fruit, drink, and raisins. Then the summary wa-min kulli al-thamarāt — and from every type of fruit — widens beyond the four named to include all fruits known and unknown. Al-Ṭabarī notes that these four are named because they were the most valuable and familiar in the Hijaz and Syria, representing both staple and luxury, both food and trade goods. The order moves from general necessity (crops) to specific valuable trees that require years to grow and thus show long-term planning. Olives and palms live decades, even centuries; their inclusion shows that the provision is not only for today but for generations.

**One Water, Many Tastes And Colors**

The same water that you drink and that grows pasture now grows crops that differ in taste, color, smell, and use. Al-Rāzī makes this a central proof of divine wisdom: one water, one earth, yet outputs are vastly diverse. The soil is one, the water is one, the sun is one, yet from them come sweet dates and sour grapes, bitter olives and bland grains, all from the same source. If nature alone acted without a Wise Maker, why would one cause produce many effects that are perfectly suited to human need? The Qur'an asks this elsewhere: *And in the earth are neighboring tracts, and gardens of vines, and crops, and palm trees sharing one root and others with single roots, watered with one water, yet We make some excel others in taste* (13:4). The diversity is not chaos; it is organized diversity that serves human life. Olives give oil for cooking and lighting, dates give concentrated energy that preserves without refrigeration, grapes give quick sugar and can be dried. Each fruit has a role. The phrase min kulli al-thamarāt emphasizes completeness: not just some fruits, but from every kind, indicating abundance that cannot be exhausted.

**A Sign For Those Who Reflect**

Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li-qawmin yatafakkarūn — surely in this is a sign for those who reflect. This seal appears several times in al-Naḥl, each time with a slightly different audience: those who reflect, those who understand, those who are mindful. Here it is yatafakkarūn, those who think deeply, from f-k-r, to reflect, to consider. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that tafakkur is thinking that turns over a matter, examining it from different sides, not passing thought. The sign in this verse is not only that crops grow, but that they grow from one water into many forms that meet human needs, and that this happens regularly, dependably, yet remains beyond human power to replicate. A person who reflects will ask: who designed this system? Who made water fall from sky, earth absorb it, seeds sprout, trees bear fruit year after year? The answer, given the previous huwa al-ladhī formula, is Allah alone. The seal therefore turns a description of agriculture into a call to reflection. The farmer who plants and harvests is invited to see his field as a book, not only as a source of income.

**Why Olives, Palms, And Grapevines Are Singled Out**

These three trees have special status in Qur'anic imagery. Zaytūn is mentioned in Sūrat al-Nūr as the blessed olive tree whose oil gives light: *lit from a blessed olive tree* (24:35). It was central to Mediterranean economy, providing oil for food, lamp, soap, and medicine. Nakhīl, palms, are called *like clustered columns* and are praised for their resilience in desert, providing shade where no other tree grows. Dates were staple food that could be stored for months, crucial for travel. Aʿnāb, grapevines, were the fruit of highlands and gardens, requiring trellising and care, symbolizing cultivation and settlement. By naming these three, the Qur'an covers different ecologies: olives for hills, palms for desert oases, grapes for valleys and terraces. It shows that Allah's provision adapts to different lands and climates, not only one. Al-Qurṭubī adds that these three are also mentioned together in 6:99 and 13:4 as examples of diverse fruits from one water, indicating they were recognized as a set representing agricultural diversity.

**The Connection To Gratitude And Ingratitude**

In the context of Sūrat al-Niʿam, this verse deepens the argument against ingratitude. If Allah produces for you crops, olives, palms, grapes, and every fruit, then to attribute this to idols or to one's own effort alone is to cover the favor, which is the literal meaning of kufr. The verse says lakum — for you — twice in verses 10-11, stressing that the production is for human benefit. The appropriate response is shukr, gratitude, which in the Qur'an includes acknowledging the Giver, using the gift in obedience, and not giving the gift's credit to others. The seal li-qawmin yatafakkarūn suggests that ingratitude is a failure of reflection. Those who do not reflect see only crops, not the One who grew them. Those who reflect see through crops to the Creator. The sūrah will later say *If you tried to count Allah's blessings, you would never number them* (16:18), and this verse gives a taste of why counting fails: even from one water, the types of fruit are too many to list.

**Translation And Linguistic Note**

The translators add ˹various˺ in brackets before crops to reflect that al-zarʿ is generic and includes many kinds, not a single crop. The Arabic lacks a word for various but the indefinite form and context imply variety. Yunbitu is from n-b-t, to cause to grow, to sprout, in form IV indicating causation: He causes to grow, not merely grows. This causative form appears throughout the agricultural passages to stress that growth is not automatic; it is caused by Allah. Thamarāt is plural of thamarah, fruit in general, including fruits of trees and fruits of effort. The phrase min kulli al-thamarāt does not mean every single fruit exists in every land, but that from every type of fruit there are examples produced by this water somewhere, showing comprehensive provision. The verse's rhythm — short list of four items then broad summary — mirrors the experience of walking through a garden: you notice specific trees, then you realize the variety is endless.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:12

> **And He has subjected for your benefit the day and the night, the sun and the moon. And the stars have been subjected by His command. Surely in this are signs for those who understand.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Subjection Of The Great Bodies**

Wa-sakhkhara lakum al-layla wa-l-nahāra wa-l-shamsa wa-l-qamara — and He has subjected for your benefit the night and day, sun and moon. Sakhkhara, from s-kh-r, means to make subservient, to compel into service, to make something do work for another without resistance. The same verb was used for sea and will be used for birds. Here it is applied to celestial phenomena that humans cannot control: day and night alternate, sun and moon move, yet their alternation serves human life. Al-Ṭabarī explains taskhīr here as making them serve human interests: night for rest and concealment, day for work and seeking bounty, sun for light, heat, and ripening, moon for months and tides and gentle light. The phrase lakum, for your benefit, appears again, tying this verse to the previous catalogue where everything was for you. Even the most majestic bodies are for you, not you for them. This reverses the pagan tendency to worship sun and moon as gods. If they are subjected for you, they cannot be gods over you.

**Stars Subjected By His Command**

Wa-l-nujūmu musakhkharātun bi-amrihi — and the stars have been subjected by His command. Nujūm, stars, includes all stars, planets, and constellations. The construction musakhkharātun bi-amrihi emphasizes that their subjection is by His command, His amr, the same word that opened the sūrah. The stars follow orbits, appear and disappear, guide travelers, yet they do so by command, not by independent will. Al-Rāzī notes that stars are mentioned separately because their benefit is different: they guide at night on land and sea, they mark seasons, they adorn the sky. The Qur'an says elsewhere *And He made the stars for you to be guided thereby in darkness of land and sea* (6:97). In pre-Islamic Arabia, star lore was advanced; Arabs navigated by stars and attributed rain to star positions, saying *we were given rain by such-and-such star*. The Qur'an corrects this by saying stars are musakhkharāt, subjected, not actors. They are commanded, not commanders. This subtle polemic against astrolatry and star-based superstition is part of the sūrah's campaign against shirk.

**A Sign For Those Who Understand**

Inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li-qawmin yaʿqilūn — surely in this are signs for those who understand. Yaʿqilūn comes from ʿa-q-l, to bind, to restrain, to understand, to use intellect that restrains from error. The shift from yatafakkarūn in verse 11 to yaʿqilūn here is purposeful. Tafakkur is reflection on details, like crops from one water; ʿaql is understanding of larger systems, like day and night and celestial orbits. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that day and night, sun and moon require ʿaql to see their regularity and to infer a Regulator. Anyone can see crops grow; it takes deeper understanding to see that night and day are subjected for you and to conclude that their Subjector must be One, Powerful, and Caring. The plural āyāt, signs, rather than āyah, indicates multiple signs within this one system: the alternation itself, the benefit of each part, the precision of timing, the fact that they serve without tiring.

**Day And Night As Mercy And Measure**

The Qur'an often pairs night and day as two mercies: *And among His signs is your sleep by night and by day and your seeking of His bounty* (30:23). Night is not absence of mercy but a mercy itself: it provides rest, coolness, cover, and a time for reflection. Day is mercy for work, seeking provision, and seeing. Together they create a rhythm that matches human biology. Modern science confirms that human circadian rhythm depends on light-dark cycles, but the Qur'an noted the benefit long before: night for sukūn, tranquility, day for movement. Al-Qurṭubī adds that without night, humans would be exhausted by constant light and heat; without day, they could not farm or travel. The subjection includes both the existence and the alternation. Allah did not create only day or only night; He created both and made them alternate in measure, as in 73:20: *Allah measures night and day*. This measurement is itself a sign of wisdom, not randomness.

**Sun And Moon: Two Lights With Different Roles**

Al-shamsa wa-l-qamara — sun and moon — are mentioned together throughout the Qur'an as two lights with different functions. Sun is diyā', intense light and heat; moon is nūr, reflected gentle light, as in 10:5: *He made the sun a radiant light and the moon a reflected light and measured for it stages that you might know number of years and calculation* (10:5). Sun ripens crops mentioned in previous verse; moon marks months for fasting, pilgrimage, and trade. Both are subjected, meaning they follow fixed courses that humans can rely on. Al-Rāzī notes that their subjection includes that they do not collide, do not deviate, do not need rest. They serve continuously. This continuous service, without wage or complaint, is presented as evidence that they are commanded, not divine. A god does not serve without ceasing for the benefit of others; a subjected creature does. The verse thus uses their very usefulness to disprove their divinity.

**How This Verse Connects To Guidance**

Verse 9 said upon Allah is to show the straight way. Verse 12 shows that He also shows the physical way via stars and celestial bodies. The link is subtle: just as stars guide travelers in darkness of land and sea, revelation guides hearts in darkness of ignorance. Both are subjected by His command. The sūrah will make this explicit in 16:16: *and landmarks, and by the stars they are guided*. The same verb hadā, to guide, is used for stars and for revelation. The reader is being taught to see guidance everywhere: in sky and in Book, both from same Commander. The verse ends with la-āyātin, surely signs, in plural, because the system contains many signs, yet it is one system under one command, pointing to One God. The invitation to yaʿqilūn is therefore both intellectual and spiritual: use your intellect to bind your understanding to its true object, Allah alone.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:13

> **And ˹He subjected˺ for you whatever He has created on earth of varying colours. Surely in this is a sign for those who are mindful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earth Itself As A Display Of Colors**

Wa-mā dhara'a lakum fī al-arḍi mukhtalifan alwānuhu — and whatever He has scattered or created for you on earth of varying colors. Dhara'a means to create, to scatter, to multiply, to spread abundantly. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as khalaqa, He created, with emphasis on spreading creation widely. The phrase fī al-arḍi, on earth, shifts focus from sky and celestial bodies in verse 12 to terrestrial diversity. Mukhtalifan alwānuhu — varying in its colors — does not mean only hues like red and green, but kinds, types, shapes, tastes, and forms. Classical commentators note that alwān includes differences in color, shape, size, and nature. The earth contains minerals of different colors, plants of different shades, animals of different patterns, fruits of different tints, humans of different complexions. All are from one earth, yet varied. The verse says lakum, for you, again, tying this diversity to human benefit. The variety is not random clutter; it is provision that delights, distinguishes, and serves.

**Why Color Is Mentioned**

Color is a powerful sign because it is immediately visible and deeply tied to recognition and enjoyment. A world in one color would be functional but monotonous. Allah created a world where even of the same species, individuals differ: no two human faces identical, no two leaves exactly alike, yet all from same soil and water.
