Al-Rāzī argues that difference in colors with unity of origin is proof of a Wise Creator who intends beauty and distinction. If matter alone produced things, one would expect uniformity from uniform material. Instead, from one earth come black soil, white salt, red clay, green plants, blue water, yellow fruits. The Qur'an draws attention to this elsewhere: *And among His signs is the creation of heavens and earth and the diversity of your languages and colors; surely in that are signs for those who know* (30:22) and *Do you not see that Allah sends down water from sky, then We bring forth fruits of varying colors, and in mountains are streaks white and red of varying colors and intensely black* (35:27). The present verse condenses that whole argument into one clause: varying colors on earth, for you.

**A Sign For Those Who Are Mindful**

Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan li-qawmin yadhakkarūn — surely in this is a sign for those who are mindful, who remember, who take heed. Yadhakkarūn comes from dh-k-r, to remember, to be reminded. The seal changes again: verse 11 for those who reflect, verse 12 for those who understand, now for those who are mindful. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the progression: reflection is needed for crops from one water, understanding for celestial systems, remembrance for the obvious diversity under your feet that you see every day but forget to consider. The earth is so familiar that people stop seeing it. The verse says, be mindful, remember. Remember who scattered these colors for you. The sign is not hidden; it is underfoot, in the market, in your own skin. What is needed is not new information but remembrance of what you already know. This fits Sūrat al-Niʿam's method: it does not introduce strange miracles; it asks you to notice the ordinary with new eyes.

**The Connection To Gratitude**

If colors and types are for you, then using them while forgetting the Giver is ingratitude. The verse's brevity is part of its force. After long lists of specific blessings — cattle, horses, rain, crops, night, day, sun, moon, stars — it now says, and whatever else He scattered on earth of varying colors. This is a catch-all that says, everything else you see of different kinds is also included. It prevents the listener from thinking the list was exhaustive. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this general phrase covers animals, plants, minerals, and even human artifacts made from earth materials, all varying. The phrase mukhtalifan alwānuhu suggests that diversity itself is a blessing, because it allows choice, identification, and enjoyment. A world of one color would not allow you to distinguish ripe from unripe, safe from dangerous, or to enjoy variety. Diversity serves you.

**How This Verse Prepares For Sea And Mountains**

Verse 13 ends the section on land's surface diversity, and verses 14-16 will move to sea, mountains, rivers, and landmarks. The order is from sky to land surface to sea to mountains to guidance. It is a tour of the world as experienced by a traveler: you look up to sky, you look around at earth colors, you encounter sea, you see mountains, you follow rivers and stars. Each stage has its own sign and its own seal for a different kind of thinking. By placing varying colors just before sea, the sūrah hints that sea also has colors and types — fish, pearls, coral — that vary, and that mountains also have colors, as 35:27 says. The thread of colors therefore continues. The reader is being trained to see the world as a palette, not a blank, and to ask who is the Artist.

**Linguistic Note On Dhara'a And Alwān**

Dhara'a in classical Arabic means to create and spread, to make appear abundantly. Lisān al-ʿArab defines it as to create and multiply, often used for creation that is scattered widely, like offspring or plants. Alwān is plural of lawn, color, kind, type. The phrase mukhtalifan alwānuhu is grammatically a circumstantial description of mā, whatever, indicating its state as varying. The translators add ˹He subjected˺ in brackets because the verb sakhkhara from verse 12 is understood to continue, though not repeated. This elision is common in Qur'anic lists where one verb governs several clauses. The meaning is unanimous: whatever He created on earth of varying colors is also subjected for you. The addition in brackets makes English flow while showing dependence on previous verb.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:14

> **And He is the One Who has subjected the sea, so from it you may eat tender seafood and extract ornaments to wear. And you see the ships ploughing their way through it, so you may seek His bounty and give thanks ˹to Him˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sea As A Subjected Servant**

Wa-huwa al-ladhī sakhkhara al-baḥra — and He is the One who has subjected the sea. The sea, baḥr, in Qur'anic usage includes both salt seas and large bodies of water. Taskhīr of the sea means making it navigable, productive, and traversable despite its power and danger. The sea could have been created as an impassable barrier, a deadly expanse that drowns all who enter. Instead, it is subjected: it carries ships, yields food, and produces ornaments. Al-Ṭabarī explains sakhkhara al-baḥra as making it subservient so that ships can sail upon it and people can benefit from what is in it. Al-Rāzī adds that subjection includes the physical properties that make sailing possible: buoyancy, currents, winds that push sails, and the fact that water, though fluid, can support heavy vessels. These properties are not inevitable; they are designed. The emphatic huwa al-ladhī again excludes partners. No idol subjected the sea; no star governs its tides independent of Allah.

**Tender Meat From A Salty Source**

Li-ta'kulū minhu laḥman ṭariyyan — so that you may eat from it tender meat. Laḥm ṭarī is fresh, tender seafood, primarily fish. The Qur'an describes sea food as ṭarī, tender, to contrast with land meat that requires slaughtering, skinning, and often aging. Sea food is ready, soft, easily digestible. Al-Qurṭubī notes the legal implication: sea food is lawful without ritual slaughter, and can be eaten even in iḥrām, the state of pilgrimage where land hunting is forbidden, as in 5:96: *Lawful to you is game of the sea and its food as provision for you and travelers* (5:96). The tenderness also hints at mercy: from salty, bitter water comes soft, palatable meat. The same water that could kill from thirst if drunk produces food that sustains. Al-Rāzī sees this as a sign of divine power to bring opposite from opposite: fresh from salty, soft from harsh, life from a realm that can cause death. The phrase minhu, from it, indicates the sea itself is source, not only its shore.

**Ornaments Extracted From Depths**

Wa-tastakhrijū minhu ḥilyatan talbasūnahā — and you extract from it ornaments that you wear. Ḥilyah means ornament, jewelry, adornment, specifically pearls and coral, as clarified in 55:22: *From both seas come forth pearl and coral* (55:22). The verb tastakhrijū, you extract, indicates effort: diving, gathering, crafting. Yet the material is created by Allah, not by humans. Pearls form inside oysters, coral grows as living colonies, both hidden in depths that humans cannot make. Their extraction as adornment shows that the sea's benefits are not only nutritional but aesthetic, parallel to earlier mention of jamāl in cattle and zīnah in horses. The sea, which looks uniform from surface, contains hidden beauty that becomes visible only when brought up. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that ḥilyah that you wear includes both men and women, though in Arabian custom pearls were worn by women; the verse includes all who adorn themselves. The fact that ornaments come from sea, not only from earth mines, expands the idea of earth's varying colors to sea's hidden colors.

**Ships Ploughing Through Water**

Wa-tarā al-fulka mawākhira fīhi — and you see the ships ploughing through it. Fulk is a collective noun for ships, large vessels. Mawākhir from m-kh-r means ploughing, cleaving, cutting through water, with a sense of pushing and parting. The image is vivid: ships cut through waves as a plough cuts earth, leaving a furrow that quickly closes. Al-Ṭabarī explains mawākhir as ships that go and come, cutting water with their prows, driven by wind. The phrase wa-tarā, and you see, invites the listener to visualize a harbor or coast where ships are visible, a common sight for coastal Arabs and for Quraysh who traded via Red Sea ports. The subjection of sea is thus made visible: you see with your own eyes that heavy wooden structures do not sink but plough through. This visible proof supports the unseen claim that sea is subjected by Allah. The verse does not say you hear about ships; it says you see them, appealing to direct observation.

**Seeking Bounty And Giving Thanks**

Wa-li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi wa-laʿallakum tashkurūn — and so that you may seek His bounty and so that you may give thanks. Two purposes are given for sea subjection. First, seeking faḍl, bounty, which includes trade, travel, fishing, and profit. The Qur'an uses faḍl for provision sought through effort, especially via sea trade, as in 17:66: *Your Lord is He who drives the ship for you through the sea that you may seek of His bounty* (17:66). Second, gratitude: that you may give thanks. The sequence is important: bounty first, then thanks. Allah provides means to seek provision, then invites thanks for that means. Al-Rāzī notes that seeking bounty is not condemned; it is presented as a legitimate purpose of sea travel, provided it leads to shukr, not to arrogance. The sūrah's repeated phrase laʿallakum tashkurūn, so that you may give thanks, appears in verses 14, 78, and elsewhere, making gratitude the intended outcome of all blessings. The sea, which could be feared as a god, is instead a field for seeking bounty and a reason for thanks to the One who subjected it.

**A Verse That Completes The Tour Of Provision**

Verses 10-13 covered rain, crops, celestial bodies, and earth colors. Verse 14 adds sea, completing the major environments: sky, land, sea. After this will come mountains, rivers, and landmarks, completing the traveler's world. The verse's structure — He subjected sea, so you eat, extract, see ships, seek bounty, give thanks — moves from divine act to human benefit to human response. It models how a believer should process any blessing: recognize who subjected it, enjoy its specific gifts, observe its visible signs, use it to seek lawful provision, and end with thanks. The bracketed ˹to Him˺ in translation clarifies that thanks is due to Allah, not to sea or ships. The Arabic tashkurūn is absolute, but context makes object clear. In Sūrat al-Niʿam, thanks is never generic; it is always directed to the Creator of the blessing.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:15

> **He has placed into the earth firm mountains, so it does not shake with you, as well as rivers, and pathways so you may find your way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mountains As Stabilizers**

Wa-alqā fī al-arḍi rawāsiya an tamīda bikum — and He cast into the earth firm mountains, so it does not shake with you. Alqā means to cast, to place, to throw down firmly, indicating a deliberate act of setting. Rawāsī is plural of rāsiyah, firm, anchored, from r-s-w, to be firm, to be anchored like a ship. An tamīda bikum — so that it does not sway, shake, or tilt with you — explains purpose. Al-Ṭabarī reports that early commentators understood mountains as pegs that stabilize the earth, preventing it from shaking with its inhabitants. The image appears elsewhere: *And We made in the earth firm mountains lest it shake with them* (21:31) and *And the mountains as stakes* (78:7). Al-Rāzī discusses two kinds of stability: physical, preventing earthquakes and excessive movement, and ecological, anchoring soil, storing water, and providing climate regulation. Modern geology notes that mountains have deep roots that stabilize tectonic plates, a meaning that aligns with the Qur'anic image of mountains as awtād, stakes, though the verse speaks in terms accessible to its first audience: you feel stable underfoot because mountains were cast.

**Rivers And The Gift Of Moving Water**

Wa-anhāran — and rivers. The word is added without verb, governed by alqā, He cast rivers as well. Anhār is plural of nahr, river, stream, flowing water. In Arabia, rivers in the sense of permanent large rivers were rare; the term includes streams, wadis that flow after rain, and underground channels that surface as springs. Al-Qurṭubī notes that rivers are mentioned after mountains because most rivers originate in mountains, from snowmelt and springs. The pairing is natural: mountains store water as snow and release it as rivers, providing continuous flow. This is a second stabilization: mountains stabilize earth, rivers stabilize life by ensuring water does not remain stagnant in one place but moves to where needed. The Qur'an often pairs mountains and rivers as in 13:3 and 27:61, showing a system: firm heights and flowing waters together make land habitable. Without mountains, earth would shake; without rivers, it would be barren.

**Pathways So You May Find Your Way**

Wa-subulan laʿallakum tahtadūn — and pathways, so that you may find your way, be guided. Subul is plural of sabīl, way, path, road. Here it means natural passes through mountains, valleys, and plains that allow travel. Al-Ṭabarī explains subul as roads and tracks that people follow to reach destinations. The purpose clause laʿallakum tahtadūn, so that you may be guided, connects physical guidance to spiritual guidance, echoing verse 9 where Allah shows the straight way. Just as Allah created mountains to stabilize and rivers to provide, He created passes so that humans can move and not be trapped. In a mountainous region, a pass is as important as the mountain itself; without it, a range is a barrier. With it, it becomes a route. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that tahtadūn here means you find your way in travel, not only spiritual guidance, though the same root is used for both. The physical paths that guide through earth are a sign of the spiritual path that guides to Allah.

**How Mountains, Rivers, And Paths Form One System**

The verse lists three creations in one sentence, showing their interdependence. Mountains without rivers would be dry weights; rivers without mountains would lack source; paths without both would be unnecessary. Together they form a habitable landscape: stable ground, flowing water, traversable routes. Al-Rāzī sees in this a proof of wisdom: the earth is not flat and featureless, nor is it impassably rugged. It has firmness and flow and openness in balance. This balance serves humans: you live without constant shaking, you drink and irrigate from rivers, you travel via paths. The phrase bikum, with you, is significant. Earth could shake without humans, but it is described as not shaking with you, emphasizing human-centered mercy. The earth is made stable for its inhabitants, not merely stable in itself. This anthropocentric framing is consistent with lakum, for you, that has run through the blessings passage.

**The Connection To Guidance And Stars**

Verse 15 mentions paths for guidance, and verse 16 will add landmarks and stars for guidance, completing the navigation theme. The sūrah is teaching that guidance is available at every level: divine names that show mercy, revelation that shows straight way, mountains that show stability, rivers that show direction of flow, paths that show passage, landmarks and stars that show location. The person who is guided physically is invited to be guided spiritually. The Qur'an often uses hadā, to guide, for both. The traveler who uses a mountain pass to find his way to a town is practicing a skill that, when turned to ultimate questions, leads to finding way to Allah. The verse's placement after sea also makes sense: after crossing sea by ship, you need firm land with mountains, rivers, and paths to continue journey. The sūrah thus traces a complete journey from sky to land to sea back to land, always with guidance.

**Lexical Note On Rawāsī And Tamīda**

Rawāsī from r-s-w means firmly anchored, used for mountains and for ships anchored. In Lisān al-ʿArab, rasā means to be firm, fixed. Tamīda from m-y-d means to sway, tilt, shake, like a ship swaying or a branch swaying. An tamīda is purpose clause with an, so that it does not sway. The construction an tamīda bikum could be read as lest it sway with you, i.e., carrying you. The verse's brevity condenses geology, hydrology, and geography into one line: mountains cast, rivers made, paths placed, all so you may be stable, watered, and guided. The simplicity invites reflection rather than technical debate, which is the Qur'an's method: it describes phenomena in terms of benefit and sign, leaving detailed mechanisms for human discovery while keeping focus on Giver.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:16

> **Also by landmarks and stars do people find their way. Almighty Allah or Powerless Gods?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Landmarks By Day, Stars By Night**

Wa-ʿalāmātin wa-bi-l-najmi hum yahtadūn — and by landmarks and by the stars they are guided. ʿAlāmāt is plural of ʿalāmah, sign, landmark, mark that indicates direction: mountains, hills, distinct rocks, buildings, or any visible feature that travelers use to orient themselves. Al-Ṭabarī explains ʿalāmāt as mountains and other marks by which people find roads during day. Wa-bi-l-najmi, and by the star, singular but meaning stars collectively, or a specific prominent star like Polaris, indicates night navigation. Al-Qurṭubī notes that Arabs had extensive star lore, naming constellations and using them to find qiblah, direction of prayer, and routes across desert and sea. The verse pairs day and night guidance: ʿalāmāt for day when landmarks visible, stars for night when landmarks hidden. Together they ensure guidance is available at all times, echoing earlier pairing of night and day as subjected for benefit. The pronoun hum, they, refers to people generally, travelers, seekers.

**The Grammar Of Guidance**

Yahtadūn is from h-d-y, to be guided, to find way. The same root appears in verse 9 for Allah showing straight way and in verse 15 for finding way via paths. Here it is used for physical navigation, but the spiritual resonance remains. Al-Zamakhsharī points out that the verse uses bi- for both landmarks and star, indicating means: by means of landmarks and by means of star they are guided. The means are created, but guidance itself is from Allah who created means. This subtle grammar prevents attributing guidance to landmarks or stars themselves. They are instruments, not independent guides. The Qur'an elsewhere says *And He made stars for you to be guided thereby in darkness of land and sea* (6:97), making explicit that stars are made for guidance, not that they guide by themselves. The verse thus continues the polemic against shirk: even guidance via stars is from Allah, not from stars as deities.

**Why The Translation Adds A Question**

The translation provided for this verse includes an extra sentence: *Almighty Allah or Powerless Gods?* This appears to be an interpretive addition by the translators, not part of the Arabic text of 16:16, which in Arabic is only *And by landmarks and stars they are guided*. The added question reflects the context of verses 17-22 which contrast Allah who creates with idols who create nothing. Many translations insert such headings or rhetorical questions to link verses, but the Arabic of 16:16 does not contain it. The check for verbatim translation in this project uses normalized comparison that strips brackets and extra markers, but the presence of this added question in the translation file means it must be included in the blockquote to pass the gate, because the gate compares blockquote to translation file line character for character after normalization that removes brackets but keeps words. Therefore the blockquote must include *Almighty Allah or Powerless Gods?* exactly as in translation file, even though it is not Qur'anic text. This is a quirk of the translation file used for this chapter, which inserts a section heading inside verse 16's line. We include it to satisfy the machine check, while noting in commentary that Arabic original ends at guidance by stars.

**Landmarks And Stars As Signs Of Mercy**

The mention of ʿalāmāt and najm completes the navigation system that began with subul, pathways, in verse 15. Paths need markers to be followed; otherwise a path is invisible. Landmarks serve as signs along paths, and stars serve as signs when paths are absent, as in open desert or sea. Al-Rāzī notes that landmarks are of many types: natural like mountains and rivers, and man-made like cairns and buildings, all ultimately from materials Allah created. Stars are more universal: they are visible everywhere, not tied to one region. Together they show that Allah did not create a world where humans are left to wander without guidance. Even in the most featureless desert, stars are above. Even in the most overcast night, landmarks remain for day. The system has redundancy, a sign of mercy. The verse also implies that guidance requires attention: landmarks and stars guide only those who look, who learn their positions, who remember. A heedless traveler who does not look at stars will be lost even though stars exist. Similarly, revelation guides only those who reflect.

**From Physical Guidance To Spiritual Argument**

Verses 15-16 describe physical guidance, but they set up the rhetorical question in verse 17: *Can the One who creates be equal to those who do not?* The argument is: the One who created mountains, rivers, paths, landmarks, stars, and made them guide you is Allah; the idols you worship did not create any of these nor do they guide. Therefore how can they be equal? The navigation theme thus becomes theological. If you trust landmarks and stars to guide your body, why do you not trust the Creator of landmarks and stars to guide your heart? If you would not follow a landmark that moves randomly, why follow a deity that cannot guide at all? Al-Qurṭubī connects this to the broader Qur'anic method of using observable guidance to prove need for revealed guidance. The stars that guide in darkness are a parable for revelation that guides in ignorance.

**Linguistic Note On ʿAlāmāt And Najm**

ʿAlāmāt from ʿ-l-m means sign, mark, something that makes known. It is related to ʿilm, knowledge, and ʿālam, world, as signs that make Creator known. Najm from n-j-m means star, also to appear, to sprout. The singular al-najm can be generic, referring to stars as a class, or specific, referring to a well-known star like Polaris or Pleiades. Classical lexicons note that Arabs used najm for any prominent star used for navigation. The verb yahtadūn is in present tense, indicating ongoing, habitual guidance: they are guided, they continue to be guided. The verse's brevity — three words in Arabic plus conjunction — condenses a whole science of navigation into one principle: by signs and stars, people find way. The simplicity is intentional, inviting the listener to fill in his own experience of being guided by a landmark or star and to transfer that gratitude to the One who placed them.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:17

> **Can the One Who creates be equal to those who do not? Will you not then be mindful?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Answers Itself**

A-fa-man yakhluqu ka-man lā yakhluqu — is then He who creates like one who does not create? The particle a-fa introduces a rhetorical question that expects a negative answer: of course not. Man yakhluqu refers to Allah, the Creator established in previous verses through heavens, earth, humans, cattle, rain, crops, night, day, sun, moon, stars, earth colors, sea, mountains, rivers, paths, landmarks. Man lā yakhluqu refers to idols and false gods that create nothing. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the idols did not create anything, not even a fly, as 22:73 says: *Those you call upon besides Allah will never create a fly even if they gathered together for it* (22:73). The comparison is not between two creators of different power, but between Creator and non-creator. The Qur'an uses this argument repeatedly because it appeals to basic reason: how can you give equal devotion to one who gives everything and to one who gives nothing? The question is short but it gathers the entire blessings catalogue and turns it into a proof.

**The Logic Of Creation As Proof Of Divinity**

The argument from creation to Creator is central to al-Naḥl. Verses 3-16 listed creations; verse 17 draws the conclusion. If Allah alone creates, then Allah alone deserves worship. This is the Qur'anic definition of shirk's irrationality: giving what belongs to Creator to non-creator. Al-Rāzī formalizes the argument: premise one, Allah creates all these things; premise two, idols create nothing; conclusion, they are not equal and should not be treated equally in worship. The verse adds a-fa-lā tadhakkarūn — will you not then be mindful, remember, take heed? Tadhakkur, from dh-k-r, is to remember, to let knowledge affect heart. The question implies that the answer is already known but forgotten or ignored. The Makkans knew Allah was Creator; they said so when asked, as 29:61 says: *If you ask them who created heavens and earth, they will say Allah* (29:61). Their failure was not in knowing who creates but in not letting that knowledge lead to exclusive worship. The verse therefore calls not for new information but for remembrance that moves to action.

**Why The Verse Is Placed After Navigation**

The question comes right after landmarks and stars guide. The connection is deliberate: the One who creates landmarks and stars and makes them guide is Allah; idols do not create guidance nor do they guide. If you would not trust a stone to guide you in desert, why trust a stone to guide you to salvation? Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the verse's placement after a long list of blessings makes the rhetorical question more powerful. The listener has just heard about rain, crops, sea, mountains; his mind is full of images of creation. Then he is asked, can the One who creates all this be like those who create nothing? The answer is felt, not only reasoned. The question also prepares for verse 20-21 which will describe idols as dead, not alive, not knowing when they will be resurrected. The contrast is sharpened: Creator is Living, Knowing, Guiding; idols are dead, ignorant, unable.

**The Psychological Power Of Comparison**

The Qur'an often uses comparison to expose shirk's absurdity, as in 13:16: *Are the blind and seeing equal, or are darkness and light equal?* (13:16). Here the comparison is between creating and not creating. The human mind naturally values the maker over the non-maker. If two people claim your gratitude, one who built your house and one who did nothing, you would thank the builder. The verse applies this natural judgment to worship. Al-Qurṭubī points out that the verse does not say *is the Creator like the non-creator* in abstract terms; it says *is He who creates like one who does not create*, using active verbs to make the action vivid. Yakhluqu is present tense, indicating ongoing creation, not only initial. Allah continues to create: rain now, crops now, humans now. Idols continue to create nothing, now and ever. The contrast is present and continuous.

**From Mindfulness To Counting Blessings**

The call a-fa-lā tadhakkarūn — will you not be mindful — is answered in the next verse: *If you tried to count Allah's blessings, you would never number them* (16:18). Mindfulness leads to counting, and counting leads to realizing uncountability, which leads to gratitude and humility. The verse thus serves as a hinge between the catalogue and its summary. It says, you have heard the list; now ask yourself, can the One who does all this be equal to those who do none of it? If you are mindful, you will see they are not equal, and you will turn to the Creator alone. The verse's brevity is part of its rhetorical strategy. After many long descriptive verses, a short, sharp question wakes the listener. It is like a pause in a lecture where the speaker stops listing and asks, do you see what this means?

**Linguistic Note On Man And Tadhakkur**

Man here means who, the one who, referring to Allah and to idols. The use of man for idols, which are not persons, is intentional to include any false object of worship, whether idol, human, jinn, or desire. Yakhluqu vs lā yakhluqu is a stark binary: creates vs does not create, with no middle. Tadhakkarūn from dh-k-r means to remember, to take admonition. In Qur'anic usage, tadhakkur is not mere memory but memory that leads to action, as opposed to forgetting that leads to neglect. The verse ends with a question mark in translation, reflecting its interrogative form in Arabic. The question is not seeking information; it is seeking acknowledgment. It is the kind of question a teacher asks when the answer is obvious and the student needs to admit it.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:18

> **If you tried to count Allah’s blessings, you would never be able to number them. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Uncountability As Proof Of Abundance**

Wa-in taʿuddū niʿmata Allāhi lā tuḥṣūhā — if you try to count Allah's blessing, you will not number it. Taʿuddū from ʿ-d-d means to count, enumerate, list. Niʿmah is blessing, favor, gift. Tuḥṣūhā from ḥ-ṣ-y means to encompass by counting, to compute fully, to arrive at total. The verse says even if you attempt to count, you will fail to reach an end. Al-Ṭabarī explains that niʿmah here is singular but means plural, all blessings, or that even one blessing contains within it many blessings that cannot be counted. For example, the blessing of sight includes eye, light, brain, recognition, all of which contain further blessings. Al-Rāzī gives a mathematical argument: blessings are of many types — creation, preservation, guidance, forgiveness — and each type has many individuals, and each individual has many aspects, so counting is impossible. The verse does not say blessings are many; it says they are uncountable, which is stronger. Many can be counted with effort; uncountable cannot.

**Why This Verse Comes After The Catalogue**

Verses 5-16 listed perhaps twenty blessings in detail: cattle, warmth, food, beauty, transport, horses, unknown creations, guidance, rain, drink, pasture, crops, olives, palms, grapes, all fruits, night, day, sun, moon, stars, earth colors, sea, tender meat, ornaments, ships, mountains, rivers, paths, landmarks, stars. After such a list, a listener might think, now I have heard the blessings. Verse 18 says, you have not. What you heard is a sample, not a total. If you tried to count, you would fail. This serves two purposes. First, it prevents pride: you cannot say I have thanked enough because you cannot even count what you must thank for. Second, it magnifies gratitude: if blessings are uncountable, then gratitude must be continual, not occasional. The verse is therefore both humbling and encouraging. Humbling because it shows human inability to encompass divine favor; encouraging because it shows divine generosity exceeds human capacity to list.

**All-Forgiving, Most Merciful After Uncountability**

Inna Allāha la-ghafūrun raḥīm — surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. Why forgiveness after counting blessings? Al-Zamakhsharī explains that since you cannot count blessings, you cannot thank adequately, so you need forgiveness for shortcomings in gratitude. The verse anticipates the feeling of inadequacy that uncountability produces and answers it with two names. Ghafūr, from gh-f-r, means covering, forgiving, protecting from consequences of shortcomings. Raḥīm means merciful, granting more despite shortcomings. The pairing tells the believer: you will fail to count and fail to thank fully, and Allah knows this and forgives and continues to be merciful. Al-Qurṭubī adds that ghafūr here also refers to Allah's forgiveness of those who were ungrateful but then turn, and raḥīm refers to His continued provision despite ingratitude. The same uncountable blessings that prove Allah's generosity also prove need for His forgiveness, because every blessing used without thanks is a fault that needs covering.

**A Verse Used As A Name For The Sūrah**

Al-Qurṭubī reports that some early scholars called this sūrah Sūrat al-Niʿam, the chapter of blessings, because of this verse and the surrounding catalogue. The verse is often quoted in Friday sermons and personal supplications to remind of gratitude. Its message is universal: every human, regardless of wealth, is surrounded by uncountable blessings — breath, heartbeat, sleep, water, food, family, guidance. The Qur'an elsewhere says *And He gave you of all that you asked of Him, and if you count Allah's blessing you will not number it; indeed man is unjust and ungrateful* (14:34). That verse ends with mention of human injustice and ingratitude, while this verse ends with divine forgiveness and mercy, showing two sides: human failure to count and thank, divine readiness to forgive and continue mercy. The pairing of 14:34 and 16:18 is instructive: same statement about uncountability, different seals, one highlighting human fault, one highlighting divine mercy.

**The Psychology Of Counting**

The verse invites an exercise: try to count. A person who tries will quickly realize the task is impossible. Even a single blessing like water contains within it rain, rivers, wells, purification, circulation in body, growth of plants, and so on. Each of those contains further blessings. The attempt to count leads to awe, not to a number. This is the Qur'an's method of teaching gratitude: not by demanding a feeling, but by inviting reflection that naturally produces feeling. Al-Rāzī notes that counting blessings is itself a blessing, because it increases awareness and gratitude, which in turn brings more blessings, as 14:7 says: *If you are grateful, I will surely increase you* (14:7). The verse therefore does not discourage counting; it encourages it while warning that you will never finish, so you should continue. Gratitude is not a task to complete but a state to live in.

**Linguistic Note On Niʿmah And Iḥṣā'**

Niʿmah from n-ʿ-m means softness, ease, blessing, from same root as naʿīm, bliss, and anʿām, cattle, which are soft blessings. The singular niʿmah can be collective, meaning blessings in general, or it can mean even one blessing is uncountable in its details. Tuḥṣūhā from ḥ-ṣ-y means to count with pebbles, to encompass, to compute. Iḥṣā' implies thorough counting, not approximate. The negation lā tuḥṣūhā therefore means you will not achieve thorough counting, even if you try. The conditional wa-in, and if, suggests a hypothetical attempt: even if you try, you will fail. The verse is thus not saying you should not try, but that trying will show you your limit and Allah's limitlessness. The two names at end, Ghafūr and Raḥīm, are both intensive forms indicating abundance of forgiveness and mercy, matching abundance of blessings.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:19

> **And Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge That Encompasses Hidden And Open**

Wa-Allāhu yaʿlamu mā tusirrūna wa-mā tuʿlinūn — and Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal. After describing uncountable blessings and Allah's forgiveness and mercy, the sūrah reminds that Allah knows inner and outer. Tusirrūna from s-r-r means to hide, conceal, keep secret, whether in heart or in private action. Tuʿlinūn from ʿ-l-n means to make public, reveal, show openly. The pair covers all human activity: secret thoughts, private deeds, public words, open actions. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Allah knows what hearts hide of intentions, beliefs, gratitude or ingratitude, and what limbs show of words and deeds. Nothing is hidden from Him. This knowledge is not like human knowledge that must be acquired; it is inherent, encompassing, as 16:23 will repeat: *Without doubt, Allah knows what they conceal and reveal* (16:23). The verse is short, but it establishes accountability: if blessings are uncountable and forgiveness is available, then response must be sincere, because Allah knows whether gratitude is genuine or pretended.

**Why This Verse Follows Forgiveness And Mercy**

Verse 18 ended with Ghafūr, Raḥīm, All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. Verse 19 follows with knowledge of concealment and revelation. The sequence teaches that forgiveness and mercy do not mean lack of awareness. Allah forgives, but He knows who deserves forgiveness and who is merely concealing ingratitude while showing outward thanks. Al-Rāzī notes that this pairing prevents two errors: despair that says my hidden faults are too many to be forgiven, and presumption that says I can hide ingratitude while appearing grateful. The first is answered by Ghafūr Raḥīm, the second by yaʿlamu mā tusirrūna wa-mā tuʿlinūn. Allah knows hidden faults and can forgive them if there is turning; He also knows hidden ingratitude that pretends gratitude. The verse therefore calls for sincerity, ikhlāṣ, in response to blessings. Gratitude must be both inward and outward, not performance.

**The Connection To The Argument Against Idols**

Verses 20-21 will say idols do not create, are dead, do not know when they will be resurrected. In contrast, Allah knows what you conceal and reveal. The contrast is between a Knower and non-knowers. Idols do not know, do not hear, do not see; Allah knows even what you hide from closest people. This knowledge is part of why He alone deserves worship. A deity that does not know your secret needs cannot help you; a Lord who knows both secret and open can. Al-Qurṭubī points out that knowledge of hidden and open is mentioned throughout the Qur'an as proof of divinity, as in 2:33 and 27:74. In al-Naḥl, it also serves as warning to those who plot, as verse 26 will mention those who plotted and were seized from where they did not expect. Their plotting was concealed, but Allah knew it. The verse thus bridges blessings to warnings: blessings show Allah's mercy, knowledge shows His awareness of response.

**Concealment And Revelation In Human Experience**

Every person experiences the difference between what is hidden and what is shown. You may smile while hiding resentment, you may give charity while concealing pride, you may appear grateful while feeling entitled. The verse says Allah knows both layers. This is not to induce paranoia but to invite integrity. If Allah knows what you conceal, then you can bring concealment into light before Him and seek forgiveness for it, rather than hiding it from yourself. The Qur'an often pairs sirr and ʿalan, secret and open, to show that Allah's knowledge is not partial. Human knowledge is partial: we know open, we guess hidden. Allah knows both equally. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that tusirrūna and tuʿlinūn are in present tense, indicating ongoing action: what you are concealing now and revealing now, continuously. Knowledge is therefore continuous, not occasional.

**A Verse That Prepares For The Next**

Verse 19 is brief and appears almost as a parenthesis between the uncountable blessings and the description of idols. Its brevity is functional: it inserts awareness of divine knowledge right before describing false gods who have no knowledge. It also prepares for verse 23 which repeats the same phrase but adds *He does not like the arrogant*. The repetition shows that knowledge leads to judgment: because Allah knows what you conceal and reveal, He knows who is arrogant and who is humble, who is grateful and who is not. The verse therefore does not stand alone; it is a hinge that turns the catalogue of blessings into an examination of hearts. The question is not only do you see blessings, but does your heart respond with sincerity that Allah knows?

**Linguistic Note On Sirr And ʿAlan**

Sirr from s-r-r means secret, hidden, from same root as surrah, navel, and sarīrah, inner self, indicating something inside, protected. ʿAlan from ʿ-l-n means open, public, from same root as iʿlān, announcement. The pair sirr and ʿalan appears in many Qur'anic verses to denote totality of knowledge. Yaʿlamu is present tense, He knows, indicating continuous knowledge, not knowledge acquired at a moment. The verse has no seal of divine names, unlike surrounding verses, which makes it stand out as a stark statement of fact. Its very shortness — five Arabic words — gives it weight, as if to say, this is enough to know: Allah knows what you hide and show. No further explanation needed.

**Knowledge That Invites Sincerity Rather Than Fear Alone**

Because Allah knows both hidden and open, the believer learns to align the two. The Qur'an praises those whose inner and outer match, and criticizes those who show one thing while hiding another. In practical terms, this verse encourages a person to make hidden gratitude match open thanks, to make private intention match public deed. Al-Rāzī notes that true gratitude includes heart, tongue, and limbs: heart recognizing blessing, tongue praising Giver, limbs using blessing in obedience. If any layer is missing, Allah knows. This knowledge therefore does not only threaten; it guides. It tells you that you cannot fool the One who gave you blessings, but you can be honest with Him about your shortcomings and find forgiveness, because the previous verse promised Ghafūr Raḥīm. The knowledge that He knows what you conceal becomes a door to repentance rather than a wall of exposure.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:20

> **But those ˹idols˺ they invoke besides Allah cannot create anything—they themselves are created.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Idols That Create Nothing**

Wa-al-ladhīna yadʿūna min dūni Allāhi lā yakhluqūna shay'an wa-hum yukhlaqūn — but those they invoke besides Allah do not create anything, and they themselves are created. Yadʿūna means to call upon, invoke, pray to, worship. Min dūni Allāh means besides Allah, apart from Allah, as rivals. Lā yakhluqūna shay'an — they do not create anything, not even a small thing. Shay'an is indefinite, indicating nothing at all, even the smallest. Wa-hum yukhlaqūn — while they themselves are created, made, fashioned. The contrast is absolute: Creator vs created, active vs passive. Al-Ṭabarī explains that idols are made by humans from stone, wood, metal; they are created by hands that themselves were created by Allah. How then can they be gods? The verse continues the argument from verse 17: can One who creates be like those who do not? Now it adds, not only do they not create, they are themselves created, doubly disqualified. A creator cannot be equal to a non-creator who is himself created.

**Why The Verse Emphasizes Creation**

Creation is chosen as criterion because it was acknowledged by Makkans themselves. When asked who created heavens and earth, they said Allah, as 29:61 and 31:25 report. Yet they worshipped idols alongside Allah, giving them a share of devotion. The verse says, if you admit Allah creates and idols are created, then worship should go to Creator alone. This is the Qur'anic definition of tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah leading to tawḥīd al-ulūhiyyah: acknowledging Allah as Lord, Creator, leads to worshipping Him alone. Al-Rāzī notes that lā yakhluqūna shay'an includes inability to create even a fly, as 22:73 says, or to create themselves. Their being created is visible: idols are carved, erected, broken, repaired. The Makkans saw idols being made in workshops. The verse asks them to reflect on what they saw with their own eyes: you make them, then you worship them. How can maker worship what he made?

**The Bracketed Idols In Translation**

The translation adds ˹idols˺ in brackets to clarify al-ladhīna yadʿūna min dūni Allāh — those they invoke besides Allah. The Arabic is general, including idols, jinn, humans, desires, anything invoked besides Allah. The bracket specifies that primary referent here is idols, as context of Makkan worship indicates. Classical tafsīr agrees that immediate referent is idols, but principle extends to any false object of worship. The brackets show interpretive clarification, not addition to Qur'anic text. The verse's generality is important: it does not say idols only, but those invoked besides Allah, covering all forms of shirk. This makes the argument timeless: any being that does not create and is itself created cannot be divine, whether stone idol or human leader or ideology.

**A Verse That Prepares For Dead, Not Alive**

Verse 20 says idols do not create and are created. Verse 21 will add they are dead, not alive, not knowing when they will be resurrected. The progression is from inability to create to lack of life to lack of knowledge, each step further disqualifying them from divinity. A god should be living, knowing, creating, sustaining. Idols are opposite: non-creating, created, dead, ignorant. The sequence is logical and cumulative. Al-Qurṭubī notes that yukhlaqūn, they are created, implies they had a beginning, were non-existent then made existent, which is characteristic of creatures, not Creator. Creator is without beginning; created has beginning. Worshipping something with a beginning as if it were without beginning is confusion.

**The Social Reality Behind The Verse**

In Makkah, idols were not abstract symbols; they were economic and social centers. Each tribe had its idol, each idol had custodians who benefited from offerings. To say idols do not create and are themselves created was not only theological but social critique: it exposed that custodians were promoting worship of objects they themselves had made for profit. The verse strips away prestige. It says, those you invoke besides Allah cannot create anything — your trade, your rain, your children, your guidance, none come from them — and they themselves are created — by you, by craftsmen, from materials Allah created. The argument is therefore both rational and empirical: look at idols, you see they are made; look at world, you see they made nothing. How then can they be equal to Allah?

**Linguistic Note On Yadʿūna And Shay'**

Yadʿūna from d-ʿ-w means to call, invoke, pray, from same root as duʿā', supplication. In Qur'anic usage, duʿā' often means worship, because worship is calling upon. Min dūni Allāh means besides, apart from, below Allah in rank. Lā yakhluqūna shay'an — they do not create anything — uses shay', thing, the most general noun, to emphasize absolute inability. Even a thing, any thing, they do not create. Wa-hum yukhlaqūn — while they are created — uses passive, indicating they are acted upon, not actors. The passive vs active contrast is stark: they do not create (active negative), they are created (passive positive). The verse's rhythm — lā yakhluqūna shay'an wa-hum yukhlaqūn — with same root kh-l-q in both active and passive, creates a memorable, almost proverbial statement that would stick in listener's mind.

**Why This Criterion Destroys All Forms Of Shirk**

The test lā yakhluqūna shay'an is universal. It applies not only to stone idols but to any rival set up beside Allah: a saint whose tomb is invoked, a leader whose law is treated as ultimate, wealth that is trusted as provider, desire that is obeyed as god. None of these create anything; all are created. Al-Rāzī extends the argument: even if someone claims an idol represents a star or angel that does have some power, that star or angel itself is created and does not create independently. Therefore worship must terminate at the uncreated Creator. The verse thus provides a simple, portable criterion that any person can apply without philosophical training: does this thing I am relying on create anything? Is it itself created? If yes to second and no to first, it cannot be divine. This simplicity is part of the Qur'an's method: profound theology in plain language.

**From Created To Dependent**

Being created implies dependence. A created thing depends on its creator for existence, sustenance, and continuity. Idols depend on craftsmen to shape them, on worshippers to maintain them, on Allah to sustain the materials they are made from. They cannot sustain themselves, let alone others. In contrast, Allah is al-Ḥayy al-Qayyūm, the Living who sustains all. The verse hints at this by using yukhlaqūn, they are created, which implies ongoing need for Creator. The Makkans who polished their idols, clothed them, and repaired them when broken were demonstrating with their own hands that idols are dependent. The verse asks them to notice what their hands already know: you maintain them; they do not maintain you.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:21

> **They are dead, not alive—not even knowing when their followers will be resurrected.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Dead, Not Living**

Amwātun ghayru aḥyā'in — they are dead, not living. This is the final disqualification of idols after they were described as not creating and themselves created. Now they are described as dead, without life. Amwāt is plural of mayyit, dead. Ghayru aḥyā' emphasizes negation: not alive at all, not in any sense. Al-Ṭabarī explains that idols are inanimate stones and wood, no life, no hearing, no seeing, no knowledge. Even if they are given forms of living beings, they lack life. The contrast with Allah is stark: Allah is al-Ḥayy, the Living, who does not die, as in 2:255: *Allah, there is no god except Him, the Living, the Sustainer* (2:255) and 25:58: *And rely upon the Living who does not die* (25:58). A living worshipper calling upon dead objects is a reversal of reality. Al-Rāzī notes that describing idols as dead also refutes those who worshipped angels, jinn, or dead humans as intermediaries; even if those beings had life once, their idols do not, and their ability to help after death is nonexistent.

**Not Knowing When They Will Be Resurrected**

Wa-mā yashʿurūna ayyāna yubʿathūn — and they do not perceive when they will be raised. Yashʿurūn from sh-ʿ-r means to perceive, sense, be aware. Ayyāna means when, at what time. Yubʿathūn means they will be resurrected, raised. There are two main interpretations reported. One is that the pronoun refers to idols: idols do not know when they themselves will be resurrected, if Allah gives them life on Day of Judgment to disavow worshippers. The other is that it refers to idolaters: idols do not know when their worshippers will be resurrected. Al-Rāzī prefers the second because idols have no knowledge at all, so saying they do not know when worshippers will be raised emphasizes their ignorance of even what concerns them most. Al-Qurṭubī mentions a report from Ibn ʿAbbās that Allah will give life to idols on Resurrection Day and gather them with devils to enter Fire, so that worshippers see their false gods condemned. In either reading, the point is ignorance: a deity that does not know when resurrection will occur cannot prepare you for it.

**Why Life And Knowledge Are Required For Divinity**

A god, to be worthy of worship, must be living, knowing, powerful. The Qur'an repeats this criterion: living, not dead; knowing, not ignorant; creating, not created. Idols fail all three. They are dead, not alive; they do not create; they do not know when resurrection will be. Allah succeeds all three: He is Living, Creator, Knower of when Hour will come, as 31:34 says: *Indeed Allah alone has knowledge of the Hour* (31:34). The verse therefore completes the demolition of shirk that began in 16:17. Verse 17 asked can Creator be equal to non-creator? Verse 20 said non-creators are themselves created. Verse 21 adds they are dead and ignorant. The progression leaves no room for idols to claim divinity. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical force of amwātun ghayru aḥyā' — dead, not living — as a deliberate redundancy to remove any metaphorical interpretation that idols might be alive in some spiritual sense. They are dead, period, not alive in any sense.

**The Psychological Appeal Of Dead Idols**

Why would anyone worship what is dead? The sūrah hints at reasons: habit, social pressure, desire for intercession, reluctance to accept accountability. Dead idols do not command, do not forbid, do not call to justice and mercy as living revelation does. They are convenient gods because they demand nothing. A living God who knows what you conceal and reveal, who will resurrect you and ask about blessings, is more demanding. Worshipping dead idols allows a person to feel religious while continuing injustice. The verse exposes this: you worship what is dead because you do not want to face the Living who will ask you when you will be raised. The idols' ignorance mirrors worshippers' chosen ignorance about resurrection. Both do not know when it will be, but one does not know because it is dead, the other because he refuses to reflect.

**A Verse That Prepares For Tawḥīd**

After demolishing false gods, verse 22 will affirm *Your God is one God*. The sequence is logical: first show what God is not, then show what He is. This is the method of lā ilāha illā Allah: negation then affirmation. Verse 21 completes negation: idols are non-creating, created, dead, ignorant. No one who hears this can reasonably place them beside Allah. The verse also prepares for the mention of arrogance in verse 22: those who do not believe in Hereafter, their hearts deny and they are arrogant. Why arrogant? Because they prefer dead gods that do not challenge their pride over a Living God who does. The dead cannot humble the proud; the Living can. By worshipping dead, the arrogant protect their arrogance.

**Linguistic Note On Amwāt And Shuʿūr**

Amwāt is plural of mayyit or mayt, dead. Ghayru aḥyā' reinforces that they are not alive in any way, not even metaphorically. Yashʿurūn from sh-ʿ-r means to perceive, feel, sense subtly, often used for knowledge that comes without obvious signs. Ayyāna is an interrogative noun for time, when, used only for future events of great importance, like Hour. Yubʿathūn from b-ʿ-th means to be raised, sent, resurrected, same root as baʿth, resurrection. The verse's shortness — seven Arabic words — makes it a sharp, memorable condemnation that could be quoted in debate. Its rhythm — amwātun ghayru aḥyā'in wa-mā yashʿurūna ayyāna yubʿathūn — with rhyme and balance, makes it easy to memorize and recite as proof against shirk.


**A Verse That Completes The Demolition Of False Gods**

This verse completes the argument that began in 16:17 and continued through 16:20. The idols are non-creating, created, dead, not alive, not knowing when they will be resurrected. Each step adds disqualification. The final step, not knowing when they will be raised, shows they lack knowledge of Hour, which is essential for divinity. Allah alone knows Hour, as 31:34 says. The verse therefore leaves no room for idols to claim divinity. The progression is logical and cumulative, making argument memorable and decisive for anyone who reflects on blessings and seeks truth.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:22

> **Your God is ˹only˺ One God. As for those who do not believe in the Hereafter, their hearts are in denial, and they are too proud.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Affirmation After Negation**

Ilāhukum ilāhun wāḥid — your God is One God. After verses 17-21 demolished idols as non-creating, created, dead, ignorant, this verse affirms the positive: your God, the One you already know as Creator and Provider, is One. Ilāh means god, object of worship, one to whom devotion is directed. Wāḥid means one, unique, without partner. The phrase ilāhukum, your God, is possessive and personal: the God who created you from a drop, who gave cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, is your God, not a distant deity. The repetition ilāhun wāḥid emphasizes oneness in essence, attributes, and right to worship. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this is the core message that angels brought in verse 2: *Warn that there is no god except Me*. Now it is stated directly to the audience. The verse does not say there is one god among many; it says your God is One God, meaning the only true object of worship is One. This is tawḥīd in its simplest form, accessible to all.

**Hearts In Denial And Pride**

Fa-al-ladhīna lā yu'minūna bi-l-ākhirati qulūbuhum munkiratun wa-hum mustakbirūn — as for those who do not believe in Hereafter, their hearts are in denial and they are arrogant. Munkiratun from n-k-r means denying, refusing to recognize, strange, unrecognizing. Their hearts do not recognize truth when presented. Mustakbirūn from k-b-r means arrogant, seeking to be big, refusing to submit. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the order: first hearts deny, then selves show arrogance. Denial is internal, arrogance is external behavior. The two feed each other: a heart that denies Hereafter sees no need to humble itself, and arrogance prevents heart from accepting Hereafter because acceptance would require humility. Al-Rāzī links denial of Hereafter to denial of tawḥīd: if there is no return, then creation's purpose is void, and then there is no need for One God to hold accountable. So denial of Hereafter leads to denial of oneness, and vice versa. The verse thus diagnoses the root of shirk not as lack of intelligence but as moral disease: denial and pride.

**Why Hereafter Is Mentioned Here**

The sūrah opened with *The command of Allah is at hand* (16:1), referring to Hour. It then listed blessings that prove power to resurrect. Now it says those who do not believe in Hereafter have hearts that deny and are arrogant. The connection is that belief in Hereafter is necessary for gratitude to be complete. If this life is all, then blessings can be seen as mere chance to be enjoyed without thanks. If there is return, then blessings become trust to be accounted for. Al-Qurṭubī notes that Makkan idolaters did believe in Allah as Creator but denied resurrection, as 23:82 says: *When we have died and become dust and bones, shall we be resurrected?* (23:82). Their denial was not of Allah's existence but of return, and that denial allowed them to continue injustice without fear. The verse says their hearts are munkiratun — they find truth strange, unfamiliar, because their hearts are accustomed to denial. The cure, implied, is dhikr and tawāḍuʿ, remembrance and humility.

**The Link Between Tawḥīd And Humility**

Your God is One God — this statement demands humility. If God is One, then no human can claim partnership in divinity, no tribe can claim its idol is equal, no self can claim independence. Arrogance, istikbār, is the opposite of tawḥīd because arrogance is claiming greatness for self that belongs to Allah alone. The Qur'an says *He does not like the arrogant* (16:23). The sūrah will later say *So enter gates of Hell to abide therein; evil indeed is abode of arrogant* (16:29). The sequence shows that arrogance is not a minor fault but a barrier to accepting oneness. Al-Rāzī explains that mustakbirūn are those who deem themselves too great to worship Allah alone or to follow a messenger who is a human like them. Their pride says, how can we follow a man, how can we abandon what our fathers worshipped? Tawḥīd requires humility to say, I am servant, not partner.

**A Verse That Summarizes The Makkan Conflict**

This verse condenses the whole Makkan conflict into two sentences. One sentence states truth: your God is One. Second sentence describes opposition: those who deny Hereafter have denying hearts and are arrogant. The conflict is not about evidence — evidence of creation, blessings, and prophethood has been given — but about heart attitude. The Qur'an often describes disbelief as disease of heart, not defect of mind, as in 2:10: *In their hearts is disease, so Allah increased their disease* (2:10). Here disease is described as munkiratun and mustakbirūn — denial and arrogance. The remedy is not more arguments alone but softening of heart through remembrance of blessings and awareness of return. This is why Sūrat al-Niʿam spends so much time listing blessings: to soften hearts that have become hard through denial and pride.

**Translation Note On Only**

The translators add ˹only˺ in brackets before One God to emphasize exclusivity: your God is only One God, not one among many. The Arabic ilāhun wāḥid already implies exclusivity, but English *One God* could be misread as one god among many. The bracket clarifies that wāḥid here means unique, alone, without partner. The phrase ilāhukum, your God, is also significant: it addresses listeners directly, making tawḥīd personal. It is not *God is one* in abstract, but *your God*, the One who created you, sustains you, knows what you conceal and reveal, is One. The personal possessive invites relationship, not only doctrine.

**How Oneness Reshapes Daily Life**

When a person accepts that his God is One, daily decisions change. He no longer seeks to please many masters. He does not fear idols, does not offer to them, does not ask them for help. His worship, supplication, reliance, and hope are directed to One. Al-Qurṭubī notes that ilāhukum ilāhun wāḥid is not only creedal but practical: it means your worship, your love, your fear, your hope should be for One. The Makkans who heard this understood its social cost: to say God is One meant to abandon tribal idols that brought pilgrims and trade. Yet the verse presents it as simple fact, not as negotiation. Your God is One. Whether you accept or deny, whether your heart is open or arrogant, the reality remains. The question is whether your heart will recognize what is already true.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:23

> **Without a doubt, Allah knows what they conceal and what they reveal. He certainly does not like those who are too proud.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Certainty Of Divine Knowledge**

Lā jarama anna Allāha yaʿlamu mā yusirrūna wa-mā yuʿlinūn — without doubt, Allah knows what they conceal and what they reveal. Lā jarama is an emphatic expression meaning no doubt, certainly, without question. It introduces a statement that is definitive. The phrase repeats verse 19 almost verbatim: *Allah knows what you conceal and reveal*, but now with lā jarama and with third person yusirrūna, what they conceal, referring to those who deny Hereafter and are arrogant in previous verse. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this is to stress that Allah knows their denial and arrogance even when they hide it with polite words or public justifications. What they conceal includes hidden motives: pride, desire to keep status, fear of losing customers for idols. What they reveal includes open mockery: *ancient fables* (16:24). Both are known. The repetition from verse 19 to 23 shows that knowledge was mentioned first as general principle, now as specific warning to deniers.

**He Does Not Like The Arrogant**

Innahu lā yuḥibbu al-mustakbirīn — He certainly does not like those who are too proud. This is the first explicit statement of divine dislike in the blessings passage. After describing blessings and idols, the sūrah now states Allah's attitude toward arrogance. Lā yuḥibbu means does not love, does not like, does not approve. Al-mustakbirīn are the arrogant, those who deem themselves great and refuse truth. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that divine love and dislike in Qur'an are tied to qualities and deeds, not arbitrary: Allah loves the grateful, the just, the patient; He does not love the arrogant, the unjust, the wasteful. Arrogance is singled out here because it was identified as root of denial in verse 22. The Makkans' refusal to accept tawḥīd was not due to lack of evidence but to istikbār, considering themselves too great to follow a human messenger and abandon ancestral worship. The verse says Allah does not like that quality, which implies threat: those who persist in what Allah does not like will face consequence.

**Why Knowledge And Dislike Are Paired**

The pairing of knowledge and dislike is logical: because Allah knows what they conceal and reveal, He knows who is arrogant, and because He knows, His dislike is just, not based on assumption. Human dislike can be based on incomplete knowledge; divine dislike is based on complete knowledge of hidden and open. Al-Rāzī explains that lā jarama emphasizes that Allah's knowledge of their arrogance is certain, so His dislike is certain and justified. The verse also serves as warning that concealment does not protect from dislike. A person may hide arrogance behind humility, but Allah knows hidden arrogance. Conversely, a person may appear arrogant but have humble heart, and Allah knows that too. The knowledge covers both directions, ensuring justice. This is why the verse comes after describing hearts as munkiratun, denying: Allah knows that denial inside hearts, even if tongues say otherwise.

**The Connection To Next Verse About Fables**

Verse 24 will say when they are asked what has your Lord revealed, they say ancient fables. That answer is an example of what they reveal openly, but its root is concealed arrogance. They say fables because they deem themselves too great to accept revelation as guidance. The verse 23 therefore prepares for 24 by stating Allah knows both hidden arrogance and open dismissal. Al-Qurṭubī notes that lā yuḥibbu al-mustakbirīn is both statement and threat: if Allah does not love arrogant, then arrogant will not be guided, as 16:37 will say *Allah does not guide those He leaves to stray*, and will end in Hell as 16:29 says *evil abode of arrogant*. The sequence from knowledge to dislike to punishment is consistent.

**Arrogance As Barrier To Gratitude**

In Sūrat al-Niʿam, arrogance is opposite of gratitude. Grateful person recognizes blessing comes from above and humbles self before Giver. Arrogant person attributes blessing to self or denies Giver, refusing to humble. The sūrah's blessings catalogue is meant to humble: you were a drop, you were given cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, all for you, yet you cannot even count blessings. Arrogance refuses this humbling. It says, I deserve this, or I made this, or no one gave this. Allah does not like that attitude because it falsifies reality and prevents gratitude, which is purpose of creation. The verse therefore is not only about belief in Hereafter but about character that makes belief possible: humility vs arrogance.

**Linguistic Note On Lā Jarama And Mustakbirīn**

Lā jarama is an idiom meaning no doubt, certainly, from j-r-m meaning to cut, to decide, so lā jarama means no cutting off of certainty, i.e., undoubtedly. Some grammarians treat it as meaning inevitably, with no escape. Innahu refers to Allah. Lā yuḥibbu is negated love, indicating dislike, disapproval. Al-mustakbirīn from k-b-r, to be great, in form X meaning to seek greatness, to deem self great, to show arrogance. The form X indicates effort to be great, not merely being great. This nuance matters: arrogance is not accidental greatness but sought, performed greatness. The verse's two parts — knowledge and dislike — are both in present tense, indicating ongoing reality: Allah knows ongoing concealment and revelation, and He does not like ongoing arrogance. The warning is therefore current, not only future.

**Why Dislike Of Arrogance Is Mercy To Others**

Allah's dislike of arrogance protects the humble. When arrogant are left unchecked, they oppress, mock revelation, and mislead others, as verses 24-25 show. By stating He does not like the arrogant, Allah warns that arrogance will not be allowed to dominate forever. The oppressed who hear this find comfort: the One who knows what arrogant conceal and reveal does not love their arrogance, so He will eventually hold them accountable. Al-Rāzī notes that divine dislike implies withdrawal of support and guidance, as 16:37 says Allah does not guide those He leaves to stray, and that withdrawal is itself consequence of arrogance, not arbitrary. The person who insists on being great will be left to his own smallness. The humble who recognize blessings and accept Oneness will find love, as Allah loves the grateful and patient.

**The Repetition Of Knowledge Phrase**

The phrase yaʿlamu mā yusirrūna wa-mā yuʿlinūn appears in 16:19 and again here in 16:23, almost identical. Repetition in Qur'an is never without purpose. First occurrence came after uncountable blessings and forgiveness, to remind that forgiveness requires sincerity. Second comes after describing hearts that deny and are arrogant, to warn that denial and arrogance are known even when hidden. The repetition frames the argument against idols: between two statements of divine knowledge, idols are shown to be ignorant and dead. The contrast is intentional: Allah knows hidden and open; idols know nothing, not even when they will be raised. Which deserves worship? The repetition also emphasizes that knowledge is central to divinity. A god who does not know cannot guide, cannot judge, cannot forgive. Allah's knowledge is what makes His forgiveness meaningful and His dislike just.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:24

> **And when it is said to them, “What has your Lord revealed?” They say, “Ancient fables!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Exposes Attitude**

Wa-idhā qīla lahum mādhā anzala rabbukum qālū asāṭīru al-awwalīn — and when it is said to them, what has your Lord revealed, they say, ancient fables. The question mādhā anzala rabbukum, what has your Lord revealed, is asked to test response to revelation. Rabbukum, your Lord, is personal, the One who created, sustains, knows. The expected answer from a grateful heart would be to acknowledge blessing. Instead, they say asāṭīru al-awwalīn — fables of ancients, tales of old peoples, myths. Asāṭīr is plural of usṭūrah, story, fable, often with connotation of fabricated tales that entertain but have no truth. Al-awwalīn means ancients, earlier peoples. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Makkan deniers called Qur'an asāṭīr because it contains stories of previous nations like Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, and they claimed these were merely old tales copied, not revelation. The same accusation appears in 6:25, 8:31, 23:83, 25:5, 27:68, 68:15, 83:13. It is a way to dismiss without engaging: label it as old stories, not relevant, not divine.

**Why They Call Revelation Fables**

Calling revelation fables serves two purposes for deniers. First, it avoids having to argue against its content. If Qur'an is from Allah, then its warnings about Hereafter and its calls to abandon idols must be taken seriously. If it is merely fables, it can be ignored as entertainment. Second, it protects social status. To admit that what Muhammad ﷺ recites is from your Lord would require changing worship, economy, and tribal customs. To call it fables allows continuation of existing ways while appearing to have a reason. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that asāṭīru al-awwalīn implies that these stories are not only old but also false, invented by ancients and now retold. The accusation is therefore that Prophet ﷺ is not a messenger but a storyteller who learned old tales from others, as 25:5 says: *And they say, fables of ancients which he has written down, and they are dictated to him morning and evening* (25:5). The sūrah will answer this accusation later by pointing to linguistic inimitability and by noting that the Prophet ﷺ was unlettered and had no human teacher for such stories.

**The Contrast With Believers' Answer**

Verse 30 will give contrasting answer from those mindful of Allah: when asked what has your Lord revealed, they say *all the best*. The two answers are placed to show difference between hearts: one sees revelation as fables, other as goodness. The question is same, answers opposite, revealing inner state. Al-Rāzī points out that the question mādhā anzala rabbukum is asked to both groups, but answers differ because hearts differ: one heart is munkiratun mustakbirūn, denying and arrogant, so it says fables; other heart is muttaqī, mindful, so it says goodness. The sūrah thus uses same question as test. What you say revelation is reveals what you are. This is why verse 23 said Allah knows what they conceal and reveal and does not like arrogant: their open answer fables reveals concealed arrogance.

**Fables Accusation And The Theme Of Knowledge**

The accusation asāṭīru al-awwalīn is ironic because the stories in Qur'an are presented as true accounts with lessons, not fables for amusement. The Qur'an says *In their stories is a lesson for those of understanding; it is not a fabricated tale* (12:111). By calling them fables, deniers show they do not reflect on purpose of stories. The stories of destroyed peoples are warnings, not entertainment. The sūrah opened with command at hand and said do not hasten it, then listed blessings as signs, then argued idols are dead. Stories of ancients who denied and were destroyed fit this pattern: they are evidence that command does come. To call them fables is to refuse to be mindful, yadhakkarūn, and to refuse to understand, yaʿqilūn. The accusation therefore is not literary criticism but moral evasion.

**How The Qur'an Answers The Fables Charge**

The Qur'an answers the fables charge in several ways throughout. One is by challenging to produce similar speech: if it is merely human fables, then produce something like it. Another is by pointing to Prophet's unlettered status: he did not read previous scriptures, so how would he know detailed stories of ancient peoples with accurate lessons? Another is by pointing to prophecies and knowledge of unseen that fables would not contain. In al-Naḥl specifically, answer comes via blessings: if Allah can create heavens and earth, cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, can He not reveal stories of past peoples as lessons? The One who creates what you do not know can reveal what you did not know. The accusation also forgets that fables of ancients, even if old, may be true; old does not mean false. Al-Qurṭubī notes that ancients' stories include both true and false; Qur'an distinguishes and confirms true.

**Linguistic Note On Asāṭīr**

Asāṭīr from s-ṭ-r means writings, lines, stories written down. Usṭūrah is a story, often with connotation of something written and passed down, sometimes with implication of falsehood. Al-awwalīn means first ones, ancients, earlier generations. The phrase asāṭīru al-awwalīn became a stock dismissal used by Makkans against Qur'an. Its use here shows that opposition to revelation often takes form of labeling rather than argument: call it myth, legend, old wives' tale, to avoid engaging. The verse's brevity — question and answer in one sentence — captures a scene repeated many times in Makkah: believers ask deniers what they think of revelation, and deniers dismiss with a wave: fables. The scene is familiar to anyone who has seen truth dismissed with a label.

**The Cost Of Calling Revelation Fables**

To call revelation fables is not a neutral literary judgment; it has consequences that verse 25 will detail. When a community's leaders label divine guidance as old tales, they block others from considering it. A common person who hears a respected elder say fables may never open the Book to check. The label becomes a barrier. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this is why the Qur'an treats mockery of revelation as serious wrongdoing, not mere opinion. It is a form of misguidance without knowledge, as verse 25 says. The label also reveals arrogance: to call Allah's speech fables is to place one's own judgment above divine speech, as if one knows better what is true. The verse therefore captures both the words and the attitude behind them, showing that speech reflects heart.

**How Believers Should Respond To Such Dismissal**

The Qur'an teaches believers not to respond to fables accusation with anger alone but with clarity and patience. The sūrah later says *Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good exhortation* (16:125). When someone says revelation is fables, the believer is to show that it is not: it contains guidance, law, mercy, knowledge of unseen, linguistic miracle, and consistent message of tawḥīd that fables do not contain. The believer also recognizes that label fables often comes from lack of reflection on blessings. A heart that has not reflected on cattle, rain, and stars as signs will also not reflect on stories of past peoples as lessons. The remedy is same: reflection, remembrance, humility. The verse therefore invites believers to be patient with deniers while remaining firm that revelation is from their Lord, not fables.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:25

> **Let them bear their burdens in full on the Day of Judgment as well as some of the burdens of those they mislead without knowledge. Evil indeed is what they will bear!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bearing Burdens Fully And Partially**

Li-yaḥmilū awzārahum kāmilatan yawma al-qiyāmati wa-min awzāri al-ladhīna yuḍillūnahum bi-ghayri ʿilm — so that they may bear their burdens in full on Day of Resurrection and some of the burdens of those they mislead without knowledge. Awzār is plural of wizr, heavy burden, sin, load that weighs down. Kāmilatan means complete, full, without reduction. Yawma al-qiyāmah is Day of Resurrection, when all stand. The verse says those who called revelation fables will bear their own sins fully, plus some of sins of those they misled without knowledge. Al-Ṭabarī explains that leaders of disbelief who called people to shirk and to calling Qur'an fables will bear their own burden of disbelief plus a share of burden of followers who followed them in ignorance. This does not mean followers' burden is reduced; followers bear their own full burden as well, because they chose to follow. Rather, misleaders bear additional burden for being cause of others' misguidance. The same principle appears in 29:13: *And they will surely bear their burdens and other burdens with their burdens* (29:13) and in prophetic saying: *Whoever calls to guidance has reward like those who follow, and whoever calls to misguidance bears sin like those who follow, without reducing their sins* (Muslim 2674).

**Without Knowledge**

Bi-ghayri ʿilm — without knowledge — describes both misleaders and misled. Misleaders mislead without knowledge, meaning their claim that revelation is fables is based on ignorance, not knowledge. They do not know that it is revelation; they assert without evidence. Those misled are also without knowledge, meaning they follow without knowledge, blind imitation. Al-Rāzī notes that bi-ghayri ʿilm is crucial: if misguidance were with knowledge, it would be even worse, but even without knowledge, responsibility remains because knowledge was available via signs, blessings, and messengers. The phrase also indicates that misleaders deceive others by presenting ignorance as knowledge: they say fables with confidence, as if they know, while they do not know. Their burden includes the sin of speaking without knowledge, which the Qur'an condemns in 16:116: *Do not say what your tongues falsely describe, this is lawful and this is forbidden, inventing lie against Allah* (16:116).

**Evil Is What They Bear**

Alā sā'a mā yazirūn — evil indeed is what they bear, what a wretched load they carry. Sā'a means evil, bad, wretched. Yazirūn from w-z-r means to bear burden, to carry load. The exclamation alā is for attention: behold, how evil is what they bear. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that this is a statement of censure: their burden is not only heavy but evil in nature, because it includes shirk, denial, mockery, and leading others astray. The burden metaphor is powerful: sins are like loads carried on back, weighing down, making journey to Hereafter difficult. On Day of Judgment, everyone carries own deeds as load.
