Misleaders carry extra because they added others' misguidance to their own. The image of bearing burdens also connects to earlier verse about cattle bearing athqāl, heavy loads, to distant lands without hardship. Cattle bear physical loads as mercy; misleaders bear sinful loads as punishment. One bearing is gift, other is consequence.

**Why Misleaders Bear Others' Burdens**

The principle that one bears burden of those he misleads may seem unjust at first: why should one bear another's sin? Al-Qurṭubī clarifies that it is not that sin is transferred, but that misleader earns additional sin for being cause. When a person calls to misguidance, he initiates a chain of wrongdoing that continues after him. Each person who follows commits his own sin, but the initiator also earns sin for initiating. This is similar to reward for calling to guidance: caller earns reward for each follower's good deed without reducing followers' reward. The justice is in causality: you are responsible for what you cause, not only what you do directly. The Qur'an says *That they may bear their burdens completely and some of burdens of those they mislead* — min, some, not all, indicating that misleaders bear a portion of followers' burden equal to their share in causing misguidance, not entire burden.

**The Connection To Fables Accusation**

Verse 24 said they call revelation ancient fables. Verse 25 says consequence: they will bear burdens fully and some of those they mislead without knowledge. The link is that calling revelation fables is not a private opinion; it is public misguidance that leads others astray. When a respected Makkan says Qur'an is fables, others who trust him may also dismiss it without investigating. Thus his words become cause for others' denial. The verse warns that speech has consequences beyond speaker. To label revelation as fable may seem like a small remark, but on Day of Judgment it will be a heavy load, plus loads of those who were influenced. This is why the Qur'an takes speech seriously: *Not a word does he utter but there is a watcher ready* (50:18).

**Linguistic Note On Wizr And Ḥaml**

Wizr from w-z-r means heavy burden, sin, from same root as wazīr, minister, one who bears burden of king, and wizr, mountain refuge, because it bears weight. Ḥaml from ḥ-m-l means to carry, bear, as in cattle bearing loads in 16:7. The same verb is used for both physical and sinful loads, creating deliberate parallel: cattle bear your physical burdens as mercy, you will bear sinful burdens as justice. Kāmilatan, complete, from k-m-l, to be complete, indicates full, not partial, bearing. The phrase alā sā'a mā yazirūn is an exclamatory condemnation, common in Qur'an to express horror at evil deed. The verse's structure — purpose clause li-yaḥmilū, then description of burdens, then exclamation — moves from cause to consequence to evaluation, guiding listener to feel weight of misguidance.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:26

> **Indeed, those before them had plotted, but Allah struck at the ˹very˺ foundation of their structure, so the roof collapsed on top of them, and the torment came upon them from where they did not expect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Plotting That Seems Secure**

Qad makara al-ladhīna min qablihim — indeed those before them had plotted. Makara means to plot, scheme, plan secretly with cunning, often to deceive or harm. Al-ladhīna min qablihim refers to previous nations who plotted against their messengers, like people of Nūḥ, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Pharaoh. Their plotting included mocking messengers, calling revelation fables, building structures of power to oppose truth. Al-Ṭabarī explains that makr here includes both ideological plotting, like saying revelation is fables, and physical plotting, like building strongholds to resist. The verse says qad, indeed, emphasizing that this is not hypothetical; it happened. Those before them plotted, just as Makkans plot now. The past is precedent. The sūrah uses past to warn present: you are not first to plot, and you will not be first to escape consequence.

**Striking At The Foundation**

Fa-atā Allāhu bunyānahum min al-qawāʿid — but Allah struck at their building from its foundations. Bunyān means structure, building, edifice, both literal and metaphorical: physical towers, palaces, and also systems of power, ideology, and social order built on falsehood. Qawāʿid means foundations, bases, supports. Al-Ṭabarī says Allah came upon their building from foundations, meaning He destroyed it from its base, causing total collapse. The image is vivid: a building that looks strong because roof and walls stand, but foundations are undermined, so whole collapses. Al-Rāzī explains that foundation here can mean literal foundations of buildings they built to defy, or metaphorical foundations of their plotting, their arguments and power structures. Allah strikes at root, not only branches. The Makkans who built their economy on idol worship and their pride on ancestry had built a bunyān; this verse says Allah can strike its qawāʿid. The same root q-w-ʿ-d appears for foundations of Kaʿbah that Ibrāhīm raised, showing that foundations can be for good or evil.

**Roof Collapsing From Above**

Fa-kharra ʿalayhim al-saqfu min fawqihim — so the roof collapsed upon them from above them. Sakhkhara, roof, ceiling, covering. Kharra means to fall down, collapse. Min fawqihim, from above them, emphasizes that collapse came from direction they felt safest, from above, where roof should protect. Instead of protecting, roof becomes instrument of punishment. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the irony: they built roof to shelter themselves from truth, but roof fell on them. The image also conveys suddenness: a roof collapse is not gradual; it is immediate, crushing. The verse says al-saqfu, the roof, definite, indicating the roof of their own building, not another. Their own construction becomes their destruction. This is divine justice: the means they built for security becomes means of ruin. The same pattern appears in stories of nations destroyed by what they trusted: Pharaoh drowned in sea he crossed, Qārūn swallowed by earth whose treasures he hoarded.

**Torment From Where They Did Not Expect**

Wa-atāhum al-ʿadhābu min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn — and torment came upon them from where they did not perceive. ʿAdhāb means torment, punishment. Min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn, from where they did not expect, did not perceive, matches verse 21's lā yashʿurūn, they do not perceive when they will be raised. Their lack of perception is part of punishment. Al-Ṭabarī explains that punishment came from direction they did not anticipate, in manner they did not expect, at time they did not expect. This could be earthquake, flood, wind, or sudden humiliation. The phrase emphasizes that plotting, however clever, cannot cover all directions. Plotters guard front, but punishment comes from below, above, or inside. The verse teaches that security is only with Allah; those who plot against His messengers feel secure in their bunyān, but true security is not in building but in obedience.

**A Warning To Makkans And All Plotters**

The verse is addressed to Makkans who plotted against Prophet ﷺ, who met in Dār al-Nadwah to decide to imprison, kill, or expel him, as 8:30 says: *And when those who disbelieved plotted against you to restrain you or kill you or expel you* (8:30). Their plotting was secret, but Allah knew, as verse 19 and 23 said He knows what they conceal. The verse says those before you plotted and their building collapsed; yours can too. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this is both warning and consolation: warning to plotters that their foundations are vulnerable, consolation to believers that plotting against truth ultimately fails. The verse's placement after discussion of bearing burdens of those misled shows that plotters not only bear own burdens but also cause collapse of those who follow them. Their bunyān includes followers who trusted their roof.

**Linguistic Note On Makr And Bunyān**

Makara from m-k-r means to plot with subtlety, often with deceptive cunning. In Qur'an, makr is attributed to both humans and Allah, but Allah's makr is just counter-plotting that defeats human plotting, not deceptive in blameworthy sense. Bunyān from b-n-y means building, structure. Qawāʿid from q-ʿ-d means foundations, bases, also used for women past childbearing, those who sit, showing root sense of sitting, being fixed. Saqf from s-q-f means roof, covering. Kharra from kh-r-r means to fall with noise, to collapse. The verse's rhythm — fa-atā Allāhu bunyānahum min al-qawāʿidi fa-kharra ʿalayhim al-saqfu min fawqihim — repeats min and fawq, emphasizing from below and from above, foundations and roof, total collapse from all sides. The verse ends with from where they did not perceive, leaving listener with sense of vulnerability: you cannot guard all directions; only Allah can protect. This final phrase also links to earlier description of idols as dead who do not perceive, showing that plotters share their idols' lack of perception. They do not perceive where punishment comes from, just as idols do not perceive when they will be raised. Both are blind to reality because they turned away from signs.

**How Foundation Imagery Teaches Humility**

Foundation imagery teaches that what is hidden matters more than what is visible. A building's beauty is in walls and roof, but its survival depends on foundations unseen. Similarly, a person's apparent success may rest on hidden foundations of gratitude or ingratitude. If foundations are shirk and arrogance, collapse is inevitable even if walls look strong. The sūrah therefore invites self-examination: what are my foundations? Are they tawḥīd and gratitude, which Allah establishes firmly, or are they plotting and denial, which Allah can strike? The answer determines whether roof will shelter or crush.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:27

> **Then on the Day of Judgment He will humiliate them and say, “Where are My ˹so-called˺ associate-gods for whose sake you used to oppose ˹the believers˺?” Those gifted with knowledge will say, “Surely disgrace and misery today are upon the disbelievers.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Humiliation After Plotting**

Thumma yawma al-qiyāmati yukhzīhim — then on Day of Resurrection He will humiliate them, disgrace them. Yukhzīhim from kh-z-y means to humiliate, disgrace, expose shame. After describing worldly collapse of their building in verse 26, this verse moves to otherworldly humiliation. Thumma indicates sequence: first worldly punishment from unexpected direction, then Resurrection Day humiliation. Al-Ṭabarī explains yukhzīhim as exposing their falsehood and making their disgrace visible to all. In world, plotters may appear powerful, respected; on Day, their plotting is exposed and they are humiliated. The verse shows that divine justice has two stages: immediate and ultimate. Worldly collapse may be seen by some, but Resurrection humiliation will be seen by all. This is both warning and consolation: oppressors may seem to escape in world, but they will not escape humiliation on Day.

**Where Are My Associate-Gods?**

Wa-yaqūlu ayna shurakā'iya al-ladhīna kuntum tushāqqūna fīhim — and He will say, where are My partners for whose sake you used to oppose believers? Shurakā'ī, My partners, is said by Allah with irony: those you claimed as partners to Me, where are they? The phrase al-ladhīna kuntum tushāqqūna fīhim means those for whose sake you used to oppose, show enmity, split from believers. Tushāqqūna from sh-q-q means to oppose, to be on opposite side, to cause split. Al-Ṭabarī explains that idolaters opposed believers, argued, fought, and broke relations for sake of idols. They defended idols, sacrificed for idols, and made enmity for idols. On Day, they will be asked, where are those idols now? Can they help you against humiliation? The question is rhetorical to expose impotence. The idols are absent, dead, not knowing when they will be raised, as verse 21 said. The question will be asked to increase humiliation: you opposed truth for sake of what cannot even appear to help you.

**Those Gifted With Knowledge Speak**

Qāla al-ladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma — those given knowledge will say. Who are they? Al-Ṭabarī says they are angels, prophets, scholars, believers who were given knowledge in world and now witness outcome. Al-Rāzī says they are those who knew in world that shirk leads to disgrace, and now they declare what they knew. Their statement: inna al-khizya al-yawma wa-l-sū'a ʿalā al-kāfirīn — surely disgrace and misery today are upon disbelievers. Khizy means disgrace, humiliation, shame. Sū' means evil, misery, suffering. The phrase indicates that today, Day of Resurrection, disgrace and evil are specifically upon disbelievers, not upon believers. Those given knowledge announce this as witness, confirming divine justice. Their speech also contrasts with earlier speech of deniers who said ancient fables. Now those with knowledge speak truth that is visible: disgrace is upon disbelievers.

**Why Knowledgeable Speak On That Day**

The fact that those given knowledge speak on Day of Judgment shows that knowledge in world has value in Hereafter. Those who sought knowledge of tawḥīd, who reflected on blessings, who understood signs, will be given role as witnesses. Al-Qurṭubī notes that ūtū al-ʿilma includes both revealed knowledge and sound reasoning that leads to truth. Their statement is not new judgment but declaration of what is already happening: disgrace is upon disbelievers. They say it to affirm and to make it known. This also shows that knowledge is not only for personal salvation but for bearing witness. In world, scholars warned disbelievers; in Hereafter, they witness that warning was true. The verse thus honors knowledge and encourages seeking it.

**The Connection To Arrogance**

Verse 27 mentions humiliation, khizy, which is opposite of arrogance, istikbār, mentioned in verses 22-23, 29. Arrogant sought greatness in world by opposing believers for sake of idols; on Day they are humiliated. The Qur'an often pairs arrogance in world with humiliation in Hereafter, as in 40:76: *Enter gates of Hell to abide therein; evil indeed is abode of arrogant* (40:76). The question *Where are My partners?* also humiliates because it exposes that what they were arrogant for is nothing. They were proud of idols, proud of opposing believers, proud of their plotting. Now idols absent, opposition failed, plotting collapsed. Humiliation is therefore fitting recompense: they sought honor through false gods, they receive disgrace because false gods cannot honor.

**Translation Note On So-Called And Believers**

Translators add ˹so-called˺ before associate-gods to indicate that shurakā' are not true partners but claimed partners. The Arabic shurakā'ī, My partners, is in Allah's speech with irony, as if Allah says you called them My partners, where are they? The bracket clarifies irony. Similarly, ˹the believers˺ is added to explain fīhim, for whose sake you opposed, meaning you opposed believers for sake of idols. The Arabic tushāqqūna fīhim could mean you opposed each other concerning them, or you opposed believers concerning them. Tafsīr explains it as you showed enmity toward believers for sake of idols, you split from believers because of idols. The brackets help English reader understand that opposition was directed at believers, not just abstract. The verse's two parts — divine question and knowledgeable answer — create a courtroom scene: Judge asks where are partners, witnesses declare disgrace upon disbelievers, leaving no doubt about verdict.

**The Courtroom Imagery And Its Effect**

The scene in this verse is judicial. Allah questions, those given knowledge testify, disgrace is declared upon disbelievers. This mirrors worldly courts where accused is asked about accomplices and witnesses testify. Here accomplices are idols who cannot appear, and witnesses are those who knew truth in world. Al-Rāzī notes that this courtroom imagery is meant to make listeners imagine themselves in that position before it happens, so they may change course. The question *Where are My partners?* is asked not because Allah does not know, but to make the accused admit their absence. The testimony *disgrace today is upon disbelievers* is not new information to Allah but declaration for all to hear. The combined effect is to show that Day of Judgment is public, not private, and humiliation is part of punishment. Those who sought status through idols will lose status publicly.

**How Knowledge Becomes Witness**

Those given knowledge in world become witnesses in Hereafter because knowledge entails responsibility to convey. The Qur'an says *Thus We have made you a middle community that you may be witnesses over people* (2:143). In this verse, those given knowledge witness that disgrace is upon disbelievers, confirming that warnings given in world were true. This encourages seeking knowledge not only for personal benefit but for role as witness for truth. Al-Qurṭubī says ūtū al-ʿilma includes prophets, angels, and scholars who taught tawḥīd. Their testimony on that Day will be against those who called revelation fables and plotted. The verse therefore honors knowledge and warns that ignoring knowledgeable is ignoring future witnesses against you.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:28

> **Those whose souls the angels seize while they wrong themselves will then offer ˹full˺ submission ˹and say falsely,˺ “We did not do any evil.” ˹The angels will say,˺ “No! Surely Allah fully knows what you used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Souls Seized While Wronging Themselves**

Al-ladhīna tatawaffāhum al-malā'ikatu ẓālimī anfusihim — those whom angels take in death while they are wronging themselves. Tatawaffā from w-f-y means to take fully, to cause to die, to take soul. Al-malā'ikah, angels, are agents of death, as in 32:11: *Say, the angel of death who is entrusted with you will take you* (32:11). Ẓālimī anfusihim means wronging themselves, wrongdoers to themselves, because sin harms self first. Al-Ṭabarī explains that wronging self here means persisting in shirk, denial, and opposition to messengers, thereby harming own souls that will face punishment. The phrase indicates that at moment of death, they are still in state of wrongdoing, not having repented. Angels come to take souls while they are in that state, making death itself a moment of exposure. The same phrase appears in 4:97 for those who wronged themselves by staying among disbelievers without emigrating when able.

**Offering Submission At Last Moment**

Fa-alqaw al-salama — then they will offer submission, peace, surrender. Alqaw al-salama means to throw down submission, to offer peace, to surrender. In this context, it means when they see angels and realize death and punishment, they will surrender and claim innocence. Al-Qurṭubī explains that alqaw al-salama here means they will offer submission and claim they were not doing evil, as continuation says: mā kunnā naʿmalu min sū' — we were not doing any evil. This is false claim to escape. Al-Rāzī notes that this submission is not sincere repentance but desperate attempt to avoid consequence when consequence is already present. The Qur'an describes similar deathbed claims in 6:23: *By Allah, our Lord, we were not polytheists* (6:23), and in 63:10: *My Lord, if only You would delay me for brief term so I would give charity* (63:10). All are too late.

**We Did Not Do Any Evil — A False Defense**

Mā kunnā naʿmalu min sū' — we were not doing any evil. Sū' means evil, bad deed, sin. They deny wrongdoing at moment of death, hoping denial will help. Al-Ṭabarī says they deny that they were associating partners, calling revelation fables, plotting, opposing believers. They claim they were not doing evil, meaning they did not consider their deeds evil or they pretend they did not do. This denial is both self-deception and attempted deception of angels. It shows that arrogance persists even at death: instead of admitting fault, they deny. The phrase min sū' with min indicates any evil, even small, they deny all. This is contrast to believers who at death are described as ṭayyibīn, good, pure, acknowledging.

**No, Allah Knows What You Did**

Balā inna Allāha ʿalīmun bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn — No, indeed Allah knows what you used to do. Balā is strong negation of their denial: no, not as you claim. Inna Allāha ʿalīmun, surely Allah is Knowing of what you used to do. The response is attributed to angels or to Allah via angels, as tafsīr differs. Some say angels respond, some say Allah responds, some say those given knowledge respond. In any case, message is that denial fails because Allah knows. This echoes verses 19 and 23: Allah knows what you conceal and reveal. At death, hidden deeds are known. The phrase bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn, what you used to do, indicates ongoing deeds, habitual wrongdoing, not single mistake. Kuntum taʿmalūn implies continuity: you used to do, you persisted.

**Why This Scene Is Placed Here**

After describing worldly collapse of plotters and Resurrection humiliation, the sūrah now describes death scene, which is intermediate between world and Resurrection. It shows that humiliation begins at death, not only on Day. Those who plotted and called revelation fables will at death try to surrender and deny, but angels will reject denial. This prepares for verse 29: *Enter gates of Hell to abide therein*. The sequence is: plotting in world, building collapse, death with false submission, angels' rebuttal, entry to Hell, then contrast with righteous. The scene also teaches that submission must be in world before death, not at death when seen. The Qur'an says *But repentance is not for those who do evil until when death comes to one of them, he says, indeed I have repented now* (4:18). True submission is before seeing angels.

**Linguistic Note On Tawaffā And Salam**

Tawaffā from w-f-y means to take fully, to receive in full, used for death because soul is taken fully. Ẓālimī anfusihim, wrongers of themselves, from ẓ-l-m, to wrong, to put thing in wrong place. Alqaw from l-q-y means to throw, cast, offer. Salama from s-l-m means peace, submission, surrender, from same root as islām. In this context, al-salam means submission, surrender, offering peace to angels to avoid punishment. Mā kunnā naʿmalu min sū' — we were not doing evil — uses mā for negation, kunnā for past continuous, naʿmalu for doing. Balā is particle for negating negative statement, meaning yes indeed, no, contrary to what you say. The verse ends without closing quote in translation file, but meaning is complete: Allah knows what you used to do, so denial fails.

**The Difference Between Two Death Scenes**

This verse describes death of wrongdoers, while verse 32 will describe death of virtuous: *those whose souls angels take while they are virtuous, saying Peace be upon you*. The two death scenes are contrasted deliberately. Wrongdoers are taken while wronging themselves, offer false submission, claim no evil, and are told Allah knows what they did, then commanded to enter Hell. Virtuous are taken while virtuous, greeted with peace, told to enter Paradise for what they did. The contrast shows that death is not uniform; it reflects life. Al-Qurṭubī notes that angels take souls in manner that matches deeds: harshly for wrongdoers, gently for righteous. The phrase ẓālimī anfusihim vs ṭayyibīn captures this: one group wronged selves, other purified selves. The lesson is that preparation for death is preparation in life.

**Why False Claim Of No Evil Fails**

Claiming *we did not do any evil* fails for three reasons. First, Allah knows, as verse says. Second, angels have recorded deeds, as 82:10-12 says *Indeed over you are keepers, noble writers who know what you do* (82:10-12). Third, limbs will testify, as 36:65 says *Today We seal their mouths and their hands speak to Us and their feet bear witness to what they used to earn* (36:65). The false claim therefore is futile. It also reveals self-deception: they truly believed their deeds were not evil because their hearts were in denial and arrogant, as verse 22 said. Their denial at death is continuation of denial in life. The angels' response *No, Allah knows* breaks self-deception and establishes truth before punishment.

**A Lesson On Sincerity Before Death**

The verse teaches that sincerity must be before death. Once angels arrive, claims of innocence are not accepted. True repentance is in world, when unseen, not at death when seen. The Qur'an says *Until when death comes to one of them, he says My Lord send me back, that I might do righteousness in what I left behind. No, it is a word he speaks* (23:99-100). The time for doing good is now, while soul is still in body and choice remains. The verse therefore urges listener not to delay repentance, not to be among those who wrong themselves and then offer false submission at last moment. The door of repentance is open now, as verse 18 said Allah is Forgiving, Merciful, but it closes at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:29

> **So enter the gates of Hell, to stay there forever. Indeed, what an evil home for the arrogant!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Command To Enter Hell**

Fa-udkhulū abwāba jahannama khālidīna fīhā — so enter gates of Hell to abide therein forever. The command udkhulū, enter, is said to those whose souls were taken while wronging themselves and who falsely claimed innocence. Abwāba jahannama, gates of Hell, indicates Hell has gates, doors, levels, as in 15:44: *It has seven gates, for each gate is a portion assigned* (15:44). Entering gates implies being ushered into punishment, not merely seeing it. Khālidīna fīhā means abiding therein forever, remaining permanently. Al-Ṭabarī explains khālidīna as remaining without end for those who died in shirk and denial. The command is given by angels after rebutting false claim of innocence. It shows that after death, there is no chance to argue; judgment is executed. The phrase fa-udkhulū, so enter, indicates consequence: because you wronged yourselves, denied, claimed no evil, and Allah knows what you did, therefore enter.

**What An Evil Home For The Arrogant**

Fa-la-bi'sa mathwā al-mutakabbirīn — indeed what an evil abode, dwelling, home for the arrogant. Bi'sa is exclamation of dispraise: how evil, how wretched. Mathwā from th-w-y means abode, dwelling, place where one stays, from same root as thāwī, resident. Al-mutakabbirīn, arrogant, those who sought greatness, refused truth. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that mathwā indicates place of residence, home, implying that Hell will be their home, where they stay, not temporary visit. The phrase la-bi'sa mathwā al-mutakabbirīn is used elsewhere: *So enter gates of Hell to abide therein; evil indeed is abode of arrogant* (16:29; 39:72; 40:76). Repetition shows that arrogance is specifically linked to Hell as its home. Why? Because arrogant refused to be servants in world, claimed greatness, so their fitting home is abode of humiliation.

**Why Arrogance Leads To Hell**

Arrogance, istikbār and takabbur, is repeatedly linked to Hell because it prevents acceptance of truth. Arrogant deem themselves too great to worship Allah alone, to follow messenger, to accept that they were created from drop and cannot count blessings. Their hearts were munkiratun, denying, and mustakbirūn, arrogant, as verse 22 said, and Allah does not like arrogant, as verse 23 said. Now consequence: evil home for arrogant. Al-Rāzī explains that arrogance is root of all sins: Iblīs refused to prostrate due to arrogance, Pharaoh claimed lordship due to arrogance, Makkan chiefs rejected Prophet ﷺ due to arrogance. Hell is fitting because Hell humiliates, and arrogant need humiliation to realize truth they denied. The Qur'an says *But those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell humiliated* (40:60). The humiliation in Hell is opposite of greatness sought in world.

**The Contrast With Righteous Home**

Verse 30-31 will contrast this evil home with excellent home of righteous: *How excellent indeed is home of righteous, Gardens of Eternity* (16:30-31). The pairing of evil home vs excellent home is deliberate: two homes, two groups, two answers to question what has your Lord revealed. Those who said ancient fables get evil home; those who said all best get excellent home. The sūrah uses mathwā for arrogant and dār for righteous? Actually verse 30 says *excellent is abode of righteous*, using mathwā? Let's check: verse 30 says *How excellent indeed is home of righteous* — same word mathwā? In Arabic, verse 30 says *wa-la-niʿma dāru al-muttaqīn*, excellent is abode of righteous, using dār, while verse 29 uses mathwā. Both mean home, but dār is more honorable. The contrast is clear: arrogant get wretched mathwā, righteous get excellent dār.

**Gates Of Hell As Levels**

Abwāba, gates, plural, indicates multiple entrances, each for different types of sinners or different levels of punishment. Some tafsīr say Hell has seven gates, each with different torment. Others say gates indicate that entry is from all sides, no escape. The command udkhulū abwāba, enter gates, suggests they will be driven through gates, not entering willingly. The Qur'an says *And those who disbelieved will be driven to Hell in groups until when they reach it, its gates are opened* (39:71). The opening of gates is moment of realization. Khālidīna fīhā, abiding forever, indicates permanence for those who died in shirk. For those who had some faith but sins, there may be eventual exit by mercy, but for arrogant who denied Hereafter and called revelation fables, abiding is forever.

**Linguistic Note On Bi'sa And Mathwā**

Bi'sa from b-'-s means to be evil, wretched, from same root as ba's, hardship, might. It is used for dispraise, opposite of niʿma, how excellent. Mathwā from th-w-y means place where one stays, dwells, from thawa, to stay, reside. Mutakabbirīn from k-b-r, to be great, form V and X meaning to show greatness, be arrogant. The phrase fa-la-bi'sa mathwā al-mutakabbirīn is exclamatory, with la- for emphasis: indeed how evil is home of arrogant. The verse's brevity — two sentences — delivers final verdict after death scene: enter, abide, evil home. It closes the passage on plotters and deniers, preparing for contrasting passage on righteous.

**Why Gates Are Mentioned**

Gates suggest that Hell is enclosed, with entry points that are controlled. No one enters without permission, and no one exits without permission. The image of gates also suggests that Hell has already been prepared, its gates exist now, waiting. The Qur'an says *Indeed Hell has been lying in wait* (78:21). The command to enter gates therefore is not creation of new punishment but ushering into already prepared abode. Al-Rāzī notes that mentioning gates also indicates that Hell has different levels of punishment appropriate to different sins, and each gate leads to different level. The arrogant who opposed believers for sake of idols will enter through gate appropriate to arrogance and shirk. The mention of gates also contrasts with gates of Paradise that will be opened for righteous, as 39:73 says *Until when they reach it, its gates are opened and its keepers say Peace be upon you* (39:73). Two sets of gates, two greetings, two homes.

**The Permanence Of Abiding**

Khālidīna fīhā, abiding therein forever, is a phrase that appears many times for both Paradise and Hell, indicating permanence. For Hell, it indicates that those who die in shirk and denial will not exit. Al-Ṭabarī explains that khulūd, abiding forever, means remaining without end, without death, without exit. This permanence is fitting because shirk and arrogance were choices that, if persisted until death, become fixed character. The person who insisted on being great and refused to submit in world has chosen a character that cannot exist in Paradise, which requires humility and submission. Therefore Hell becomes their fitting, permanent home, mathwā, place where that character belongs. The verse ends with exclamation of dispraise to make listener feel horror and avoid that home by abandoning arrogance now, while choice remains.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:30

> **And ˹when˺ it is said to those mindful ˹of Allah˺, “What has your Lord revealed?” They say, “All the best!” For those who do good in this world, there is goodness. But far better is the ˹eternal˺ Home of the Hereafter. How excellent indeed is the home of the righteous:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Contrasting Answer To Same Question**

Wa-idhā qīla li-l-ladhīna ittaqaw mādhā anzala rabbukum qālū khayran — and when it is said to those who are mindful of Allah, what has your Lord revealed, they say, all the best, goodness. This mirrors verse 24 where same question was asked to deniers and they said ancient fables. Now question asked to muttaqīn, those mindful, reverent, God-conscious, and they say khayran, goodness, best. Khayr means good, best, beneficial. Al-Ṭabarī explains that muttaqīn answer with khayran because they see revelation as good, containing guidance, mercy, law, stories that teach, promises that encourage. Their answer is short but comprehensive: all the best. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that khayran is indefinite, indicating great goodness, all kinds of goodness, not limited. The contrast between fables and goodness reveals hearts: deniers see revelation as old tales to dismiss, mindful see it as best to follow. Same revelation, opposite perceptions, due to difference in taqwa.

**Goodness In This World For Those Who Do Good**

Li-l-ladhīna aḥsanū fī hādhihi al-dunyā ḥasanatun — for those who do good in this world, there is goodness. Aḥsanū from ḥ-s-n means to do good, excellence, beautiful deeds. Ḥasanah means goodness, good reward, good outcome. The verse says those who do good in this world will have goodness in this world as well as Hereafter. Al-Ṭabarī says ḥasanah in world includes provision, health, honor, good mention, help. Al-Rāzī adds that doing good includes both faith and righteous deeds, and goodness in world includes contentment, guidance, and blessings that are experienced as good even if not material. The phrase fī hādhihi al-dunyā, in this world, indicates that goodness is not delayed only to Hereafter; it begins here. This is encouragement: doing good is not loss in world; it brings goodness now.

**Far Better Is Home Of Hereafter**

Wa-la-dāru al-ākhirati khayrun — but home of Hereafter is better. Lā here is emphatic, surely, indeed. Dāru al-ākhirah, home of Hereafter, is better than goodness in world. Al-Qurṭubī explains that even if worldly goodness is given to righteous, Hereafter is better in quality, quantity, and duration. Worldly goodness is mixed with hardship, temporary, limited; Hereafter goodness is pure, eternal, unlimited. The comparison wa-la-dāru al-ākhirati khayrun is similar to 87:17: *But Hereafter is better and more lasting* (87:17). The verse does not say worldly goodness is bad; it says Hereafter is better, so believer should not be satisfied with only worldly goodness but should seek Hereafter as well. This balances encouragement for worldly goodness with reminder that ultimate goal is Hereafter.

**How Excellent Is Home Of Righteous**

Wa-la-niʿma dāru al-muttaqīn — and how excellent indeed is home of righteous. Niʿma is exclamation of praise: how excellent, how good. Dāru al-muttaqīn, home of those mindful, those who are conscious of Allah. This mirrors verse 29's *evil home for arrogant* with opposite: excellent home for righteous. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that niʿma vs bi'sa creates deliberate contrast: bi'sa mathwā al-mutakabbirīn, evil home for arrogant, vs niʿma dāru al-muttaqīn, excellent home for righteous. Two homes, two groups, two answers. The verse ends with colon in translation, indicating that next verse will describe that home: Gardens of Eternity. The colon is translator's addition to show connection.

**Why Mindful See Revelation As Goodness**

Muttaqīn, those mindful, see revelation as khayr because taqwa, mindfulness, opens eyes to see goodness. A heart that is mindful of Allah recognizes that what comes from Allah must be good, even if it includes commands that require effort or stories that warn. The deniers' hearts were munkiratun, denying, and mustakbirūn, arrogant, so they saw fables. The mindful's hearts are aware, humble, so they see goodness. This shows that perception of revelation depends on heart state, not only on text. The same sun hardens clay and softens wax; same revelation hardens arrogant heart and softens mindful heart. The sūrah's blessings catalogue was meant to cultivate taqwa by showing uncountable blessings, so that when question what has your Lord revealed is asked, answer will be goodness.

**Linguistic Note On Khayr And Dār**

Khayr from kh-y-r means good, best, choice, from same root as ikhtiyār, choice, indicating what is chosen as good. In this verse, khayran is accusative, meaning they said: He revealed goodness, or it is goodness. Ḥasanah from ḥ-s-n means good, beautiful, from same root as iḥsān, excellence. Dār from d-w-r means house, home, abode, place where one circles, resides. Muttaqīn from w-q-y means those who guard themselves, mindful, from same root as taqwā. The verse's structure — question, short answer khayran, then elaboration on goodness in world and Hereafter, then praise of home of righteous — moves from concise to expansive, reflecting that goodness of revelation cannot be captured in one word khayran but needs elaboration: goodness now, better home later, excellent home for righteous.

**Goodness Now And Better Later**

The promise li-l-ladhīna aḥsanū ḥasanah — for those who do good there is goodness — is both immediate and future. In world, goodness includes guidance, tranquility, provision that is blessed, and good reputation. Al-Rāzī notes that aḥsanū includes doing good to others, not only worship, so goodness in return includes love of people and help from Allah. The verse says fī hādhihi al-dunyā, in this world, to emphasize that goodness is not only deferred. Many think righteousness means losing worldly enjoyment; this verse says opposite: those who do good get goodness here too. Then it adds Hereafter is better, so believer does not become attached to worldly goodness as final goal. The balance is precise: enjoy worldly goodness as gift, but seek better home.

**Why The Home Is Called Excellent**

Niʿma dāru al-muttaqīn — how excellent is home of righteous — is praise that invites desire. The Qur'an often uses niʿma to praise Paradise, as in 3:136 and 29:58. The home is excellent because it is free from hardship, pure, eternal, and contains whatever righteous desire, as next verse says: *they will have whatever they desire* (16:31). Al-Qurṭubī explains that dār here is excellent because its Builder is Allah, its inhabitants are righteous, its duration is forever, and its contents are beyond imagination. The colon at end of translation indicates that description follows, linking this verse to next. The listener who hears excellent home will naturally ask, what is that home? Next verse answers: Gardens of Eternity under which rivers flow.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:31

> **the Gardens of Eternity which they will enter, under which rivers flow. In it they will have whatever they desire. This is how Allah rewards the righteous—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gardens Of Eternity**

Jannātu ʿadnin yadkhulūnahā tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru — Gardens of Eternity which they will enter, under which rivers flow. Jannāt is plural of jannah, garden, Paradise. ʿAdn means eternity, permanence, residence, from ʿ-d-n, to remain, to be fixed. Gardens of Eternity are gardens where residence is permanent, not temporary. Yadkhulūnahā, they will enter, indicates future entry as reward. Tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhāru, under which rivers flow, is a phrase repeated dozens of times in Qur'an to describe Paradise, indicating abundance of water, coolness, greenery, and continuous provision. Al-Ṭabarī explains that rivers flowing beneath gardens means beneath their trees and palaces, visible, accessible, providing beauty and drink. The phrase emphasizes that Paradise is not dry or barren but lush, with water that does not cease. The gardens are for muttaqīn, righteous, whose home was described as excellent in previous verse.

**Whatever They Desire**

Lahum fīhā mā yashā'ūn — in it they will have whatever they desire, whatever they wish. Mā yashā'ūn means whatever they will, desire, want. This is a comprehensive promise: in Paradise, desires are fulfilled without restriction, unlike world where desires often exceed ability or are prohibited. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this includes both what they desired in world and what they will desire in Paradise, and that their desires themselves will be purified, so they will not desire anything harmful. The phrase appears in several descriptions of Paradise, as in 41:31: *You will have therein whatever your souls desire* (41:31). Al-Rāzī explains that having whatever one desires is the ultimate freedom, because in world humans are constrained by need, law, and inability, while in Paradise constraints are removed for righteous, yet their will is aligned with good, so they desire only good.

**How Allah Rewards The Righteous**

Kadhālika yajzī Allāhu al-muttaqīn — thus does Allah reward the righteous, the mindful. Kadhālika means like this, thus, in this way. Yajzī means rewards, recompense. Al-muttaqīn, those mindful of Allah, those who guarded themselves by obedience. The phrase indicates that Gardens described are how Allah rewards taqwa. It is not arbitrary; it is fitting recompense: because they were mindful of Allah in world, guarded themselves from shirk and sin, Allah rewards with Gardens where they have whatever they desire. Al-Ṭabarī says kadhālika refers to reward described: Gardens with rivers, fulfillment of desires, as reward for taqwa. The verse ends with dash in translation, indicating that next verse continues description: those whose souls angels take while virtuous.

**Why Eternity Matters**

ʿAdn, eternity, is stressed because worldly gardens are temporary: they wither, need maintenance, can be lost. Gardens of Eternity do not wither, do not need maintenance that tires, and are not lost. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that ʿadn also implies residence that is settled, not traveling, so inhabitants feel at home, not as visitors. This contrasts with worldly life which is travel, as Prophet ﷺ said *Be in world as stranger or traveler* (Bukhārī 6416). In world, even excellent home is temporary; in Hereafter, excellent home is eternal. The promise of eternity also answers those who said life is only worldly and there is no return. It says there is return and it is eternal, so choices in temporary world have eternal consequences.

**The Connection To Earlier Blessings**

Gardens with rivers flowing recall earlier blessings: rain from sky, rivers on earth, crops, fruits. In world, those blessings are signs and temporary provision. In Paradise, they become permanent reward, perfected. Rivers in world can flood or dry; rivers in Paradise flow continuously without harm. Fruits in world are seasonal; fruits in Paradise are always available, as 13:35 says *Its fruit is permanent and its shade* (13:35). Whatever they desire in Paradise includes best of what they had in world, purified and increased. The sūrah thus moves from worldly blessings that are uncountable but temporary to otherworldly blessings that are eternal, showing that gratitude for worldly blessings leads to eternal reward.

**Linguistic Note On ʿAdn And Jazā'**

ʿAdn from ʿ-d-n means to remain, to be fixed, to stay, also used for mine, maʿdin, because minerals remain in earth. Jannātu ʿadnin therefore means Gardens of permanence, residence. Yadkhulūnahā from d-kh-l means to enter, with plural indicating group entry, not solitary. Tajrī from j-r-y means to flow, run. Anhār plural of nahr, river. Mā yashā'ūn from sh-y-ʾ means whatever they will, wish. Yajzī from j-z-y means to reward, recompense, from same root as jazā', reward. Al-muttaqīn from w-q-y means those who guard, mindful. The verse's structure — Gardens, entry, rivers, desires, reward — moves from place to action to provision to wish to recompense, creating a complete picture of reward that is both objective (Gardens, rivers) and subjective (whatever they desire), both gift and fitting reward for taqwa.

**Why Rivers Flowing Is Repeated**

The phrase under which rivers flow appears dozens of times in Qur'an as anchor of imagination. Water in desert is life, coolness, beauty, and its flow indicates continuity. A garden with river that stops would disappoint; a garden where rivers flow continuously is secure. Al-Rāzī notes that rivers flowing beneath indicates inhabitants are above rivers, in elevated position, looking down at flowing water, a scene of honor. The rivers are of different types: water, milk, honey, wine that does not intoxicate, as 47:15 says. The diversity matches diversity of desires. Repetition is not filler but reminder that Paradise provision is flowing, not stagnant, always fresh.

**Desire Fulfilled As Ultimate Freedom**

Having whatever they desire is ultimate freedom because in world desires are constrained by inability, prohibition, and consequence. In Paradise, constraints are removed for righteous whose desires have been purified by taqwa. Al-Qurṭubī explains that they will not desire harmful because hearts purified, as 15:47 says. So fulfillment does not lead to excess; it leads to contentment. The phrase mā yashā'ūn indicates desires granted as they arise, without delay, without work. This is opposite of world where desire requires effort and may not be fulfilled. The promise is both about provision and will: your will, frustrated in world, will be fulfilled in Paradise as reward for aligning will with Allah's will through taqwa.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:32

> **those whose souls the angels take while they are virtuous, saying ˹to them˺, “Peace be upon you! Enter Paradise for what you used to do.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Souls Taken While Virtuous**

Al-ladhīna tatawaffāhum al-malā'ikatu ṭayyibīn — those whose souls angels take while they are virtuous, good, pure. Tatawaffāhum al-malā'ikatu, angels take them, same verb as in verse 28 for wrongdoers, but state differs: ẓālimī anfusihim vs ṭayyibīn. Ṭayyibīn means good, pure, virtuous, from ṭ-y-b, to be good, pure, pleasant. Al-Ṭabarī explains ṭayyibīn as those who are pure from shirk, sincere in faith, good in deeds, or those whose souls are taken in easy, gentle manner, without hardship. The same root ṭayyib is used for good things, pure provisions. The contrast with verse 28 is deliberate: same angels, same act of taking souls, but different states and different greetings. One group wronged themselves, offered false submission, told they know what they did, commanded to enter Hell. Other group is virtuous, greeted with peace, told to enter Paradise for what they did. Death reveals what life cultivated.

**Peace Be Upon You**

Yaqūlūna salāmun ʿalaykum — saying, peace be upon you. Angels say salām, peace, security, greeting. Salāmun ʿalaykum is greeting of Paradise, also greeting among believers in world, and greeting from Allah, as 36:58 says *Peace, a word from a Merciful Lord* (36:58). Al-Qurṭubī says angels greet righteous at death with peace to calm fear, give glad tidings, and convey that they are safe from punishment. The greeting is in present tense, indicating ongoing peace, not temporary. It also indicates that angels are not harsh to righteous; they are gentle, welcoming. The same greeting will be heard in Paradise from angels and inhabitants, as 13:24 says *Peace be upon you for what you patiently endured* (13:24). The death greeting is foretaste of Paradise greeting.

**Enter Paradise For What You Used To Do**

Udkhulū al-jannata bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn — enter Paradise for what you used to do, because of what you used to do. Bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn indicates cause: because of your deeds, your faith, your patience, your good works. Al-Ṭabarī explains that entry to Paradise is by Allah's mercy, but deeds are cause and evidence of sincerity, and reward is according to deeds. The phrase does not mean Paradise is earned solely by deeds without mercy; it means deeds are sign of faith that Allah rewards. Al-Rāzī notes that bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn includes both inward and outward deeds: belief, gratitude, patience, trust, as verses 41-42 will mention for emigrants. The same phrase appears in other descriptions of Paradise entry, as in 16:32 and 52:19. It shows that Paradise is not arbitrary; it is for those who did what was required.

**Why This Verse Follows Gardens Description**

Verse 31 described Gardens of Eternity with rivers and fulfillment of desires as reward for righteous. Verse 32 explains who those righteous are: those whose souls angels take while virtuous, greeted with peace, told to enter Paradise for what they did. The sequence moves from reward to recipients to manner of transition. It also contrasts with verses 28-29 which described wrongdoers' death and Hell entry. The two death scenes are placed close to show two paths: one ends with peace and Paradise, other with denial and Hell. The listener is invited to choose which death he wants by choosing which life he lives. The sūrah's blessings catalogue was meant to cultivate ṭayyib, virtuous state, so that death will be while virtuous.

**The Meaning Of Ṭayyibīn**

Ṭayyibīn can mean pure from shirk, pure in speech and action, or those whose death is easy, without hardship. Al-Ṭabarī reports both interpretations and says they are compatible: those who are pure in faith have easy death. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that ṭayyib is opposite of khabīth, foul, impure, used for shirk and evil deeds. The righteous are ṭayyib because they avoided khabīth of shirk and ingratitude. Their souls are ṭayyibah, good, so angels take them in ṭayyib manner, with peace. The word also suggests that their lives were ṭayyibah, good life, as 16:97 says *We will surely give them good life* (16:97). Good life leads to good death leads to good home.

**Linguistic Note On Salām And Bi-mā**

Salām from s-l-m means peace, safety, wholeness, from same root as islām, submission that brings peace. Salāmun ʿalaykum is greeting that wishes peace upon addressee. Udkhulū from d-kh-l means enter, imperative plural, indicating invitation to group. Bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn, for what you used to do, uses bi- for cause, mā for what, kuntum for you were, taʿmalūn for doing continuously. The phrase indicates habitual deeds, not single act. The verse's structure — those whom angels take while virtuous, saying peace, enter Paradise for what you did — moves from state at death to greeting to command to reason, creating a complete, comforting picture of righteous death that contrasts sharply with harsh death of wrongdoers described earlier.

**How Virtuous State Is Cultivated**

Ṭayyibīn state is not accidental; it is cultivated through gratitude for blessings, reflection on signs, and patience in hardship. The sūrah has listed uncountable blessings to cultivate ṭayyib heart that recognizes Giver. It has warned against arrogance and denial that make heart khabīth. It has described emigrants who patiently endured and trusted Lord, as verse 42 will say. All these cultivate ṭayyib. At death, angels recognize that cultivation and greet with peace. The greeting salāmun ʿalaykum therefore is not only for that moment but for life that led to it. It says, your life was peaceful in submission, so your death is peaceful, and your next life will be peace.

**Enter Paradise As Invitation**

Udkhulū al-jannah, enter Paradise, is imperative of invitation, not command of compulsion. Angels invite righteous to enter what they have been promised, what they worked for, what Allah prepared. The invitation is gentle, with reason bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn, for what you used to do, acknowledging their effort. This contrasts with fa-udkhulū abwāba jahannama, so enter gates of Hell, which is command of humiliation. Both use same verb udkhulū but tone differs: one is honor, other is disgrace. The righteous enter Paradise for what they did, meaning their deeds are recognized and rewarded, not ignored. This recognition is part of reward: to be told your deeds mattered.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:33

> **Are they only awaiting the coming of the angels or the command of your Lord ˹O Prophet˺? So were those before them. And Allah never wronged them, but it was they who wronged themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Are They Waiting For?**

Hal yanẓurūna illā an ta'tiyahum al-malā'ikatu aw ya'tiya amru rabbika — are they only waiting for angels to come or command of your Lord to come? Hal is interrogative, are they, do they? Yanẓurūna from n-ẓ-r means to wait, await, expect. Illā an ta'tiyahum al-malā'ikatu, except that angels come to them, meaning angels of death to take souls, or angels of punishment, or angels for reckoning. Aw ya'tiya amru rabbika, or command of your Lord comes, meaning punishment in world or Hour in Hereafter. The question is rhetorical, criticizing delay in believing until undeniable signs come. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Makkan deniers were waiting for angels to come with punishment or for Allah's command of destruction or Resurrection to come before believing, but by then belief will not benefit. The same question appears in 6:158: *Do they await except that angels should come to them or your Lord should come or some signs of your Lord should come?* (6:158). The answer is that waiting until then is foolish, because then it is too late.

**So Were Those Before Them**

Kadhālika faʿala al-ladhīna min qablihim — so did those before them. Those before them also waited for angels or command before believing, and when it came, they were destroyed. Al-Ṭabarī says previous nations also demanded angels or punishment before believing, and when it came, they perished. The phrase kadhālika, like that, so, indicates that current deniers follow same pattern as previous, and will meet same outcome unless they change. The sūrah has repeatedly used those before them as warning: they plotted, their building collapsed, they were humiliated, they wronged themselves. Now it says they also waited for angels or command. The past is mirror for present. Al-Rāzī notes that this repetition is to make Makkans see that they are not original in denial; they are repeating old story whose ending is known.

**Allah Did Not Wrong Them, They Wronged Themselves**

Wa-mā ẓalamahum Allāhu wa-lākin kānū anfusahum yaẓlimūn — and Allah did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves. Ẓalama means to wrong, to oppress, to put thing in wrong place. Allah did not wrong them by punishing without reason or by not giving signs; He gave blessings, sent messengers, showed straight way, gave time. They wronged themselves by choosing denial, arrogance, calling revelation fables, plotting, opposing believers. Al-Qurṭubī explains that self-wronging includes harming own souls by committing deeds that lead to punishment, and also includes putting worship in wrong place by giving it to idols. The phrase appears many times in Qur'an, as in 16:118 and 29:40, to emphasize divine justice and human responsibility. It answers hidden objection: if Allah could have guided all, as verse 9 said, why does He punish those not guided? Answer: He did not wrong them; they wronged themselves by refusing guidance shown.

**The Connection To Angels Taking Souls**

Verse 33 mentions angels coming, which connects to verses 28 and 32 where angels take souls of wrongdoers and righteous. Those who wait for angels to come will indeed see angels, but angels will come to take their souls while wronging themselves, as verse 28 described, not as they hope. The waiting is therefore futile: angels will come, but not as bringers of faith that benefits; they will come as takers of souls. Similarly, command of your Lord will come, as verse 1 said command is at hand, but when it comes, it will be too late to believe. The verse therefore urges not to wait: believe now, while angels have not yet come to take soul, while command has not yet arrived as punishment.

**Why This Warning Comes After Paradise Description**

After describing excellent home for righteous and peaceful death, the sūrah returns to warning for those who still wait. The contrast is intentional: righteous have Gardens, wrongdoers await angels or command. Which waiting is better? Righteous awaited reward and received peace; wrongdoers await punishment and will receive humiliation. The verse also prepares for next verse: evil consequences overtook them and they were overwhelmed by what they ridiculed. Those who wait for punishment will be overtaken by it. The warning is both logical and compassionate: do not wait until it is too late.

**Linguistic Note On Naẓara And Amr**

Yanẓurūna from n-ẓ-r means to look, wait, expect, from same root as naẓar, sight, consideration. Hal yanẓurūna illā, are they waiting except, is common Qur'anic construction for criticizing delay. Ta'tiyahum from a-t-y means to come to them. Al-malā'ikatu, angels, plural. Amru rabbika, command of your Lord, same amr as in verse 1, command at hand. Kadhālika faʿala al-ladhīna min qablihim, so did those before them, with faʿala, did. Wa-mā ẓalamahum Allāhu, and Allah did not wrong them, with mā for negation. Anfusahum yaẓlimūn, themselves they wrong, with yaẓlimūn present tense indicating ongoing self-wronging. The verse's structure — question, past precedent, justice statement — moves from present waiting to past example to principle of justice, guiding listener to see that waiting is old pattern and that punishment is self-inflicted, not divine wrongdoing.

**The Danger Of Waiting For Undeniable Signs**

Waiting for angels or command before believing is dangerous because by then choice is removed. The Qur'an says *On the Day when some signs of your Lord come, no soul will benefit from its faith if it had not believed before* (6:158). Faith that comes after seeing angels of death or punishment is forced, not chosen, and therefore does not benefit. The sūrah has given many signs that are not overwhelming but invite reflection: cattle, rain, crops, night, day, sea, mountains. These signs allow choice; they can be reflected upon or ignored. Angels and command are signs that do not allow choice; they overwhelm. Allah in mercy sends first kind so that people may choose; if they refuse, second kind will come that does not allow choice. The verse therefore urges to believe in first signs before second comes.

**Self-Wronging As Explanation Of Justice**

The statement Allah did not wrong them but they wronged themselves is central to Qur'anic justice. It appears in many places to refute idea that punishment is arbitrary. Al-Rāzī explains that wronging self includes both committing sin and failing to do what would save self, like failing to reflect on blessings, failing to ask knowledgeable, failing to emigrate when able. In al-Naḥl, self-wronging includes calling revelation fables, plotting, opposing believers, and misusing divine will as excuse. All are choices that harm self. The phrase also comforts oppressed: those who were wronged by plotters were not wronged by Allah; their suffering is part of test that will be rewarded, as verses 41-42 will show for emigrants. Divine justice is preserved even when worldly injustice occurs.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:34

> **Then the evil ˹consequences˺ of their deeds overtook them, and they were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Evil Consequences Overtaking**

Fa-aṣābahum sayyi'ātu mā ʿamilū — then evil consequences of what they did overtook them, befell them. Aṣābahum from ṣ-w-b means to hit, befall, overtake. Sayyi'āt means evil deeds and their evil consequences, plural of sayyi'ah, evil, bad. Mā ʿamilū, what they did, what they used to do. Al-Ṭabarī explains that sayyi'āt here means punishment for evil deeds, evil outcome that results from deeds. Their deeds were shirk, denial, calling revelation fables, plotting, opposing believers. The evil consequences are punishment in world and Hereafter that results. Fa- indicates consequence: because they wronged themselves and waited for angels or command, then evil consequences overtook them. The verb aṣābahum suggests that consequences are not random but targeted, hitting them as arrow hits target. Their deeds created consequences that then caught up with them, as 30:36 says *If evil befalls them for what their hands sent forth, they despair* (30:36).

**Overwhelmed By What They Ridiculed**

Wa-ḥāqa bihim mā kānū bihi yastahzi'ūn — and they were overwhelmed, surrounded, encompassed by what they used to ridicule. Ḥāqa from ḥ-w-q means to surround, encompass, befall from all sides, often used for punishment that surrounds. Bihi yastahzi'ūn, what they used to ridicule, mock, make fun of. Al-Ṭabarī says they ridiculed punishment, Hour, resurrection, saying when will it come, bring it if truthful. Then punishment they ridiculed surrounded them, as flood surrounded mockers of Nūḥ, wind surrounded mockers of Hūd, etc. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that yastahzi'ūn is present tense indicating habitual ridicule: they used to ridicule continuously. What they ridiculed becomes what overwhelms them, a fitting recompense: you mocked punishment, now punishment mocks you by surrounding you from where you did not expect, as verse 26 said torment came from where they did not perceive.

**The Pattern Of Ridicule And Overwhelming**

The Qur'an often pairs ridicule of punishment with being overwhelmed by it, as in 6:10: *And indeed messengers before you were ridiculed, but those who mocked them were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule* (6:10) and 21:41 and 46:26. The pattern is consistent: deniers ridicule warning, warning becomes reality that surrounds them. In al-Naḥl, ridicule took form of calling revelation ancient fables (16:24) and demanding hastening of command (16:1). The verse says that very thing ridiculed — command, punishment, Hour — will surround them. The ridicule does not prevent reality; it ensures that when reality comes, it comes as overwhelming, not as gentle reminder. Al-Rāzī explains that ḥāqa bihim indicates that punishment they thought would never come actually encompasses them from all sides, leaving no escape, as 29:54 says *Indeed Hell will encompass disbelievers* (29:54).

**Why This Verse Follows Self-Wronging**

Verse 33 said Allah did not wrong them but they wronged themselves. Verse 34 says evil consequences of what they did overtook them and they were overwhelmed by what they ridiculed. The connection is that self-wronging leads to being overtaken by evil consequences. They wronged themselves by deeds, and deeds' consequences caught up. This is divine justice: you are not wronged by Allah; you are overtaken by what your own hands did and by what you mocked. The verse also answers those who think sins have no consequences because punishment is delayed. It says consequences will overtake, even if delayed, and when they do, they will overwhelm.

**A Lesson On Speech And Consequence**

The verse highlights that ridicule, yastahzi'ūn, is not harmless speech. It is deed with consequence. To ridicule revelation, messengers, or punishment is to put oneself in position where what is ridiculed will overwhelm. The Qur'an warns against mocking: *O you who believe, let not a people ridicule another people, perhaps they may be better* (49:11). If mocking people is prohibited, mocking Allah's signs is worse. The verse teaches that speech should be guarded, because what is said in mockery may become reality that surrounds speaker. The Makkans who said *bring punishment if truthful* were in fact calling punishment upon themselves, as 8:32 shows.

**Linguistic Note On Sayyi'āt And Ḥāqa**

Sayyi'āt from s-w-ʾ means evil, bad, from same root as sū', evil. In this context, sayyi'ātu mā ʿamilū means evil consequences of what they did, not just evil deeds themselves, though both meanings overlap. Aṣābahum from ṣ-w-b means to hit, befall, as arrow hits target. Ḥāqa from ḥ-w-q means to surround, encompass, befall, often with sense of inevitable encirclement. Bihi yastahzi'ūn, what they used to ridicule, from h-z-ʾ, to mock, ridicule, with present tense indicating habitual mockery. The verse's two parts — evil consequences overtook them, and they were overwhelmed by what they ridiculed — are parallel, both indicating that punishment is result of own deeds and own mockery, not arbitrary. The brevity — two clauses — delivers final outcome of waiting and self-wronging: overtaken and overwhelmed.

**How Consequences Catch Up**

Consequences catching up is law of moral world as gravity is law of physical world. A person may do evil and feel nothing immediate, thinking he escaped. But deeds create consequences that travel, like arrow shot, and eventually hit. The Qur'an says *And whatever affliction befalls you is for what your hands earned* (42:30). In al-Naḥl, evil deeds included shirk, denial, calling revelation fables, plotting, opposing believers, misusing divine will as excuse. Each has consequence: shirk leads to Hell, denial leads to humiliation, calling fables leads to bearing burdens of misled, plotting leads to building collapse, opposing believers leads to disgrace. The verse says these consequences overtook them, meaning they could not outrun them. The Makkans who plotted in Dār al-Nadwah thought they were securing future; their plotting's evil consequences overtook them at Badr and beyond.

**Ridicule As Self-Entrapment**

Ridicule entraps because what is ridiculed is often true, and by ridiculing, person closes mind to truth that could save him. When punishment that was ridiculed arrives, it overwhelms because person has no preparation, no repentance, no defense. Al-Rāzī notes that ḥāqa bihim indicates encirclement from all sides, leaving no escape, because ridicule was from all sides — they ridiculed revelation, messengers, believers, and punishment. So punishment surrounds from all sides as recompense. The verse teaches to take warnings seriously, not to ridicule, because ridicule does not make warning disappear; it makes warning surround when it comes. The wise person listens to warning even if he doubts, and investigates, rather than mocking.

**A Verse That Closes The Warning Section**

Verse 34 closes the section that began with plotting and waiting. It started with those before plotted and building collapsed, then Day humiliation, then death with false submission, Hell as evil home, contrast with righteous, then question what are they waiting for, self-wronging, now evil consequences overtook and they were overwhelmed by what they ridiculed. The section shows progression from plotting to collapse to death to Hell to waiting to overtaking. It is complete warning. Next verse will shift to polytheists' excuse using divine will, showing another form of denial. The warning section therefore ends with being overwhelmed by what was ridiculed, a fitting end that makes listener feel that ridicule is not safe.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:35

> **The polytheists argue, “Had Allah willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have worshipped anything other than Him, nor prohibited anything without His command.” So did those before them. Is not the messengers’ duty only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Using Divine Will As Excuse For Shirk**

Wa-qāla al-ladhīna ashrakū law shā'a Allāhu mā ʿabadnā min dūnihi min shay'in naḥnu wa-lā ābā'unā wa-lā ḥarramnā min dūnihi min shay' — and those who associate partners said, had Allah willed, we would not have worshipped anything besides Him, neither we nor our fathers, nor would we have prohibited anything besides Him. This is argument of polytheists who claim that since Allah allowed them to worship idols and prohibit certain animals as ḥarām, therefore Allah wills and approves their shirk and prohibitions. Al-Ṭabarī explains that they argued: if Allah did not like our worship of idols and our prohibiting cattle, He would have prevented us, so since He did not prevent, He approves. This is misuse of divine will to justify disobedience. Al-Rāzī notes that they confused divine will that allows human choice with divine approval. Allah wills to allow humans to choose, but does not approve shirk. The same argument appears in 6:148: *Those who associate will say, had Allah willed, we would not have associated nor would our fathers, nor would we have prohibited anything* (6:148). The Qur'an answers that this argument was used by those before them and is invalid.

**Our Forefathers And Prohibitions**

Naḥnu wa-lā ābā'unā, we nor our forefathers, indicates that they appealed to tradition: our fathers did shirk, so it must be acceptable, and if Allah willed otherwise, He would have stopped our fathers. Wa-lā ḥarramnā min dūnihi min shay', nor would we have prohibited anything besides Him, refers to their practice of prohibiting certain cattle as ḥarām for idols, as 6:139-144 describes: they prohibited certain camels, cattle, saying this is for idols. They claimed that since Allah allowed them to prohibit, He approves prohibition. Al-Qurṭubī explains that they made unlawful what Allah made lawful, like certain cattle, and claimed it was by Allah's command, when in fact it was by their own invention, as 16:116 will say *Do not say what your tongues falsely describe, this is lawful and this is forbidden, inventing lie against Allah* (16:116). Their argument uses divine will to justify both shirk in worship and shirk in legislation.

**So Did Those Before Them**

Kadhālika faʿala al-ladhīna min qablihim — so did those before them. Those before them also argued that had Allah willed, they would not have worshipped others, using same excuse. Al-Ṭabarī says previous nations also used divine will as excuse for persistence in shirk. The phrase indicates that this argument is not new; it is old pattern of those who refuse responsibility. Al-Rāzī notes that kadhālika faʿala indicates that their action was same as previous: they associated, prohibited, and used will as excuse. The past precedent shows that argument leads to destruction, as previous nations were destroyed despite using it. Therefore current polytheists should not rely on it.

**Is Messengers' Duty Only To Deliver Clearly?**

Fa-hal ʿalā al-rusuli illā al-balāghu al-mubīn — is not messengers' duty except clear delivery? Balāgh means delivery, conveyance, communication. Mubīn means clear, manifest, clarifying. The verse says messengers' duty is only to deliver message clearly, not to compel guidance or prevent shirk by force. Al-Ṭabarī explains that messengers are not responsible for guiding all; they are responsible for conveying clearly, and they have done so. The excuse *had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others* is answered by saying messengers have delivered clear message that Allah does not approve shirk, so you cannot claim you did not know. The duty of clear delivery has been fulfilled, so responsibility now lies with listeners. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that al-balāghu al-mubīn includes both clarity of content and clarity of evidence, so that no one can say message was unclear.

**Why This Argument Is Invalid**

The argument *had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others* is invalid for several reasons. First, divine will that allows choice does not equal divine approval. Allah allows humans to choose disbelief, but does not approve it, as 39:7 says *If you disbelieve, indeed Allah is free from need of you, and He does not approve disbelief for His servants* (39:7). Second, if argument were valid, it would justify all sins: had Allah willed, we would not have stolen, killed, etc., which would eliminate moral responsibility. Third, Allah has willed to show straight way and to send messengers to clarify, so excuse that He did not will guidance is false: He did will to show way, as verse 9 said *upon Allah is to show straight way*. Fourth, they themselves do not consistently apply argument: they do not excuse theft against themselves by saying had Allah willed thief would not have stolen; they demand justice. So they apply determinism selectively to excuse own shirk.

**The Connection To Guidance And Will**

Verse 9 said upon Allah to show straight way, and had He willed He would have guided all. Verse 35 shows polytheists misusing that idea: if He could guide all, why did He not guide us, so our shirk must be His will. The answer is that He willed to allow choice and to send messengers with clear delivery. Guidance has been shown, messengers have delivered, so responsibility remains. The verse thus clarifies that divine will to allow choice does not remove human accountability. The messengers' duty is clear delivery, which has been done, so excuse fails.

**Linguistic Note On Law Shā'a And Balāgh**

Law shā'a, had He willed, from sh-y-ʾ, to will, wish. Mā ʿabadnā min dūnihi min shay', we would not have worshipped anything besides Him, with min shay' indicating anything at all. Ḥarramnā from ḥ-r-m, to prohibit, make unlawful. Kadhālika faʿala al-ladhīna min qablihim, so did those before them, same as verse 33. Fa-hal ʿalā al-rusuli illā al-balāghu al-mubīn, is not upon messengers except clear delivery, with hal for interrogative expecting negative: is there anything upon messengers except clear delivery? No, only clear delivery. Rusul plural of rasūl, messenger. Balāgh from b-l-gh, to reach, deliver. Mubīn from b-y-n, to be clear, manifest. The verse's structure — polytheists' argument, past precedent, messengers' duty — moves from false excuse to historical pattern to clarification of responsibility, guiding listener to reject excuse and accept clear message.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:36

> **We surely sent a messenger to every community, saying, “Worship Allah and shun false gods.” But some of them were guided by Allah, while others were destined to stray. So travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Messenger To Every Community**

Wa-laqad baʿathnā fī kulli ummatin rasūlan — and We surely sent in every community a messenger. Laqad emphasizes certainty, indeed, surely. Baʿathnā from b-ʿ-th means to send, raise, resurrect, used for sending messengers and for resurrection. Fī kulli ummatin, in every community, nation, people, indicates universality of messengership. No community left without warner, as 35:24 says *There is no community but that a warner has passed among them* (35:24). Al-Ṭabarī explains that ummah here means people, community, generation that shares time and place. Every ummah had messenger to establish proof. The message was same across all: ani iʿbudū Allāha wa-ijtanibū al-ṭāghūt — worship Allah and shun false gods, avoid ṭāghūt. Ṭāghūt from ṭ-gh-y means to exceed bounds, to transgress, and refers to anything worshipped besides Allah that exceeds limits, including idols, tyrants, devils. The core call is two parts: affirmation, worship Allah, and negation, shun ṭāghūt, exactly tawḥīd.

**Worship Allah And Shun False Gods**

Ani iʿbudū Allāha wa-ijtanibū al-ṭāghūt — that worship Allah and avoid false gods. Iʿbudū from ʿ-b-d means to worship, serve, be servant. Ijtanibū from j-n-b means to avoid, shun, keep away from side, indicating active avoidance, not passive. Al-Ṭabarī says ṭāghūt includes idols, devils, and any object of worship besides Allah. Al-Rāzī explains that worshipping Allah and shunning ṭāghūt are two sides of same coin: you cannot worship Allah truly while also worshipping ṭāghūt; you must do both. The phrase ani iʿbudū Allāha wa-ijtanibū al-ṭāghūt is summary of all messengers' message, from Nūḥ to Muḥammad ﷺ, as 21:25 says *We did not send any messenger before you except We revealed to him there is no god but Me, so worship Me* (21:25). The universality shows that tawḥīd is not new or particular to Arabs; it is original message to all peoples, and shirk is deviation.

**Some Guided, Some Destined To Stray**

Fa-minhum man hadā Allāhu wa-minhum man ḥaqqat ʿalayhi al-ḍalālah — but among them are those whom Allah guided and among them are those upon whom straying was justified, destined. Fa-minhum, among them, from communities. Man hadā Allāhu, whom Allah guided, meaning those who accepted message, reflected on blessings, humbled themselves. Man ḥaqqat ʿalayhi al-ḍalālah, upon whom straying was justified, meaning those who refused, were arrogant, called revelation fables, so misguidance became deserved. Al-Ṭabarī explains ḥaqqat ʿalayhi al-ḍalālah as misguidance became due, established, because they chose denial. Al-Rāzī notes that this does not mean Allah forced some to stray arbitrarily; it means guidance was shown to all via messengers, but some accepted and were guided further, while others refused and straying became deserved. The same idea appears in 2:26: *He misguides many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misguides not except the defiantly disobedient* (2:26). The verse preserves both divine will and human choice.

**Travel And See Fate Of Deniers**

Fa-sīrū fī al-arḍi fa-unẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-mukadhdhibīn — so travel through land and see how was end of deniers. Sīrū from s-y-r means to travel, journey. Fī al-arḍi, through land, earth. Unẓurū from n-ẓ-r means to look, see, consider. Kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-mukadhdhibīn, how was end, outcome, of those who denied. Al-Ṭabarī says this is command to consider ruins of destroyed nations as evidence: travel and see traces of ʿĀd, Thamūd, Pharaoh, etc., and see that their end was destruction because they denied messengers. Al-Qurṭubī notes that travel here can be physical travel to see ruins, or travel of reflection through history via stories. The purpose is to learn: denial leads to bad end, as verses 26-34 showed. The command to travel appears many times in Qur'an, as in 12:109 and 30:42, to make history a teacher.

**Why This Verse Comes After Excuse Using Divine Will**

Verse 35 said polytheists argued had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others, and that messengers' duty is only clear delivery. Verse 36 says We sent messenger to every community with same message, some guided, some strayed, so travel and see fate of deniers. The connection is that excuse using divine will fails because messengers were sent to every community to clarify, so people cannot claim they did not know. The fact that some were guided shows guidance is possible; the fact that some strayed shows straying is due to choice, not lack of messenger. Travel and see fate of deniers shows consequence of choosing straying. The verse therefore completes answer to deterministic excuse: messengers sent universally, guidance shown, outcomes differ based on response, and history shows end of deniers.

**Linguistic Note On Ummah And Ṭāghūt**

Ummah from a-m-m means community, group, from same root as imām, leader, and umm, mother, indicating group with common purpose. Rasūl from r-s-l means messenger, one sent. Iʿbudū from ʿ-b-d means worship, serve. Ijtanibū from j-n-b means avoid, shun, keep at side. Ṭāghūt from ṭ-gh-y means to transgress, exceed, and refers to anything worshipped beyond bounds, including idols, tyrants, devils. Hadā from h-d-y means to guide. Ḥaqqat from ḥ-q-q means to be true, justified, due. Ḍalālah from ḍ-l-l means straying, misguidance. Sīrū from s-y-r means travel.
