Unẓurū from n-ẓ-r means look, see. ʿĀqibah from ʿ-q-b means end, outcome, consequence. Mukadhdhibīn from k-dh-b means deniers, those who called truth lie. The verse's structure — universal sending, universal message, two outcomes, command to travel and see — moves from divine action to human response to evidence in history, guiding listener to choose guidance and avoid fate of deniers.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude. Whether it describes Gardens, death, waiting, consequences, or divine command, the purpose is to make the reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah therefore weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate a heart that is mindful, humble, and grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:37

> **Even though you ˹O Prophet˺ are keen on their guidance, Allah certainly does not guide those He leaves to stray, and they will have no helpers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Keenness For Guidance**

In taḥriṣ ʿalā hudāhum — even though you are keen, eager, desirous for their guidance. Taḥriṣ from ḥ-r-ṣ means to be keen, eager, to desire strongly, to strive. Hudāhum, their guidance. The verse addresses Prophet ﷺ who was deeply keen for his people to be guided, grieved when they refused, as 18:6 says *Perhaps you would kill yourself with grief over them if they do not believe* (18:6) and 26:3 says *Perhaps you will kill yourself that they are not believers* (26:3). Al-Ṭabarī explains that Prophet ﷺ was eager for Quraysh to accept tawḥīd, to be saved from Hell, to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed. His keenness was from mercy, as he is described as *merciful to believers* (9:128). The verse acknowledges that keenness while clarifying limits: even with your keenness, guidance is by Allah's will and human choice.

**Allah Does Not Guide Those He Leaves To Stray**

Fa-inna Allāha lā yahdī man yuḍillu — but indeed Allah does not guide those He leaves to stray, whom He lets go astray. Man yuḍillu means whom He leaves to stray, whom He allows to stray as consequence of their choice. Al-Ṭabarī explains that yuḍillu here means those whom Allah has judged to be astray because they chose denial, arrogance, calling revelation fables, using divine will as excuse. Their straying became deserved, as verse 36 said *upon whom straying was justified*. Allah does not guide them further because they refused initial guidance shown via blessings, messengers, and clear delivery. Al-Rāzī notes that lā yahdī here does not mean Allah prevents them from guidance arbitrarily, but that He does not guide them to success because they chose straying. The same idea appears in 16:107: *That is because they preferred worldly life over Hereafter and Allah does not guide disbelieving people* (16:107). Guidance requires willingness.

**They Will Have No Helpers**

Wa-mā lahum min nāṣirīn — and they will have no helpers, supporters, protectors. Nāṣirīn plural of nāṣir, helper, from n-ṣ-r, to help, support. Al-Ṭabarī says they will have no helpers to protect them from Allah's punishment, to intercede, to prevent humiliation described in verse 27, to prevent entry to Hell described in verse 29. Their idols, whom they worshipped for help, will not help, as verse 27's question *Where are My partners?* shows. Their forefathers, whom they invoked as tradition, will not help. Their plotting, building, wealth will not help. The phrase mā lahum min nāṣirīn appears many times for deniers, as in 2:270 and 3:192, to emphasize that on Day, help comes only from Allah, and those who associated others will find those others absent.

**Why This Verse Follows Universal Messengership**

Verse 36 said We sent messenger to every community, some guided, some straying justified, travel and see fate of deniers. Verse 37 addresses Prophet's keenness for guidance of his own community, Quraysh, who are among those who may be guided or stray. It says even though you are keen, Allah does not guide those He leaves to stray, and they will have no helpers. The connection is that universal sending does not mean universal acceptance; some will stray despite messenger's keenness. The Prophet's duty is clear delivery, as verse 35 said, not compelling guidance. His keenness is praiseworthy but does not override divine wisdom and human choice. The verse consoles Prophet: you have done your duty, you are keen from mercy, but guidance is ultimately by Allah and by people's willingness.

**The Balance Between Keenness And Reliance**

The verse teaches balance for anyone calling to truth: be keen, eager, strive for others' guidance, but rely on Allah and do not despair if some refuse. Al-Qurṭubī notes that taḥriṣ ʿalā hudāhum is natural for compassionate caller, but fa-inna Allāha lā yahdī man yuḍillu reminds that result is not in caller's hand. The caller delivers clearly, is keen, prays, but guidance is by Allah. This prevents both negligence, saying guidance is only by Allah so I need not be keen, and despair, saying I am keen but they refuse so I failed. The correct attitude is keenness plus reliance plus acceptance that some will stray and have no helpers, while continuing to call with wisdom and good exhortation as verse 125 will say.

**Linguistic Note On Ḥirṣ And Naṣīr**

Taḥriṣ from ḥ-r-ṣ means to be keen, eager, greedy, desirous, often used for strong desire to obtain something. In this context, it is praiseworthy keenness for others' good. Hudā from h-d-y means guidance. Fa-inna Allāha lā yahdī man yuḍillu, but Allah does not guide whom He leaves to stray, uses yuḍillu from ḍ-l-l, to stray, misguide. The construction man yuḍillu could be read as whom He misguides, but tafsīr explains it as whom He judges to be astray due to their choice. Mā lahum min nāṣirīn, they will have no helpers, uses mā for negation, lahum for them, min for any, nāṣirīn for helpers. The verse's structure — concession, even though you are keen, then principle, Allah does not guide those left to stray, then consequence, no helpers — moves from Prophet's attitude to divine principle to outcome for deniers, guiding listener to understand limits of human keenness and necessity of divine guidance and personal choice.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude. Whether it describes Gardens, death, waiting, consequences, or divine command, the purpose is to make the reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah therefore weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate a heart that is mindful, humble, and grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:38

> **They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that Allah will never raise the dead to life. Yes ˹He will˺! It is a true promise binding on Him, but most people do not know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Solemn Oaths Denying Resurrection**

Wa-aqsamū bi-Allāhi jahda aymānihim lā yabʿathu Allāhu man yamūt — and they swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that Allah will never raise the dead. Aqsamū from q-s-m means to swear, oath. Bi-Allāhi, by Allah, indicating they swear by Allah, whom they acknowledge as Creator. Jahda aymānihim, most earnest, strongest, most solemn oaths, from j-h-d, to strive, exert utmost, indicating they exert all effort in oath to emphasize. Lā yabʿathu Allāhu man yamūt, Allah will not raise dead, will not resurrect those who die. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Makkan polytheists swore by Allah with strongest oaths that there is no resurrection, that dead will not be raised, that Hour will not come. This is ironic: they swear by Allah that Allah will not do something, using Allah's name to deny Allah's power. The same oath appears in 35:42 and 6:109, where deniers swear they will believe if sign comes, but when sign comes they do not. Here they swear opposite: they swear resurrection will not happen. Their oath shows certainty in denial, not doubt.

**Yes, He Will — A Divine Rebuttal**

Balā waʿdan ʿalayhi ḥaqqan — Yes, indeed, it is a true promise binding upon Him. Balā is strong affirmation negating previous negative: yes indeed, contrary to what they swear, He will raise dead. Waʿdan, promise. ʿAlayhi ḥaqqan, binding upon Him, true, incumbent, right, from ḥ-q-q, to be true, binding. Al-Ṭabarī explains that balā affirms resurrection: yes, Allah will raise dead. Waʿdan ʿalayhi ḥaqqan means it is promise that Allah has made binding upon Himself, true promise that will be fulfilled, as 10:55 says *Indeed Allah's promise is true* (10:55). The promise of resurrection is ḥaqq, true, not false, and it is ʿalayhi, upon Him, meaning He has taken it upon Himself to fulfill, as He took upon Himself to show straight way in verse 9. The rebuttal is short but decisive: they swear no resurrection, Allah says yes, it is true promise binding on Him.

**Most People Do Not Know**

Wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yaʿlamūn — but most people do not know. Akthara al-nāsi, most people, majority, do not know that resurrection is true promise, do not know its wisdom, do not know its necessity. Al-Rāzī explains that most people do not know because they do not reflect on blessings, do not consider that creation with purpose implies return, do not know that Allah's power to create from drop implies power to recreate. Their lack of knowledge is not due to lack of evidence but lack of reflection, as earlier seals said for those who reflect, understand, are mindful. Most people do not know because they are heedless, not because evidence absent. The phrase appears many times in Qur'an to indicate that truth is often not known by majority, but majority's ignorance does not make falsehood true.

**Why Resurrection Is Central To This Sūrah**

Sūrat al-Naḥl opened with command at hand, then listed blessings that prove power to resurrect, then argued idols are dead and do not know when they will be raised, then described two death scenes, then asked are they waiting for angels or command. Now it addresses explicit denial of resurrection via solemn oaths. Resurrection is central because without it, blessings catalogue is merely worldly enjoyment, and warnings are empty. With resurrection, blessings become trust to be accounted for, and warnings become real. Al-Qurṭubī notes that denial of resurrection was main doctrine of Makkan polytheists, who said *When we are bones and crumbled dust, shall we be raised as new creation?* (17:49). The sūrah answers with creation arguments: He created heavens and earth, He created you from drop, He creates what you do not know, He says Be and it is. All prove power to resurrect.

**The Irony Of Swearing By Allah To Deny Allah's Power**

They swear by Allah that Allah will not raise dead. This is ironic because swearing by Allah acknowledges Allah's greatness and power, yet they deny power to resurrect. If Allah is great enough to be sworn by, He is great enough to resurrect. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that jahda aymānihim, utmost effort in oaths, shows they are earnest in denial, not casual. Their earnestness makes their error more serious. The Qur'an often notes that deniers swear by Allah, as in 6:109 and 16:38, to show that they do recognize Allah as deity to swear by, but they misplace worship by associating others. Their oath is therefore self-contradictory: they invoke Allah's name to deny Allah's promise.

**Linguistic Note On Qasam And Baʿth**

Aqsamū from q-s-m means to swear, oath, from same root as qasam, oath, and qismah, division. Bi-Allāhi, by Allah, with bi- for oath. Jahda from j-h-d means utmost effort, striving, from same root as jihād. Aymānihim from y-m-n, right hand, oath, because oath often taken with right hand. Lā yabʿathu, will not raise, from b-ʿ-th, to raise, send, resurrect, same root as baʿth, resurrection. Man yamūt, who dies, from m-w-t, death. Balā particle for affirming after negative, yes indeed. Waʿdan from w-ʿ-d, promise. ʿAlayhi ḥaqqan, binding upon Him, true, from ḥ-q-q. Akthara al-nāsi lā yaʿlamūn, most people do not know, with akthar, most, from k-th-r, many. The verse's structure — oath of denial, divine rebuttal yes promise true, most people do not know — moves from human claim to divine counter-claim to explanation of why many deny, guiding listener to reject oath of deniers and accept true promise.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude. Whether it describes Gardens, death, waiting, consequences, or divine command, the purpose is to make the reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah therefore weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate a heart that is mindful, humble, and grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:39

> **˹He will do that˺ to make clear to them what they disagreed on, and for the disbelievers to know that they were liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Purpose Of Resurrection Is Clarification**

Li-yubayyina lahum al-ladhī yakhtalifūna fīhi — to make clear to them what they disagreed on, what they differed about. Li- indicates purpose: He will raise dead for this purpose, to clarify. Yubayyina from b-y-n means to make clear, manifest, explain, from same root as bayyinah, clear proof, and mubīn, clear. Al-ladhī yakhtalifūna fīhi, what they differed on, disagreed about, includes tawḥīd vs shirk, resurrection vs denial, lawful vs prohibited, truth of revelation vs fables. Al-Ṭabarī explains that resurrection will make clear truth of matters they disputed in world: whether Allah is One, whether resurrection is true, whether revelation is from Allah. In world, disputes remain unresolved because deniers refuse evidence; in Hereafter, truth becomes manifest and dispute ends. The same purpose appears in 16:64: *We sent down Book to you only to make clear to them what they differed about* (16:64), showing that both revelation in world and resurrection in Hereafter serve clarification.

**For Disbelievers To Know They Were Liars**

Wa-li-yaʿlama al-ladhīna kafarū annahum kānū kādhibīn — and for disbelievers to know that they were liars. Li-yaʿlama, so that they may know, from ʿ-l-m, to know. Al-ladhīna kafarū, those who disbelieved, covered truth. Annahum kānū kādhibīn, that they were liars, those who lied. Kādhbīn from k-dh-b means liars, those who called truth lie and told falsehood. Al-Ṭabarī explains that disbelievers will know on Day that they were liars in world when they said Allah has partners, resurrection will not happen, revelation is fables, had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others. Their lies will be exposed. Al-Rāzī notes that li-yaʿlama indicates that knowledge in Hereafter will be certain, not speculative, because they will see punishment and truth with own eyes, as 32:12 says *If you could see when criminals bow heads before Lord, Our Lord, we have seen and heard, so send us back* (32:12). Knowledge that they were liars will be part of humiliation described in verse 27.

**Why Clarification Requires Resurrection**

In world, disputes about unseen matters cannot be fully resolved because unseen is not seen. People differ about whether Allah is One, whether Hour will come, whether revelation is true, and each claims to be right. Resurrection resolves by making unseen seen: Allah's oneness becomes manifest, Hour arrives, revelation's truth becomes clear. Al-Qurṭubī notes that li-yubayyina lahum indicates that resurrection is necessary for justice: without it, disputes would remain unresolved, wrongdoers would not be exposed, and truth would not be made clear to all. The sūrah's blessings catalogue also needs resurrection for full clarification: blessings were given, but were they from Allah alone or from idols? Resurrection will make clear that they were from Allah alone, and those who attributed them to idols were liars.

**The Connection To Oaths Denying Resurrection**

Verse 38 said they swore solemn oaths that Allah will not raise dead, but it is true promise. Verse 39 says He will raise them to make clear what they disagreed on and for disbelievers to know they were liars. The connection is that their oath that there is no resurrection was lie, and resurrection will expose it as lie. They swore by Allah that Allah will not raise dead; resurrection will show they swore falsely. Their lie about resurrection is central lie that includes other lies: if no resurrection, then no accountability, then shirk and prohibitions they invented have no consequence. Resurrection exposes all as lies. The verse therefore answers oath with purpose: you swore no resurrection, but resurrection will happen to show you were liars.

**Liars In What Sense**

Kādhibīn, liars, can mean they lied when they said Allah has partners, when they said revelation is fables, when they said had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others, when they said Allah will not raise dead. All are lies. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that kādhibīn here means they were liars in their claims about Allah, not merely mistaken, because evidence was available via blessings, messengers, and clear delivery, so their denial was not honest error but willful lying. The Qur'an distinguishes between those who err out of ignorance and those who lie knowingly; disbelievers in this context are described as liars because they had access to truth but rejected and misrepresented.

**Linguistic Note On Bayyana And Kādhibīn**

Li-yubayyina from b-y-n means to make clear, clarify, from same root as bayān, clarification, and mubīn, clear. Lahum, to them, for them. Al-ladhī yakhtalifūna fīhi, what they differ in, from kh-l-f, to differ, disagree, be behind. Wa-li-yaʿlama, and for them to know, from ʿ-l-m. Al-ladhīna kafarū, those who disbelieved, from k-f-r, to cover. Annahum kānū kādhibīn, that they were liars, from k-dh-b, to lie. The verse's two purposes — to make clear what they differed on, and for disbelievers to know they were liars — are complementary: clarification for all, and specific realization for disbelievers that they were lying. The verse ends without mentioning believers, but implication is that believers will also know they were truthful, as contrast. The structure — purpose of resurrection is clarification and exposure of lies — shows that resurrection is not arbitrary but necessary for truth to be made manifest and for justice to be established.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude. Whether it describes Gardens, death, waiting, consequences, or divine command, the purpose is to make the reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah therefore weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate a heart that is mindful, humble, and grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:40

> **If We ever will something ˹to exist˺, all We say is: “Be!” And it is!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Be And It Is**

Innamā qawlunā li-shay'in idhā aradnāhu an naqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn — our word for a thing when We will it is only to say to it Be and it is. Qawlunā, Our word, speech. Li-shay'in, for a thing, for something. Idhā aradnāhu, when We will it, wish it, intend it. An naqūla lahu kun, that We say to it Be. Fa-yakūn, and it is, becomes. Al-Ṭabarī explains that when Allah wills something to exist, whether resurrection, creation, provision, punishment, He only says to it kun, Be, and it is, without effort, without delay, without intermediary. The phrase innamā, only, emphasizes ease: Our word is only Be. This is same amr, command, that opened sūrah: command at hand, angels descend with spirit from command, stars subjected by command. Now command is explained: command is kun, Be. Al-Rāzī notes that kun fa-yakūn shows that Allah's will is executed by His word alone, without need for tools, time, or assistance, unlike human creation which requires effort and time.

**Why This Verse Comes After Resurrection**

Verses 38-39 said they swear Allah will not raise dead, but it is true promise to make clear what they differed and for disbelievers to know they were liars. Verse 40 says if We will something, We say Be and it is. The connection is that resurrection, which they think impossible, is easy for Allah: He only says Be and it is. If He could create heavens and earth, create humans from drop, create cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, all with kun, then resurrecting dead is even easier, as 30:27 says *It is He who originates creation then repeats it, and that is easier for Him* (30:27). The verse therefore answers denial of resurrection with ease of divine command. They think resurrection is difficult because they compare to human ability; Allah says for Us it is only Be and it is.

**The Power Of Kun**

Kun, Be, is imperative of kāna, to be, exist. It is shortest command in Arabic, two letters, kāf and nūn, yet it brings existence from non-existence. Al-Qurṭubī explains that kun is not that Allah speaks with sound like human, but that His will is executed immediately, without delay, as if He said Be. The phrase fa-yakūn, and it is, indicates immediate existence after command, without interval. Some commentators discuss whether kun is literal speech or metaphor for immediate execution; majority say it is literal speech that Allah speaks, but its nature is beyond human comprehension, as 42:51 says *It is not for any human that Allah should speak to him except by revelation or from behind veil or by sending messenger* (42:51). The point is not modality but ease and power: Allah's command does not need preparation.

**If We Ever Will Something**

Innamā qawlunā li-shay'in idhā aradnāhu — Our word for a thing when We will it. Shay'in, thing, is general, includes anything: resurrection, provision, punishment, guidance. Idhā aradnāhu, when We will it, indicates that will precedes command, and command executes will. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that aradnāhu, We willed it, shows that existence follows divine will, not random, not forced. The phrase li-shay'in, for a thing, indicates that thing is non-existent before will, then willed, then commanded Be, then exists. This sequence refutes those who think world is eternal without beginning; it had beginning by will and command. It also refutes those who think resurrection is impossible because thing no longer exists; if Allah willed it to exist first time from nothing, He can will it to exist second time from remains.

**A Verse That Summarizes Divine Power**

This verse summarizes divine power that has been theme since verse 3: He created heavens and earth, He created humans from drop, He created cattle, horses, rain, crops, night, day, sun, moon, stars, earth colors, sea, mountains, rivers, paths, landmarks, stars. All are by kun. The verse says Our word for a thing when We will it is only Be and it is, meaning all previous creations were by Be. This makes gratitude more profound: the same easy command that creates uncountable blessings also can resurrect you to ask about gratitude. The ease of Be also shows mercy: Allah can provide blessings easily, without hardship to Himself, yet He provides abundantly. The verse ends with exclamation in translation, indicating wonder: Be and it is, immediate, without delay.

**Linguistic Note On Qawl And Kun**

Qawlunā from q-w-l means our word, speech, saying. Li-shay'in, for a thing, from sh-y-ʾ, thing. Idhā aradnāhu, when We willed it, from r-w-d, to will, wish. An naqūla lahu, that We say to it, from q-w-l. Kun from k-w-n, to be, imperative Be. Fa-yakūn, and it is, from k-w-n, with fa- indicating immediate consequence. The verse's structure — Our word for a thing when We will it is only to say Be and it is — is concise, with innamā for exclusivity, only, emphasizing ease. The verse is often quoted to show divine power and to encourage reliance: if Allah wills something for you, it will be by Be, so trust His will, not your own planning alone. The brackets ˹to exist˺ in translation clarify that shay' refers to something to exist, not just any thing.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude. Whether it describes Gardens, death, waiting, consequences, or divine command, the purpose is to make the reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah therefore weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate a heart that is mindful, humble, and grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:41

> **As for those who emigrated in ˹the cause of˺ Allah after being persecuted, We will surely bless them with a good home in this world. But the reward of the Hereafter is far better, if only they knew.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Emigration After Persecution**

Wa-al-ladhīna hājarū fī Allāhi min baʿdi mā ẓulimū — as for those who emigrated in cause of Allah after being persecuted, wronged. Hājarū from h-j-r means to emigrate, to leave home, to abandon. Fī Allāhi means in cause of Allah, for sake of Allah, because of faith. Min baʿdi mā ẓulimū, after being wronged, oppressed, persecuted. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this refers to Muslims who were persecuted in Makkah and emigrated, first to Abyssinia, then to Madinah. Some say verse 41 refers specifically to Abyssinian emigrants, while verse 110 refers to Madinan emigrants. Others say it is general for all who emigrate after being wronged for faith. The persecution included torture, boycott, and social pressure, as experienced by Bilāl, ʿAmmār, and others. The verse acknowledges their wronging and promises recompense.

**Good Home In This World**

La-nubawwi'annahum fī al-dunyā ḥasanatan — We will surely bless them, settle them, with good home, good place in this world. Nubawwi' from b-w-ʾ means to settle, to give abode, to lodge. Ḥasanatan means good, beautiful, goodly. Al-Ṭabarī says this means We will give them good abode in world, either in Madinah after emigration, where they found security and provision, or good mention and honor. Al-Rāzī adds that good home in world includes both physical settlement and spiritual contentment, honor among people, and opening of provision. The oath la- indicates certainty: We will surely. The promise is for those who left homes for Allah after being wronged; Allah will give them better homes. This was fulfilled for emigrants to Madinah who became leaders, and for emigrants to Abyssinia who found protection under Najāshī.

**Reward Of Hereafter Far Better**

Wa-la-ajru al-ākhirati akbaru — but reward of Hereafter is greater, far better. Ajr means reward, wage. Ākhirah, Hereafter, is greater than worldly settlement. Al-Qurṭubī explains that even if worldly good home is given, Hereafter reward is greater in quality, quantity, duration. The comparison akbaru, greater, bigger, indicates that worldly settlement is good but Hereafter is far better, so believer should not be satisfied with worldly settlement alone but should seek Hereafter. The verse ends with law kānū yaʿlamūn — if only they knew, if they knew, indicating that if people knew how great Hereafter reward is, they would emigrate and endure persecution more willingly. Most people do not know, as verse 38 said.

**Why Emigration Is Praised Here**

Emigration after persecution is praised because it shows preference for faith over home, for Allah over comfort. In Makkan context, leaving home meant leaving family, tribe, wealth, status, and facing unknown. Those who did so demonstrated sincerity. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that hājarū fī Allāhi indicates motive: in cause of Allah, not for worldly gain. Min baʿdi mā ẓulimū indicates timing: after being wronged, not before, showing they endured as long as possible then left when staying would mean compromising faith. The verse therefore honors patience and sacrifice. It also consoles those who were wronged and considering emigration: you will have good home in world and far better in Hereafter.

**Connection To Patience And Trust**

Verse 42 will say they are those who patiently endured and in Lord put trust. Emigration requires both patience, ṣabr, and trust, tawakkul. Patience to endure persecution and hardship of leaving, trust that Allah will provide good home. The sūrah's theme of gratitude and patience appears here: those who emigrated after being wronged show gratitude by preserving faith despite persecution, and patience by enduring. Their reward is both worldly and otherworldly, showing that Allah does not let sacrifice for Him go unrewarded.

**Linguistic Note On Hājarū And Nubawwi'**

Hājarū from h-j-r means to emigrate, to cut off, to leave, from same root as hijrah, emigration, and hajr, abandonment. Fī Allāhi, in Allah, meaning in cause of Allah, for Allah. Ẓulimū from ẓ-l-m, to be wronged. La-nubawwi'annahum from b-w-ʾ, to settle, lodge, give abode, with la- for oath, surely. Ḥasanatan, good, from ḥ-s-n. Ajru al-ākhirati akbaru, reward of Hereafter greater, from a-j-r, reward, and k-b-r, great. Law kānū yaʿlamūn, if they knew, from ʿ-l-m, to know. The verse's structure — those who emigrated after being wronged, We will settle them with good in world, but Hereafter reward greater, if they knew — moves from deed to worldly reward to otherworldly reward to awareness, encouraging emigration and patience by showing both immediate and ultimate recompense.

**A Lesson For All Who Are Wronged**

Though verse refers to specific historical emigration, its principle is general: those who are wronged for sake of Allah and who leave wrongdoing environment for Allah will be blessed with good home in world and greater in Hereafter. Al-Qurṭubī extends it to anyone who leaves sinful environment, abandons bad company, or migrates from place where he cannot practice faith. The promise of good home in world is not necessarily physical house but good life, as 16:97 says We will give them good life. The verse therefore offers hope to anyone feeling persecuted for faith: your sacrifice will be rewarded with good now and far better later, if you knew how great reward is, you would not hesitate.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:42

> **˹It is˺ they who have patiently endured, and in their Lord they put their trust.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Patience And Trust As Two Pillars**

Al-ladhīna ṣabarū wa-ʿalā rabbihim yatawakkalūn — those who patiently endured and in their Lord they put trust. Ṣabarū from ṣ-b-r means to be patient, to endure, to hold firm. Yatawakkalūn from w-k-l means to put trust, rely, depend. The verse describes those who emigrated after being wronged in previous verse: they are characterized by patience and trust. Al-Ṭabarī explains that ṣabarū refers to patience in face of persecution, in emigration, in obedience, and in avoiding disobedience. Yatawakkalūn means they rely on Lord for provision, protection, and reward, not on own strength or on people. The two qualities are paired throughout Qur'an as keys to success: patience and trust, as in 29:59: *Those who are patient and upon their Lord rely* (29:59). The order is significant: patience first, then trust, because patience is needed to endure hardship, and trust is needed to look forward to reward.

**Why Patience Is Mentioned**

Patience, ṣabr, in Qur'anic usage includes patience in obedience, patience against disobedience, and patience in face of hardship and persecution. For emigrants, all three were needed: patience to continue obeying Allah despite persecution, patience to avoid retaliating with wrongdoing, patience to endure loss of home and hardship of travel. Al-Rāzī notes that ṣabarū here is general, covering all types, because emigration after being wronged requires comprehensive patience. The verse does not say what they were patient about, leaving it open to include all. This generality makes it applicable beyond historical emigrants to anyone who endures for Allah. The reward for patience is good home in world and greater in Hereafter, as previous verse promised.

**Trust In Lord**

Wa-ʿalā rabbihim yatawakkalūn — and upon their Lord they rely. ʿAlā indicates exclusivity: upon their Lord, not upon others. Rabb is Lord, Nurturer, Master who raises and sustains. Yatawakkalūn is present tense, indicating ongoing, habitual trust, not single act. Al-Qurṭubī explains that trust means to entrust affairs to Allah, to rely on Him for provision and protection, while still taking permissible means. For emigrants, trust meant leaving known homes and going to unknown land, trusting Allah to provide good home as promised. It also meant trusting that Hereafter reward is far better, even though unseen. Trust is therefore both practical and spiritual: practical reliance for worldly settlement, spiritual reliance for otherworldly reward.

**The Connection To Good Home Promise**

Verse 41 promised good home in world and greater reward in Hereafter for those who emigrated after being wronged, if only they knew. Verse 42 explains who they are: those who were patient and trust Lord. The connection is that good home is not given arbitrarily but to those with these qualities. Patience and trust are conditions for reward. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that ṣabarū wa-ʿalā rabbihim yatawakkalūn are two qualities that make emigration successful: without patience, person would return to persecution or compromise; without trust, person would despair about future. Together they enable perseverance. The sūrah's theme of gratitude also appears: patience and trust are forms of gratitude that show recognition that blessings and tests are from Lord.

**A Verse That Applies Beyond Emigration**

Though verse describes emigrants, its description of patience and trust is general and applies to all believers. Al-Rāzī says any believer who is patient in face of hardship and trusts Lord will have good home in world, good life, and greater reward in Hereafter. The verse is therefore encouragement for anyone facing difficulty for faith: be patient and trust Lord. The promise of good home in world may be fulfilled as contentment, honor, provision, or actual settlement, depending on case, but Hereafter reward is certainly greater. The verse's brevity — two qualities in one sentence — makes it memorable and comprehensive.

**Linguistic Note On Ṣabr And Tawakkul**

Ṣabarū from ṣ-b-r means to restrain, to hold, to be patient, from same root as ṣabir, patient, and ṣabr, patience. In Arabic, ṣabr originally means to restrain self from complaint or despair. Yatawakkalūn from w-k-l means to rely, entrust, from same root as wakīl, trustee, agent. Tawakkul means to take Allah as wakīl, trustee, to rely on Him. ʿAlā rabbihim, upon their Lord, indicates that trust is directed exclusively to Lord. The verse's structure — those who were patient and upon Lord they rely — with wa- connecting two qualities, shows that both are needed together, not one alone. Patience without trust could be stoic endurance without hope; trust without patience could be wishful thinking without endurance. Together they form complete attitude of believer who endures hardship while relying on Lord for reward.

**How Patience And Trust Lead To Good Life**

The Qur'an links patience and trust to good life, as 16:97 says *Whoever does righteousness, male or female, while believing, We will surely give them good life* (16:97). Good life includes contentment, guidance, provision, and honor, all part of good home promised in verse 41. Patience and trust cultivate good life because they free heart from anxiety and resentment. Patient person does not despair over past wronging; trusting person does not worry about future provision. Together they allow heart to be present, grateful, and focused on obedience. The verse therefore not only describes emigrants but prescribes path to good life for all: be patient with what befalls, and trust Lord for what is to come.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:43

> **We did not send ˹messengers˺ before you ˹O Prophet˺ except mere men inspired by Us. If you ˹polytheists˺ do not know ˹this already˺, then ask those who have knowledge ˹of the Scriptures˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Messengers Were Mere Men Inspired**

Wa-mā arsalnā min qablika illā rijālan nūḥī ilayhim — We did not send before you except men We inspired. Mā arsalnā, We did not send, negates that messengers were angels or gods. Min qablika, before you, O Prophet, indicating continuity of messengership. Illā rijālan, except men, human males, not angels, not supernatural beings. Nūḥī ilayhim, We inspired them, revealed to them, from w-ḥ-y, to inspire, reveal secretly. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Makkan polytheists found it strange that messenger is human like them, eating food, walking in markets, as 25:7 says *What is this messenger who eats food and walks in markets?* (25:7). The verse answers that all messengers before you were men, not angels, inspired by Us. This is to make message relatable: a human messenger understands human needs, speaks human language, lives human life. Al-Rāzī notes that being men is wisdom: if messengers were angels, people would say we cannot follow angels because we are not angels; since they are men, following is possible.

**If You Do Not Know, Ask Those Who Know**

Fa-is'alū ahla al-dhikri in kuntum lā taʿlamūn — so ask people of remembrance, knowledge, if you do not know. Ahla al-dhikri means people of dhikr, remembrance, knowledge, specifically people of previous scriptures, Jews and Christians who have knowledge of previous messengers being men. Al-Ṭabarī says ahla al-dhikr are scholars of Torah and Gospel who know that messengers before Muhammad ﷺ were men, not angels, so they can confirm. Al-Qurṭubī adds that phrase is general and includes any knowledgeable people: if you do not know about something, ask those who know. This is principle for seeking knowledge: ask experts. In context, polytheists doubted that messenger could be human; they are told to ask those who have knowledge of previous scriptures, who will confirm that previous messengers were men. The verse therefore uses testimony of People of Book to refute Makkan objection.

**Why Human Messengers Are Mercy**

Sending men as messengers is mercy because it allows human connection. A human messenger can be example: he marries, has children, suffers, is persecuted, emigrates, is patient, trusts Lord, as verses 41-42 described. If messenger were angel who does not eat, does not suffer, does not emigrate, he could not be example for emigrants. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that rijālan, men, also implies maturity, strength, not children, indicating messengers were mature men capable of bearing responsibility. Nūḥī ilayhim, We inspired them, shows that though they are men, they are distinguished by revelation, not ordinary. Their humanity does not reduce their honor; it increases relatability while revelation increases guidance.

**The Principle Of Asking Knowledgeable**

Fa-is'alū ahla al-dhikri, ask people of remembrance, is principle that extends beyond this specific issue. Al-Rāzī says it establishes that when one does not know religious matter, one should ask knowledgeable, not speak without knowledge. This is opposite of polytheists who spoke without knowledge when they said revelation is fables and had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others. They spoke without knowledge; they are told to ask those who know. The verse therefore encourages seeking knowledge via questioning experts, which is method of learning. In Sūrat al-Niʿam, which emphasizes reflection, understanding, mindfulness, this verse adds asking as method: reflect yourself, but also ask those who have knowledge.

**Connection To Previous Verses About Emigration And Patience**

Verses 41-42 described emigrants who were patient and trusted Lord. Verse 43 says We did not send before you except men inspired, ask people of knowledge if you do not know. The connection is that emigration and patience of believers follow example of previous messengers who were men, were persecuted, emigrated, were patient. The Makkans who persecuted emigrants were repeating pattern of previous nations who persecuted messengers who were men. Asking people of knowledge would confirm that previous messengers were men and were persecuted and that their peoples were destroyed for denial, as verse 36 said travel and see fate of deniers. The verse therefore places current emigration in continuity of prophetic history.

**Linguistic Note On Rijāl And Dhikr**

Rijāl from r-j-l means men, mature males, from same root as rijl, foot, indicating one who walks, stands. In Qur'anic usage, rijāl often means men of strength and responsibility, not just male gender. Nūḥī from w-ḥ-y means to inspire, reveal, from same root as waḥy, revelation. Ahla al-dhikri, people of remembrance, from a-h-l, people, family, and dh-k-r, remembrance, knowledge, mention. Dhikr can mean Qur'an, previous scriptures, or knowledge generally. In kuntum lā taʿlamūn, if you do not know, from ʿ-l-m, to know. The verse's structure — We did not send before you except men We inspired, so ask people of remembrance if you do not know — moves from principle of human messengership to method of verification via asking knowledgeable, guiding deniers to check their objection against history and knowledge.

**A Lesson On Humanity Of Messengers**

Humanity of messengers is lesson that guidance comes through human example, not only divine command. A human messenger shows that tawḥīd, patience, trust, gratitude can be lived by human, not only angels. This encourages believers: if messengers who were men could be patient and trust Lord while being persecuted and emigrating, so can you. The verse also refutes idea that religion is for angels only; it is for humans, delivered by humans, lived by humans. The command to ask knowledgeable encourages humility: if you do not know, ask, do not deny. This humility is opposite of arrogance that says ancient fables and had Allah willed we would not have worshipped others.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:44

> **˹We sent them˺ with clear proofs and divine Books. And We have sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Reminder, so that you may explain to people what has been revealed for them, and perhaps they will reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Clear Proofs And Divine Books**

Bi-l-bayyināti wa-l-zubur — with clear proofs and divine Books. Bayyināt from b-y-n means clear proofs, evidences, signs that make truth manifest. Zubur from z-b-r means Books, writings, scriptures, from same root as zabūr, Psalms. Al-Ṭabarī explains that previous messengers were sent with bayyināt, clear proofs like miracles, and zubur, Books containing guidance. The phrase bi- indicates they were sent with these as support, so that people cannot claim message was unclear. The previous verse said messengers were men inspired; this verse adds they were not without evidence: they had clear proofs and Books. This answers objection that human messengers have no proof. They did have proof: miracles like staff of Mūsā, and Books like Torah, Gospel, Psalms. The Makkans who demanded proof were told previous messengers had proof, and still many denied, so proof alone does not compel belief; willingness is needed.

**We Sent Down To You The Reminder**

Wa-anzalnā ilayka al-dhikra — and We sent down to you the Reminder, the dhikr. Dhikr here means Qur'an, Reminder, that reminds of Allah, of previous messages, of blessings, of accountability. Al-Ṭabarī says dhikr is Qur'an, called Reminder because it reminds of what was forgotten, of tawḥīd, of previous scriptures. The verb anzalnā, We sent down, indicates descent from above, as with rain and revelation earlier. Ilayka, to you, O Prophet, indicates that Qur'an was sent to Prophet ﷺ specifically to deliver. The phrase wa-anzalnā ilayka al-dhikra after mentioning previous messengers with bayyināt and zubur shows continuity: previous messengers had Books, you have Reminder that confirms and completes.

**So That You May Explain To People**

Li-tubayyina li-l-nāsi mā nuzzila ilayhim — so that you may explain, make clear to people what has been revealed for them. Tubayyina from b-y-n means to explain, clarify, make manifest. Li-l-nāsi, to people, generally, all people to whom message reaches. Mā nuzzila ilayhim, what has been revealed for them, what was sent down to them, meaning Qur'an and previous Books. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Prophet's role is to explain Qur'an to people, to clarify its meanings, rulings, stories, via his words, actions, and example. Al-Rāzī notes that tubayyina indicates that Qur'an needs explanation by Prophet, not only recitation, because it contains general principles that need detailing, as in prayer, zakat, etc. This is basis for Sunnah as explanation of Qur'an. The phrase li-tubayyina also shows that previous Books needed explanation, and Qur'an is explanation, and Prophet explains Qur'an, creating chain of clarification.

**Perhaps They Will Reflect**

Wa-laʿallahum yatafakkarūn — and perhaps they will reflect, think deeply. Yatafakkarūn from f-k-r, to reflect, think, as in verse 11 seal for those who reflect. Laʿallahum indicates hope, purpose: so that perhaps they will reflect. Al-Qurṭubī says reflection is needed to understand explanation and to see that what was revealed is for their benefit. The sūrah has repeatedly called to reflection, understanding, mindfulness, and now adds that Prophet's explanation is to facilitate reflection. The sequence is: Allah sends Reminder, Prophet explains, people reflect. Reflection is not automatic; it requires explanation and willingness. The verse therefore places Prophet as mediator who makes reflection possible by clarifying what was revealed.

**Why Explanation Is Needed**

Qur'an contains concise verses that need elaboration, as well as stories that need context, and rulings that need detail. Without explanation, people might differ on meaning, as they differed before, as verse 39 said resurrection will make clear what they differed on. In world, Prophet's explanation makes clear what they differed on, as verse 64 will say *We revealed Book only to clarify what they differed about* (16:64). Explanation is therefore mercy that prevents dispute. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that li-tubayyina li-l-nāsi indicates that explanation should be in language people understand, with wisdom and good exhortation as verse 125 will say. The Prophet ﷺ explained via speech, action, and character, making abstract principles concrete.

**Connection To Asking Knowledgeable**

Verse 43 said if you do not know, ask people of remembrance. Verse 44 says We sent messengers with clear proofs and Books and sent down to you Reminder so you may explain to people what was revealed and perhaps they will reflect. The connection is that asking knowledgeable is part of reflection, and Prophet is most knowledgeable to explain. Those who do not know should ask, and Prophet explains, so that they may reflect. The two verses together establish chain of knowledge: Allah sends, messengers deliver with proofs and Books, knowledgeable preserve, people ask, Prophet explains, people reflect. This chain ensures guidance is accessible.

**Linguistic Note On Bayyināt, Zubur, Dhikr**

Bayyināt from b-y-n means clear, manifest, from same root as bayān, clarification. Zubur from z-b-r means Books, writings, from zabara, to write. Dhikr from dh-k-r means remembrance, reminder, mention, from same root as dhikr, remembrance of Allah. Anzala from n-z-l means to send down. Tubayyina from b-y-n means to make clear, explain. Nuzzila from n-z-l means to be sent down, revealed. Yatafakkarūn from f-k-r means to reflect, think deeply. The verse's structure — with clear proofs and Books, and We sent down to you Reminder so you may explain what was revealed and perhaps they will reflect — moves from previous messengers' equipment to current messenger's Book to his role to purpose, showing continuity and progression in guidance, with reflection as intended outcome.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:45

> **Do those who devise evil plots feel secure that Allah will not cause the earth to swallow them? Or that the torment will not come upon them in ways they cannot comprehend?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Feeling Secure While Plotting Evil**

A-fa-amina al-ladhīna makarū al-sayyi'āti an yakhsifa Allāhu bihim al-arḍa — do those who plot evil feel secure that Allah will not cause earth to swallow them? A-fa-amina, do they feel secure, safe, from a-m-n, security, safety. Al-ladhīna makarū al-sayyi'āt, those who plot evil, devise bad plots, from m-k-r, plot, and s-y-ʾ, evil. An yakhsifa Allāhu bihim al-arḍa, that Allah will cause earth to swallow them, from kh-s-f, to swallow, sink, eclipse. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this refers to those who plot against messengers and believers, like Makkans who plotted against Prophet ﷺ in Dār al-Nadwah, feeling secure that no punishment will come. The question a-fa-amina is rhetorical, expecting no, they should not feel secure. The punishment of earth swallowing is mentioned as example of worldly punishment that came to previous nations, like Qārūn who was swallowed, as 28:81 says *So We caused earth to swallow him and his house* (28:81). The verse warns that feeling secure while plotting evil is delusion.

**Torment From Ways They Cannot Comprehend**

Aw ya'tiyahum al-ʿadhābu min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn — or that torment will not come to them from where they do not perceive. This repeats phrase from verse 26: torment came upon them from where they did not expect. Al-Ṭabarī says torment can come from unexpected direction, manner, time, as building collapse from foundations and roof from above in verse 26. The phrase min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn, from where they do not perceive, emphasizes that plotters guard some directions but cannot guard all. Their plotting is based on perceived security, but security is only with Allah. The verse lists two possibilities: earth swallowing from below, and torment from unexpected direction, covering below and surrounding.

**Why Earth Swallowing Is Mentioned**

Earth swallowing is powerful image because earth is seen as stable, firm, thanks to mountains that prevent shaking, as verse 15 said. If earth that is stable can be made to swallow, then no stability is independent of Allah. Al-Rāzī notes that earth swallowing is fitting punishment for those who plot evil because they plot on earth, using earth as base for building and plotting, so earth itself becomes instrument of punishment. Qārūn was swallowed because he was proud of earthly treasures. The Makkans who felt secure in Makkah, with its firm mountains and sanctuary, are warned that earth beneath them can swallow if Allah wills. The mention of earth swallowing also connects to mountains as stabilizers: mountains prevent earth from shaking with you, but if Allah wills, earth can still swallow despite mountains, showing that ultimate stability is by Allah's will, not by mountains alone.

**Feeling Secure As Disease**

Amina, to feel secure, safe, is praised when it is security in Allah, but condemned when it is security from Allah's punishment while persisting in evil. The Qur'an says *Do they feel secure from Allah's plan? None feels secure from Allah's plan except losing people* (7:99). Feeling secure while plotting evil is sign of heedlessness and arrogance. It indicates that person thinks his plotting is clever enough to avoid consequence, or that Allah does not see, or that punishment is distant. The verse asks rhetorical question to break that false security: do you feel secure that earth will not swallow you? The expected answer is no, you should not feel secure. Al-Qurṭubī says feeling secure from Allah's punishment while persisting in sin is major sin, because it shows lack of fear of Allah.

**Connection To Previous Plotting Verses**

Verses 26-27 described those before plotted and building collapsed and they were humiliated. Verses 45-47 continue theme of plotters feeling secure, asking if they feel secure from earth swallowing, from torment from unexpected, from being seized while going about, from gradual destruction. The four possibilities cover different modes of punishment: sudden from below, sudden from unexpected, sudden while active, gradual. The list shows that punishment can come in many ways, so feeling secure from one mode does not mean secure from others. The plotters who guard against one may be caught by another. The sūrah thus systematically dismantles false security.

**Linguistic Note On Makara And Khasafa**

Makarū from m-k-r means to plot, scheme with subtlety. Al-sayyi'āt, evil, from s-w-ʾ, evil, here meaning evil plots. Amina from a-m-n means to feel secure, safe, from same root as īmān, faith, which is security. An yakhsifa, that He causes to swallow, from kh-s-f, to sink, swallow, eclipse, used for earth swallowing and for eclipse. Bihim al-arḍa, with them the earth, meaning earth swallows them. Aw ya'tiyahum al-ʿadhābu, or torment comes to them, from a-t-y, to come. Min ḥaythu lā yashʿurūn, from where they do not perceive, from sh-ʿ-r, to perceive. The verse's structure — question, do they feel secure that earth will not swallow them or torment will not come from where they do not perceive — with two possibilities, emphasizes that security is false and punishment can come from below or from unexpected, leaving no direction safe for plotter except repentance.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:46

> **Or that He will not seize them while they go about ˹their day˺, for then they will have no escape?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Seizure While Going About**

Aw ya'khudhahum fī taqallubihim — or that He will not seize them while they are going about, turning, moving. Taqallub from q-l-b means to turn, to go about, to move in daily affairs, travel, trade, seeking provision. Al-Ṭabarī explains that taqallubihim means while they are going about in land for trade, travel, work, feeling secure, not expecting punishment. The question is continuation of previous: do those who plot evil feel secure that Allah will not seize them while going about? The answer is no, they should not feel secure. Seizure while going about means punishment can come during normal activity, not only during plotting. A person may plot at night and feel secure by day while trading, but punishment can come while trading. Al-Rāzī notes that taqallub includes turning in beds, turning in markets, turning in journeys, indicating all daily movements. No moment is safe from Allah's seizure if one persists in evil.

**No Escape When Seized**

Fa-mā hum bi-muʿjizīn — for then they will have no escape, will not be able to escape, frustrate, evade. Muʿjizīn from ʿ-j-z means to be unable, to escape, to make helpless, from same root as iʿjāz, inimitability. Al-Ṭabarī says fa-mā hum bi-muʿjizīn means they will not be able to escape Allah's punishment, to frustrate His plan, to make Him unable. When Allah seizes, there is no escape. The phrase appears in other warnings, as in 29:22: *You will not be able to escape on earth nor in heaven* (29:22). The verse emphasizes that feeling secure while going about is delusion because when seizure comes, escape is impossible. The time to seek security is before seizure, by abandoning evil plotting and turning to gratitude and tawḥīd.

**Why Seizure While Active Is Mentioned**

Previous verse mentioned earth swallowing from below and torment from unexpected direction. This verse adds seizure while active, covering another mode: punishment while person is engaged in worldly activity, feeling secure in routine. Al-Qurṭubī says this includes being seized while traveling, trading, farming, etc., when person thinks he is safe because he is busy with lawful activity. The warning is that evil plotting taints all activity; even while doing apparently lawful daily going about, punishment can come because plotting continues in heart. The verse also shows that Allah's punishment is not limited to moments of sin; it can come at any time, so one should not feel secure at any time while persisting in evil.

**The Connection To Security And Gratitude**

Sūrat al-Niʿam emphasizes that security is from Allah, not from plotting or wealth. Mountains prevent earth from shaking with you as mercy, but if you plot evil, that security can be removed. The verse asks do they feel secure that He will not seize them while going about, implying that true security is only for those who are grateful and mindful, not for plotters. The righteous who emigrated after being wronged and were patient and trusted Lord were given good home in world as security, but that security came after patience and trust, not while plotting evil. The contrast is clear: plotters feel false security while plotting, righteous receive true security after patience and trust.

**A Lesson On Constant Awareness**

The verse teaches constant awareness that Allah can seize at any moment, so one should not feel secure in disobedience at any moment. Al-Rāzī notes that taqallubihim, while going about, includes all states: awake, asleep, traveling, staying, working, resting. No state is safe from seizure if one persists in evil. Therefore one should be mindful in all states, not only during worship. This is meaning of taqwa, mindfulness, being conscious of Allah in all going about. The sūrah's call to reflect, understand, be mindful applies to all moments, not only moments of ritual.

**Linguistic Note On Taqallub And Muʿjizīn**

Taqallub from q-l-b means to turn, flip, go about, from same root as qalb, heart, which turns, and taqlīb, turning. In this context, fī taqallubihim means in their going about, turning in affairs. Ya'khudhahum from a-kh-dh means to seize, take. Fa-mā hum bi-muʿjizīn, they will not be able to escape, from ʿ-j-z, to be unable, to escape. The construction mā hum bi-muʿjizīn with bi- emphasizes negation: they are not at all able to escape. The verse's brevity — two clauses, question and answer — delivers warning that seizure can come during daily activity and then no escape, urging listener not to feel secure while persisting in evil plotting.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:47

> **Or that He will not destroy them gradually? But your Lord is truly Ever Gracious, Most Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gradual Destruction**

Aw ya'khudhahum ʿalā takhawwufin — or that He will not seize them gradually, with fear, by diminishing. Takhawwuf from kh-w-f means fear, but also gradual reduction, taking away little by little, causing fear through erosion. Al-Ṭabarī explains that ʿalā takhawwufin means Allah seizes them gradually, reducing their wealth, strength, numbers, little by little, so they fear and anticipate total destruction, or that He seizes some and leaves others to fear. Al-Rāzī says takhawwuf can mean that punishment comes with fear, causing them to be in fear while being seized, or that it comes by diminishing their sides, reducing their territory, as 13:41 says *Do they not see that We come to land reducing it from its borders?* (13:41). The question is continuation: do plotters feel secure that He will not destroy them gradually? They should not, because gradual destruction is also possible and is happening.

**But Your Lord Is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful**

Fa-inna rabbakum la-ra'ūfun raḥīm — but indeed your Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. This seal appears earlier in verse 7 after mentioning cattle bearing loads without hardship. Here it appears after listing four modes of punishment: earth swallowing, torment from unexpected, seizure while going about, gradual destruction. After such warnings, the verse ends with mercy to show that delay in punishment is due to graciousness and mercy, not inability or approval. Al-Ṭabarī says ra'ūf, gracious, means tender, preventing harm, and raḥīm, merciful, means granting good. The fact that plotters are not immediately swallowed, seized, or destroyed gradually is due to Allah's graciousness and mercy that gives time to repent. Al-Rāzī notes that this seal also consoles believers who are persecuted: your Lord is gracious and merciful, so He will not let plotters succeed forever, and He will provide good home for those who emigrated.

**Why Gradual Punishment Is Mentioned**

Gradual punishment, takhawwuf, is subtle: wealth decreases little by little, health declines, supporters leave, status erodes, so person feels fear and loss but may not realize it is punishment. Al-Qurṭubī says this is more frightening than sudden punishment because it gives time to reflect but also allows heedlessness to continue. The verse lists gradual as fourth mode after sudden from below, sudden from unexpected, sudden while active, to show that punishment can be sudden or gradual, overt or subtle, so feeling secure from one mode does not mean secure from others. The plotters who feel secure because earth has not swallowed them yet may be experiencing gradual erosion without noticing.

**Graciousness And Mercy As Reason For Delay**

The seal la-ra'ūfun raḥīm explains why punishment is delayed despite plotting. Allah is gracious, preventing immediate destruction, and merciful, granting time and provision even to plotters, as 10:11 says *If Allah were to hasten evil for people as they hasten good, their term would have ended* (10:11). Delay is mercy that allows repentance. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that ra'ūf is more intense than raḥīm in preventing harm, so mention of both indicates Allah both prevents immediate harm and grants time for good. The verse therefore balances warning with hope: warning that punishment can come in many ways, hope that delay is due to mercy that invites return.

**Connection To Previous Verses About Security**

Verses 45-47 form unit asking do plotters feel secure from four modes: earth swallowing, torment from unexpected, seizure while going about, gradual destruction. All four are answered by implication: they should not feel secure. Then verse ends with but your Lord is gracious, merciful, indicating that if they are secure so far, it is due to mercy, not due to their plotting's success. The sūrah's blessings catalogue also showed graciousness and mercy: cattle bearing loads without hardship because Lord is gracious, merciful. Now same names explain delay in punishment: Lord is gracious, merciful, so He delays. The two uses show that graciousness and mercy appear both in provision and in forbearance.

**Linguistic Note On Takhawwuf**

Takhawwuf from kh-w-f means fear, but in form V it can mean to cause fear, to diminish, to take from sides. Lisān al-ʿArab defines takhawwafa as to diminish, to take away gradually, causing fear. ʿAlā takhawwufin, upon fear, with gradual diminishing, indicates mode of seizure that causes fear through reduction. Ya'khudhahum, He seizes them, from a-kh-dh. Fa-inna rabbakum la-ra'ūfun raḥīm, but your Lord is gracious, merciful, with la- for emphasis, surely. Ra'ūf from r-'-f, to be gracious, tender, and raḥīm from r-ḥ-m, mercy. The verse's structure — question, or that He will not destroy them gradually, then statement, but your Lord is gracious, merciful — moves from warning to reason for delay, guiding listener to see that security is due to mercy that should lead to repentance, not to continued plotting.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.
