## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:48

> **Have they not considered how the shadows of everything Allah has created incline to the right and the left ˹as the sun moves˺, totally submitting to Allah in all humility?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shadows Inclining Right And Left**

A-wa-lam yaraw ilā mā khalaqa Allāhu min shay'in yaẓillu ẓilāluhu ʿan al-yamīni wa-l-shamā'ili sujjadan li-Allāhi — have they not considered how shadows of everything Allah created incline to right and left, prostrating to Allah? Yaraw from r-ʾ-y means to see, consider, reflect. Mā khalaqa Allāhu min shay'in, whatever Allah created of things, general, includes everything with shadow: humans, animals, trees, mountains, buildings. Yaẓillu ẓilāluhu, its shadows incline, from ẓ-l-l, to shade, shadow, incline. ʿAn al-yamīni wa-l-shamā'ili, to right and left, indicating movement of shadows as sun moves from east to west, shadows move from west to east, inclining. Sujjadan li-Allāhi, prostrating to Allah, in submission. Al-Ṭabarī explains that shadows prostrate to Allah by inclining, submitting to His command, following sun's course that Allah decreed. The prostration is not like human prostration with intention, but subjection to divine command, as 13:15 says *And to Allah prostrates whoever is in heavens and earth willingly or unwillingly, and their shadows in mornings and evenings* (13:15).

**Shadows As Submitting Creatures**

Shadows are described as sujjadan li-Allāhi wa-hum dākhirūn — prostrating to Allah while they are humble, submissive. Dākhirūn from d-kh-r means to be humble, submissive, lowly, from same root as dakhara, to enter humbly. Shadows are humble because they follow light, cannot stand alone, cannot resist sun's movement. Al-Rāzī notes that shadows prostrating is sign of Allah's power: even shadows, which are non-material, intangible, obey Allah's command to incline. If shadows obey, how can humans who have intellect and will refuse? The verse uses shadows as example of universal submission: everything, even shadows, submits to Allah, while some humans refuse out of arrogance. The contrast is stark: shadows, which have no choice, submit humbly; humans, who have choice, often refuse arrogantly.

**Why Shadows Are Mentioned After Punishment Warnings**

Verses 45-47 warned plotters who feel secure from earth swallowing, torment from unexpected, seizure while going about, gradual destruction, but Lord is gracious, merciful. Verse 48 shifts to shadows prostrating humbly to Allah, showing that everything submits, even shadows, so plotters' feeling of security and arrogance is out of sync with creation. All creation submits, even shadows; why do plotters not submit? Al-Qurṭubī says shadows prostrating is visible sign that can be seen daily: look at shadows morning and evening, they incline right and left, submitting. If you see that, you should submit too. The verse therefore calls to reflection via observable phenomenon that is constant and undeniable.

**The Science Of Shadows And Sun Movement**

Shadows inclining right and left as sun moves is due to sun's apparent movement from east to west, causing shadows to move opposite. In morning, shadows long to west, at noon short, in evening long to east, inclining. The Qur'an describes this as sujjadan, prostrating, indicating that shadows' movement is not random but commanded, following divine law. Al-Rāzī discusses that shadows' prostration is by subjection to natural law that Allah created, and that law is itself command. Modern understanding of light and shadow confirms regularity, but Qur'an points to meaning behind regularity: submission to Creator. The verse invites to see shadows not as mere absence of light but as creatures submitting.

**A Sign For Those Who Reflect**

Though seal here is not explicitly for those who reflect, the question a-wa-lam yaraw, have they not considered, have they not seen, invites reflection. The same root r-ʾ-y appears in many calls to reflection. Shadows prostrating is sign that everything submits, so human should submit too. The verse also prepares for next verses about everything in heavens and earth bowing to Allah, including living creatures and angels who are not too proud. Shadows are non-living, yet they submit; living creatures and angels also submit, as next verses say. The progression is from shadows to living creatures to angels, showing universal submission, with humans who refuse being exception.

**Linguistic Note On Ẓill And Sujūd**

Ẓill from ẓ-l-l means shadow, shade, from same root as ẓullah, shade, and ẓalīl, shady. Ẓilāl plural of ẓill. Yaẓillu from ẓ-l-l means to incline, to be shady. Yamīni, right, and shamā'il, lefts, plural of shimāl, left. Sujjadan, prostrating, from s-j-d, to prostrate, submit. Dākhirūn, humble, from d-kh-r. The verse's structure — have they not considered how shadows of everything Allah created incline right and left prostrating to Allah while humble — with question and description, invites observation of daily phenomenon and interpretation as submission, guiding listener to see that even shadows submit, so he should too.

**How Shadows Teach Humility**

Shadows are humble because they depend on light and object; without light, no shadow; without object, no shadow. They cannot exist independently. Their humility, dākhirūn, teaches that all creation depends on Allah and is humble before Him. A human who sees his own shadow inclining should remember that he too depends on Allah and should be humble, not arrogant. The verse therefore uses shadow as teacher of humility, opposite of arrogance that leads to Hell as evil home for arrogant. The shadow that prostrates humbly will be in Paradise where shadows are permanent and pleasant, as 13:35 says shade permanent, while arrogant who refused to prostrate will be in Hell where no shade.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:49

> **And to Allah ˹alone˺ bows down ˹in submission˺ whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth of living creatures, as do the angels—who are not too proud ˹to do so˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Universal Prostration To Allah**

Wa-li-Allāhi yasjudu mā fī al-samāwāti wa-mā fī al-arḍi min dābbatin wa-l-malā'ikatu — and to Allah bows down whatever is in heavens and whatever on earth of living creatures, and angels. Yasjudu from s-j-d means to prostrate, bow down, submit. Mā fī al-samāwāti wa-mā fī al-arḍi, whatever is in heavens and earth, includes all creation. Min dābbatin, of living creatures, from d-b-b, to crawl, creep, move, meaning all animals that move on earth, including humans, animals, insects. Wa-l-malā'ikatu, and angels, mentioned separately though they are in heavens, to emphasize their prostration despite high rank. Al-Ṭabarī explains that everything prostrates to Allah, willingly or unwillingly, as 13:15 says *To Allah prostrates whoever is in heavens and earth willingly or unwillingly* (13:15). Living creatures prostrate by subjection to Allah's command, by following natural laws, by being under His control. Angels prostrate willingly, constantly, without pride.

**Angels Not Too Proud**

Wa-hum lā yastakbirūn — while they are not too proud, not arrogant. Yastakbirūn from k-b-r, to be arrogant, as in previous verses about arrogant. Angels are not too proud to prostrate to Allah, despite being near to Allah, pure, powerful. Al-Qurṭubī says this is to shame humans who are too proud to prostrate, while angels who are greater in creation are not too proud. The phrase lā yastakbirūn appears in description of angels in 21:19: *Those near Him are not too proud to worship Him* (21:19). Angels' lack of pride is model for humans: if angels who have no sin are not too proud to bow, how can humans who have sins be too proud? The verse therefore contrasts angels' humility with humans' arrogance that leads to Hell as evil home.

**Why Living Creatures Are Mentioned**

Min dābbatin, of living creatures, emphasizes that prostration includes moving, living beings, not only inanimate shadows as previous verse. Dābbah includes all that moves on earth: humans, animals, insects, as 11:6 says *There is no living creature on earth but its provision is upon Allah* (11:6). Their prostration is by subjection to Allah's command in their creation, movement, life, death. Al-Rāzī explains that dābbah prostrates by following instincts and laws that Allah placed, like bee inspired to make homes in mountains as verse 68 will say, and by being under Allah's control. Even if they do not prostrate like humans with forehead on ground, they prostrate by submission to divine decree. The verse invites to see all moving creatures as submitting, so human who moves should also submit.

**The Connection To Shadows**

Verse 48 said shadows incline right and left prostrating to Allah in humility. Verse 49 says whatever is in heavens and earth of living creatures and angels bow to Allah, not too proud. The progression is from non-living shadows to living creatures to angels, showing universal prostration across all categories: inanimate, animate, spiritual. Shadows prostrate, living creatures prostrate, angels prostrate. Who remains who does not prostrate? Only arrogant humans and jinn who refuse out of pride. The sūrah thus isolates arrogant as exception to universal submission, making their refusal appear unnatural and foolish. All creation bows; why do you not?

**A Verse That Prepares For Command Not To Take Two Gods**

Verse 51 will say *Do not take two gods, there is only One God, so be in awe of Me alone*. The universal prostration to Allah alone prepares for that command: if everything prostrates to Allah alone, then taking two gods contradicts universal prostration. The verse also prepares for verse 52: *To Him belongs whatever is in heavens and earth and to Him alone is everlasting devotion*. If everything belongs to Him and prostrates to Him, then devotion should be to Him alone. The sūrah's argument is consistent: creation, subjection, prostration all point to tawḥīd.

**Linguistic Note On Dābbah And Istikbār**

Dābbah from d-b-b means to crawl, move slowly, used for any living creature that moves on earth, including humans in some usages. Yasjudu from s-j-d means to prostrate, bow, submit. Wa-l-malā'ikatu, and angels, from m-l-k, angel. Lā yastakbirūn, not too proud, from k-b-r, to be great, form X meaning to deem self great, be arrogant. The verse's structure — and to Allah bows whatever in heavens and earth of living creatures and angels, while they are not too proud — with universal quantifier mā and specific mention of angels and negation of pride, emphasizes that prostration is universal and humility is characteristic of those who prostrate, especially angels who are high yet not proud.

**How This Verse Teaches Humility**

The verse teaches humility by showing that even angels, who are near Allah and pure, are not too proud to bow. If angels bow, human should bow. The phrase wa-hum lā yastakbirūn, they are not too proud, is both description of angels and indirect criticism of humans who are too proud, as verses 22-23, 29 described arrogant. The remedy for arrogance is to join universal prostration: bow to Allah alone, as everything else does. The verse therefore invites to be part of universal submission rather than exception that will be humiliated on Day as verse 27 said.


**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes emigration, patience, trust, human messengers, clear proofs, earth swallowing, or angels fearing, the purpose is to make reader recognize blessings and respond with thanks and reflection. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on this verse with that mindset will find both knowledge and motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death and enter Gardens.

**How This Verse Fits The Sūrah's Argument**

The sūrah moves from blessings to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to guidance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either reward for patience and trust, or principle of human messengers, or modes of punishment, or universal submission. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next command. The reader is invited to see connection and to respond with gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, rather than with denial, arrogance, and plotting.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:50

> **They fear their Lord above them, and do whatever they are commanded.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fear Of Lord Above Them**

Yakhāfūna rabbahum min fawqihim — they fear their Lord above them. The pronoun refers to angels who prostrate and are not too proud, as described in previous verse. Yakhāfūna from kh-w-f means to fear with reverence. Rabbahum, their Lord, who creates, sustains, commands. Min fawqihim, from above them, indicates Allah is above creation, high, exalted, as 6:18 says He is the Subjugator above His servants. Al-Ṭabarī explains angels fear Lord above because they know His greatness and that He is above them in rank and dominion. The phrase also indicates Allah is above in literal highness as understood by early generations without asking how. Al-Rāzī discusses fawq can mean above in rank and power, not only spatial, but includes both. Angels fear despite being sinless and near, because they know Allah's greatness. If sinless angels fear, how much more should sinful humans fear? This fear is reverential, with knowledge that Lord is above, watching, commanding.

**Doing Whatever They Are Commanded**

Wa-yafʿalūna mā yu'marūn — and they do whatever they are commanded. Yafʿalūna from f-ʿ-l means to do, act. Mā yu'marūn, what they are commanded, from a-m-r, command. Al-Ṭabarī says angels do whatever Allah commands without disobedience, delay, or questioning, as 66:6 says they do not disobey Allah in what He commands and do what commanded. The phrase indicates complete obedience: they do all that is commanded, not some. This contrasts with humans who often do not do what commanded due to arrogance or heedlessness. Angels' obedience is model: fear Lord above, do what commanded. Their obedience is constant, not occasional, and covers all commands, from taking souls to carrying revelation to prostrating.

**Why Fear And Obedience Are Paired**

Fear and obedience are paired because fear of Lord above leads to doing what is commanded. Without fear, obedience may be superficial; with fear, obedience is sincere and prompt. Al-Qurṭubī notes angels fear despite being sinless and near to Allah, because they know Allah's greatness. If sinless angels fear, how much more should sinful humans fear? The verse therefore encourages fear that leads to obedience, not fear that paralyzes. Fear is reverential, with knowledge that Lord is above, watching, commanding, able to punish or forgive. Obedience without fear can be mechanical; fear without obedience can be despair. Together they form complete worship: inward fear and outward obedience, heart and limbs aligned.

**Connection To Universal Prostration**

Verse 49 said whatever in heavens and earth of living creatures and angels bow to Allah, not too proud. Verse 50 explains why angels bow: they fear Lord above and do what commanded. Their prostration is not forced but from fear and obedience. This explains universal prostration: all creation prostrates because it fears Lord above and does what commanded by natural law, while angels prostrate willingly with fear and obedience. Humans who refuse lack fear and disobey, making them exception. The sūrah thus shows prostration is natural response to Lord above, and refusal is unnatural. The progression from shadows to living creatures to angels shows universal submission, with arrogant humans as exception who will be humiliated.

**A Verse That Prepares For Command Of Tawḥīd**

The next verse will command not to take two gods, there is only One, so be in awe of Me alone. Mention that angels fear Lord above and do what commanded prepares for that command. If angels who are near fear and obey, humans should fear and obey Allah alone, not take two gods. The verse also prepares for to Him belongs whatever in heavens and earth and to Him alone is everlasting devotion. If angels fear Lord above and do what commanded, then devotion should be to Him alone, as angels do. The verse's placement after universal prostration and before command of tawḥīd is deliberate: it shows model of worship that is fearful, obedient, humble, not proud, prostrating.

**How Angels Model Worship For Humans**

Angels model worship that is fearful, obedient, humble, not proud, prostrating. They fear Lord above, not equal, not below, showing hierarchy: Lord above, angels below, yet not too proud to bow. Humans are below angels in some ways, yet often too proud to bow. The verse invites to imitate angels: fear Lord above, do what commanded, bow, not be proud. Earlier description of shadows prostrating humbly and living creatures bowing shows all creation models worship; angels model it most perfectly because they do whatever commanded without exception. The believer who wants good home of righteous should imitate angelic fear and obedience, making fear of Lord above a daily awareness that leads to doing what is commanded, whether prayer, gratitude, patience, or trust.

**A Lesson That Connects To Gratitude And Reflection**

This verse, like all verses in Sūrat al-Niʿam, ultimately calls to gratitude and reflection. Whether it describes angels fearing and obeying, the purpose is to make reader recognize that even angels, who have no sin, fear and obey, so human who has many blessings and many shortcomings should fear and obey even more. Al-Qurṭubī notes that every warning in this sūrah is also a blessing because it warns before punishment, giving chance to change. Every promise is also a blessing because it encourages good. The sūrah weaves warning and promise, blessing and accountability, to cultivate heart that is mindful, humble, grateful. The reader who reflects on angels' fear and obedience with that mindset will find motivation to be among those who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, and who are greeted with peace at death.


**The Practical Meaning Of Above**

Min fawqihim, above them, teaches that Allah is above creation, not within creation, distinct, high. This has practical meaning: when you fear someone above you, you are humble, you look up, you obey. Angels fear Lord above, so they obey. Humans who fear Lord above will also obey, not take two gods, not associate others, will be in awe of Him alone as next verse says. The concept of above also gives hope: Lord above sees, knows what you conceal and reveal, knows your patience and trust, and will reward. Fear of Lord above therefore includes both awe and hope, both reverence and reliance, leading to complete worship that angels model.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:51

> **And Allah has said, “Do not take two gods. There is only One God. So be in awe of Me ˹alone˺.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prohibition Of Taking Two Gods**

Wa-qāla Allāhu lā tattakhidhū ilāhayni ithnayn — and Allah has said, do not take two gods, two deities. Qāla Allāhu, Allah has said, direct speech from Allah, showing importance. Lā tattakhidhū, do not take, do not adopt, from a-kh-dh, to take. Ilāhayni ithnayn, two gods, dual, emphasizing prohibition of duality. Innama huwa ilāhun wāḥid — indeed He is only One God. Innama for exclusivity, only, He is One God. Fa-iyyāya fa-irhabūn — so be in awe of Me alone, fear Me alone, from r-h-b, to fear, be in awe. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse explicitly forbids taking two gods, meaning forbids shirk, forbids worshipping Allah and idols together. The dual ilāhayni ithnayn is emphatic: do not take two, meaning do not take more than One, whether two or many. The command lā tattakhidhū is general, for all people, all times. The reason innama huwa ilāhun wāḥid, He is only One God, gives basis: because He is One, you should not take two.

**Why Two Is Mentioned**

Why say two gods specifically, not many? Al-Zamakhsharī explains that two is minimal plural that constitutes shirk; if two is forbidden, then many is even more forbidden. Also, Arabs sometimes said Allah and idols as two kinds, so prohibition of two directly addresses their practice: Allah plus idols as two. Al-Rāzī notes that ithnayn, two, after ilāhayni is for emphasis and clarification, to remove any ambiguity that ilāhayn could mean two types of one god; it means two distinct gods. The verse therefore closes door to any duality: do not take two gods, whether you think second is equal or lesser, intercessor or partner. All duality is forbidden.

**So Be In Awe Of Me Alone**

Fa-iyyāya fa-irhabūn — so Me alone be in awe of, fear. Iyyāya is for emphasis: Me alone, only Me. Irhabūn from r-h-b means to fear with awe, to be in dread, to revere. Al-Ṭabarī says irhabūn means fear Me, be in awe of Me, not of idols, not of people. The command to fear Allah alone follows prohibition of two gods because fear is part of worship. If you take two gods, you will fear both, hope from both, which divides heart. If you take only One God, you fear only One, which unifies heart. The sūrah's earlier description of angels fearing Lord above them and doing what commanded models this: they fear One above, not two. The command fa-iyyāya fa-irhabūn is therefore call to exclusive awe.

**Connection To Universal Prostration**

Verses 48-50 described shadows prostrating, living creatures and angels bowing, fearing Lord above, doing what commanded. Verse 51 says Allah said do not take two gods, there is only One, so be in awe of Me alone. The connection is that universal prostration to One God shows that taking two gods contradicts what all creation does. All creation bows to One; why do you take two? The command to be in awe of Me alone is supported by universal submission: if everything bows to One, you should too. The verse also prepares for next: to Him belongs whatever in heavens and earth and to Him alone is everlasting devotion, will you fear other than Allah? If you fear Me alone, you will give everlasting devotion to Me alone.

**A Verse That Summarizes Tawḥīd**

This verse summarizes tawḥīd in three parts: prohibition, affirmation, consequence. Prohibition: do not take two gods. Affirmation: indeed He is One God. Consequence: so be in awe of Me alone. This mirrors lā ilāha illā Allāh: no god except Allah, so fear Him alone. Al-Qurṭubī says this verse is one of clearest statements of tawḥīd in Qur'an, comparable to 112:1-4. It is direct speech from Allah, not reported, to emphasize authority: Allah Himself says do not take two gods. The direct speech makes it more powerful than if messenger said it. The listener hears Allah speaking, commanding oneness and awe.

**Linguistic Note On Ilāhayn And Rahbah**

Ilāhayn dual of ilāh, god, from a-l-h, to worship. Ithnayn, two, from th-n-y, two. Innama huwa ilāhun wāḥid, He is only One God, with innama for exclusivity. Fa-iyyāya, so Me, with iyyā for exclusive object. Fa-irhabūn, so be in awe, from r-h-b, to fear, awe, with fa- for consequence. Rahbah is fear with reverence, stronger than khawf, includes awe. The verse's structure — Allah said do not take two gods, indeed He is One, so be in awe of Me — moves from divine speech to prohibition to affirmation to command, creating clear, memorable statement of tawḥīd that is both theological and practical: believe One, fear One.

**How This Verse Fits Sūrah's Argument**

Sūrah al-Naḥl argues from blessings to Creator to oneness to accountability. This verse is pivot from argument to command: after showing that all creation submits to One, that idols create nothing and are dead, that Allah knows what you conceal and reveal, now Allah commands directly: do not take two gods. The command is not new information but conclusion of previous argument. If you accept that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and that everything prostrates to Him, then you should not take two gods. The command to be in awe of Me alone also prepares for next verses about blessings being from Allah and crying to Him alone in hardship, showing that exclusive awe leads to exclusive reliance.

**A Lesson On Exclusive Devotion**

Exclusive awe of Allah alone means heart fears displeasing Him more than displeasing others, hopes for His mercy more than others' approval, and relies on Him alone. Al-Rāzī notes that irhabūn, be in awe, includes both fear of punishment and awe of greatness, which together produce obedience. When heart is in awe of One alone, it is freed from fear of many, which is true freedom. The verse therefore offers liberation: do not fear idols, tyrants, or people; fear Me alone, and you will be free from many fears. This is practical tawḥīd that transforms daily life: decisions made to please One, not many.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:52

> **To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, and to Him ˹alone˺ is the everlasting devotion. Will you then fear any other than Allah?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**To Him Belongs Whatever In Heavens And Earth**

Wa-lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi — and to Him belongs whatever is in heavens and earth. Lahu, to Him, belongs, possessive. Mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi, whatever is in heavens and earth, includes all creation: angels, stars, humans, cattle, sea, mountains, etc. Al-Ṭabarī explains that everything in heavens and earth belongs to Allah as creation and possession; He created it, owns it, controls it. This is basis for tawḥīd: if everything belongs to Him, then no one else owns anything independently to be worshipped. The phrase appears many times in Qur'an to establish Allah's ownership and dominion, as in 2:284 and 10:68. In al-Naḥl, it comes after command not to take two gods and to be in awe of Me alone, to give reason: to Him belongs everything, so devotion should be to Him alone.

**Everlasting Devotion To Him Alone**

Wa-lahu al-dīnu wāṣiban — and to Him alone is everlasting devotion, religion, obedience. Dīn here means devotion, obedience, religion, submission, as well as recompense. Wāṣiban means everlasting, perpetual, constant, obligatory, from w-ṣ-b, to be constant, lasting, also to be sick, but here means perpetual. Al-Ṭabarī explains wāṣiban as always, perpetually, everlasting, meaning devotion to Allah is perpetual, not temporary, and also that Allah's religion is everlasting, not abrogated in its principle of tawḥīd. Al-Zamakhsharī says wāṣiban means constant, obligatory, everlasting, indicating that devotion to Allah is not for a time then stops, but is perpetual, and that Allah's dominion is perpetual. The phrase wa-lahu al-dīnu wāṣiban therefore means everlasting devotion belongs to Him alone, always, not only in hardship.

**Will You Fear Other Than Allah?**

A-fa-ghayra Allāhi tattaqūn — will you then fear other than Allah? Fa- is for consequence, then, so. Ghayra Allāhi, other than Allah. Tattaqūn from w-q-y, to fear, be mindful, guard, same root as taqwā. The question is rhetorical, expecting no, you should not fear other than Allah. Al-Ṭabarī says this questions why you fear idols, tyrants, or others besides Allah when everything belongs to Him and everlasting devotion is His. Fear should be of Allah alone, as previous verse said be in awe of Me alone. Al-Rāzī notes that fearing other than Allah is shirk in fear, which is part of worship. If you fear other than Allah as you fear Allah, or more, you have taken other as god. The question exposes irrationality: you fear idols that belong to Allah and cannot harm or benefit, while not fearing Allah who owns everything and whose devotion is everlasting.

**Why Ownership Implies Exclusive Fear**

If everything belongs to Allah, then nothing can harm or benefit without His permission, so fear should be of Him alone. Al-Qurṭubī explains that ownership includes power to dispose: owner can give, withhold, harm, benefit. If Allah owns everything, then only He can truly harm or benefit, so only He should be feared. Idols that are owned cannot harm or benefit independently. This is same argument as in 10:106-107: *Do not call besides Allah what cannot benefit nor harm you* (10:106) and *If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him* (10:107). The verse therefore links ownership to fear: because He owns all, fear Him alone.

**Connection To Blessings And Hardship**

Next verses will say whatever blessings you have are from Allah, then when hardship touches you, to Him alone you cry, then when He removes hardship, some associate others. The present verse about ownership and everlasting devotion and fearing other than Allah prepares for that: blessings are from Owner, hardship removal is from Owner, so fear and devotion should be to Owner alone. The sūrah's blessings catalogue showed that all blessings are from Allah; this verse says all things belong to Him, so devotion should be everlasting to Him. The question will you fear other than Allah anticipates that in hardship you cry to Him alone, showing you know He is Owner, but after hardship you associate others, which is inconsistent.

**Linguistic Note On Dīn And Wāṣib**

Mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi, whatever in heavens and earth, general. Lahu, to Him. Dīn from d-y-n means religion, devotion, obedience, recompense, debt, from same root as dayn, debt, indicating that devotion is debt owed to Allah. Wāṣiban from w-ṣ-b means constant, perpetual, everlasting, from waṣaba, to be constant, to last. Some lexicons say wāṣib means obligatory, constant, from same root as waṣab, to be sick, but meaning here is perpetual. A-fa-ghayra Allāhi tattaqūn, will you fear other than Allah, with a-fa- for interrogative and consequence, ghayra for other than, tattaqūn from w-q-y, to fear, guard. The verse's structure — to Him belongs whatever in heavens and earth, to Him is everlasting devotion, will you fear other than Allah — moves from ownership to devotion to question, creating argument: because He owns all and devotion is His perpetually, you should not fear other than Him.

**How This Verse Teaches Tawḥīd In Fear**

Fear is part of worship, as hope and love are. To fear other than Allah as you fear Allah, or to fear other more than Allah, is to give to other what belongs to Allah alone. Al-Rāzī notes that tattaqūn here includes both fear of punishment and fear of displeasure, which should be directed to Allah alone. If you fear idol's displeasure, you have taken it as god. The verse teaches to fear Allah alone, which frees from fear of creation. When you fear One who owns all, you do not need to fear those owned. This is liberation: many fears collapse into one fear that is reverent awe, which brings peace, as angels fear Lord above and do what commanded, and are not too proud.

**A Lesson On Everlasting Devotion**

Everlasting devotion, al-dīnu wāṣiban, indicates that devotion to Allah is not seasonal, not only in hardship, but constant. In world, people often remember Allah in hardship and forget in ease, as next verses will describe. But devotion should be wāṣib, perpetual, everlasting. Al-Qurṭubī says wāṣiban also means that Allah's religion, tawḥīd, is everlasting, not abrogated, from first messenger to last. The core call worship Allah and shun ṭāghūt is everlasting, as verse 36 said messenger to every community. Therefore devotion to Him alone should be everlasting, not temporary. The question will you fear other than Allah then becomes: will you give temporary devotion to others while everlasting devotion belongs to Him alone?

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:53

> **Whatever blessings you have are from Allah. Then whenever hardship touches you, to Him ˹alone˺ you cry ˹for help˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**All Blessings From Allah**

Wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin fa-min Allāhi — and whatever blessings you have are from Allah. Mā bikum, whatever is with you, whatever you have. Min niʿmatin, of blessing, favor. Fa-min Allāhi, then it is from Allah. The particle mā is general, includes all blessings: faith, health, family, provision, cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains, guidance. Min niʿmatin, of blessing, indicates any blessing, even small. Fa-min Allāhi, then from Allah, with fa- for consequence, then, so. Al-Ṭabarī explains that all blessings you have, whether you recognize or not, are from Allah, not from idols, not from self, not from chance. This is summary of blessings catalogue in verses 5-18: all those blessings are from Allah. The verse states it explicitly to remove any misattribution. Al-Rāzī notes that min niʿmatin is indefinite, indicating even one blessing is from Allah, let alone many, and that fa-min Allāhi with fa- emphasizes that whenever you consider a blessing, you will find it is from Allah.

**Hardship Makes You Cry To Him Alone**

Thumma idhā massakum al-ḍurru fa-ilayhi taj'arūn — then when hardship touches you, to Him alone you cry for help. Thumma, then, after blessings. Idhā massakum al-ḍurru, when hardship, harm, distress touches you, from m-s-s, to touch, and ḍ-r-r, harm, hardship. Fa-ilayhi taj'arūn, then to Him you cry loudly, from j-ʾ-r, to cry out, to moan, to supplicate loudly in distress. Al-Ṭabarī says when hardship like illness, poverty, drought, fear touches you, you cry to Allah alone for help, forgetting idols, because you know deep down that only Allah can remove hardship. This is natural disposition, fiṭrah, that emerges in distress. Al-Qurṭubī notes that even polytheists, when in distress at sea, cry to Allah alone, as 17:67 says *When harm touches you at sea, those you call upon besides Him vanish, except Him* (17:67). The verse describes human reality: in ease, you may attribute blessings to others, but in hardship, you return to Allah alone.

**Why Blessings And Hardship Are Paired**

Blessings and hardship are paired to show that both should lead to Allah alone. Blessings are from Allah, so gratitude should be to Him alone. Hardship removal is by Allah alone, so supplication should be to Him alone. In both ease and hardship, Allah is source. The verse says whatever blessings you have are from Allah, then when hardship touches you, to Him you cry. The sequence is blessings then hardship then crying to Him, showing that both states point to Him. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that fa-ilayhi taj'arūn, to Him you cry, with ilayhi, to Him, indicates exclusive direction: to Him alone, not to idols. The brackets ˹alone˺ and ˹for help˺ in translation clarify exclusivity and purpose. The verse therefore teaches tawḥīd in both gratitude and supplication.

**The Inconsistency That Follows**

Next verse will say then as soon as He removes hardship, a group associates others with Lord. The present verse sets up that inconsistency: you know blessings from Allah, you cry to Him alone in hardship, but after hardship removed, you associate others. This is inconsistent and ungrateful. The sūrah's theme of gratitude vs ingratitude appears here: to attribute blessings to other than Allah and to cry to Him alone only in hardship then associate after is ingratitude, kufr, covering favor. Al-Rāzī says this is common human pattern: in ease, heedless; in hardship, supplicating; after hardship removed, heedless again. The verse exposes pattern to make reader aware and change.

**A Verse That Summarizes Sūrat al-Niʿam**

This verse summarizes Sūrat al-Niʿam's core message: all blessings from Allah, so be grateful to Him alone; hardship removal from Allah, so cry to Him alone. It is often quoted as proof that all blessings are from Allah, and as reminder to be grateful. Al-Qurṭubī says it is also proof against those who attribute blessings to stars, idols, or self, as some Arabs said we were given rain by such-and-such star, or attributed provision to own effort. The verse says fa-min Allāhi, from Allah, to correct all misattribution. The phrase wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin, whatever blessings you have, is comprehensive, leaving no blessing outside Allah's giving.

**Linguistic Note On Niʿmah And Ḍurr And Ja'r**

Niʿmah from n-ʿ-m means blessing, favor, from same root as naʿīm, bliss. Mā bikum min niʿmatin, whatever blessing is with you. Fa-min Allāhi, then from Allah. Ḍurr from ḍ-r-r means harm, hardship, distress, from same root as ḍarar, harm. Massakum from m-s-s, to touch, indicating hardship touches, reaches. Taj'arūn from j-ʾ-r means to cry loudly, to moan, to supplicate in distress, from same root as ju'ār, loud crying. The verb taj'arūn indicates loud, desperate crying, not quiet supplication, showing intensity of hardship. The verse's structure — whatever blessings you have are from Allah, then when hardship touches you, to Him you cry — with two clauses, blessings and hardship, both leading to Allah, creates concise summary of tawḥīd in both ease and hardship, inviting gratitude in ease and supplication in hardship, both directed to Allah alone.

**How This Verse Teaches Gratitude**

Gratitude, shukr, requires recognizing that blessings are from Allah, as this verse says. If you think blessings from self or idols, you will not be grateful to Allah. The verse says wa-mā bikum min niʿmatin fa-min Allāhi, whatever blessings you have are from Allah, to teach recognition. Then it says in hardship you cry to Him alone, showing that deep down you know He is source of relief. The lesson is to make conscious what is already known in hardship: that Allah is source of blessings and relief, so gratitude and supplication should be to Him alone always, not only in hardship. The believer who lives by this verse will be grateful in ease and patient in hardship, both with Allah alone as focus.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:54

> **Then as soon as He removes the hardship from you, a group of you associates ˹others˺ with their Lord ˹in worship˺,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Human Pattern Of Forgetting After Relief And Its Warning**

Thumma idhā kashafa al-ḍurra ʿankum idhā farīqun minkum bi-rabbihim yushrikūn — then when He removes hardship from you, behold, a group of you associates others with their Lord. This verse describes a pattern that appears throughout Qur'an and human experience: in hardship you turn to Allah alone, in ease you forget. Al-Ṭabarī explains that when Allah removes hardship that made them cry to Him alone, a group returns to associating idols, forgetting that Allah alone removed hardship. The pattern is described in 10:12 where after harm removed he passes on as if he never called Us, and in 17:67 where after being saved at sea they turn away on land. The verse uses idhā twice to show suddenness: when He removes, behold, a group associates. The suddenness indicates ingratitude and heedlessness. The sūrah's theme of gratitude vs ingratitude appears here: to associate after being saved is to return favour with ingratitude, as next verse says only returning Our favours with ingratitude. The verse exposes human inconsistency: in hardship tawḥīd, in ease shirk, and calls to consistency in all states. The lesson is that true faith remains after relief, not only during distress.

**Why A Group Is Specified And Not All**

Farīqun minkum, a group of you, indicates not all who were in hardship associate after, but some. Al-Qurṭubī says this is to be just: not all who cry to Allah in hardship return to shirk after; some remain grateful and mindful. The verse says farīqun to indicate association after ease is not inevitable but done by some who are blameworthy. Al-Rāzī notes that farīqun minkum also indicates group is from among those who experienced hardship and cried to Allah, so their association is more blameworthy because they just experienced mercy in removal, yet attribute to others. The phrase also shows that even among those who experienced hardship and relief, some remain grateful, some become ungrateful, so outcome depends on heart, not only experience. This distinction is important for justice: not all are condemned, only group who associates. It also gives hope: you can be among those who remain grateful after relief, not among group who associates.

**The Meaning Of Returning Favours With Ingratitude And Its Consequence**

Li-yakfurū bi-mā ātaynāhum — to be ungrateful for what We gave them. Yakfurū from k-f-r means to cover, be ungrateful, disbelieve, from same root as kāfir, one who covers. Bi-mā ātaynāhum, for what We gave them, from a-t-y, to give. Al-Ṭabarī explains that li-yakfurū indicates consequence: they associate after hardship removed so that they are ungrateful for what We gave, they cover blessings, deny Giver. Favours include all blessings listed earlier in sūrah — cattle, rain, crops, sea, mountains — and removal of hardship. To return favours with ingratitude is worst ingratitude: Giver gives, receiver denies Giver and gives thanks to other. Al-Rāzī notes li- here can be for consequence, meaning association results in ingratitude. The verse therefore diagnoses association after relief as ingratitude, not only theological error but moral failure that contradicts purpose of blessings which is to be recognized and thanked.

**Enjoyment As Temporary Test Followed By Soon Knowing The Reality**

Fa-tamattaʿū fa-sawfa taʿlamūn — so enjoy yourselves, you will soon know. Tamattaʿū from m-t-ʿ means to enjoy temporary pleasure, to have enjoyment for a time. Fa-sawfa taʿlamūn, you will soon know consequence, from ʿ-l-m, to know, with sawfa indicating near future, soon. Al-Ṭabarī says fa-tamattaʿū is threat, not permission: enjoy now in world with ingratitude, but soon you will know consequence in Hereafter, when disgrace and misery are upon disbelievers as verse 27 said, when evil consequences overtake as verse 34 said. Enjoyment is temporary, as 16:117 says brief enjoyment then painful punishment. Phrase fa-sawfa taʿlamūn appears many times as warning that knowledge of consequence will come soon, when too late to change. Al-Zamakhsharī notes tamattaʿū is imperative but means threat: enjoy if you can, but you will soon know that enjoyment was short and punishment near.

**How This Pattern Fits Sūrat al-Niʿam And Its Call To Gratitude**

Sūrat al-Niʿam is about blessings and gratitude, called Sūrat al-Niʿam by some early scholars because it enumerates blessings systematically. This pattern of hardship, supplication, relief, association shows opposite of gratitude: blessings from Allah, yet share given to idols, hardship removed by Allah, yet association returns. The sūrah lists blessings as signs for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, but some use blessings to assign share to idols, showing they did not reflect. The verse about returning favours with ingratitude and enjoying soon knowing warns that misuse of blessings has consequence and will be questioned, as verse 56 says you will be questioned about lies fabricated. The reader is invited to be among those who attribute blessings to Allah alone and are grateful, not among those who assign share to idols and fabricate lies. The sūrah weaves warning and promise to cultivate mindful, humble, grateful heart.

**Practical Lessons On Consistency And Sincere Worship In All States**

The verse teaches consistency: worship Allah alone in hardship and ease, in blessings and hardship removal, not only in hardship. Al-Rāzī says true tawḥīd is to worship Allah alone in all states, not only when in need. Group who associates after relief shows their tawḥīd in hardship was need-based supplication that disappears when need disappears. True tawḥīd remains after need removed, with gratitude. Believer should strive to be among those who do not associate after relief, who remain grateful, who say goodness when asked what Lord revealed, who are greeted with peace at death. The lesson is to make conscious what is known in hardship: Allah is source of blessings and relief, so gratitude and supplication should be to Him alone always, not only in hardship. The wise person knows now before soon knowing when too late, and acts on that knowledge by being grateful and worshipping Allah alone in all states.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:55

> **only returning Our favours with ingratitude. So enjoy yourselves, for you will soon know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Human Pattern Of Forgetting After Relief And Its Warning**

Thumma idhā kashafa al-ḍurra ʿankum idhā farīqun minkum bi-rabbihim yushrikūn — then when He removes hardship from you, behold, a group of you associates others with their Lord.
