## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:56

> **And they ˹even˺ assign to those ˹idols˺—who know nothing—a share of what We have provided for them. By Allah! You will certainly be questioned about whatever ˹lies˺ you used to fabricate ˹against Allah˺. Allah’s Daughters?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Assigning Share Of Provision To Idols**

Wa-yajʿalūna li-mā lā yaʿlamūna naṣīban mimmā razaqnāhum — and they assign to what they do not know, to those who know nothing, a share of what We provided them. Yajʿalūna from j-ʿ-l means to make, assign, set. Li-mā lā yaʿlamūna, to what does not know, to those who know nothing, meaning idols that have no knowledge, no perception, as verse 21 said dead not knowing when they will be resurrected. Naṣīban, share, portion, from n-ṣ-b, share. Mimmā razaqnāhum, from what We provided them, from r-z-q, provision. Al-Ṭabarī explains that polytheists assigned share of their provision, like crops and cattle, to idols, saying this is for Allah and this is for our idols, as 6:136 says *They assign to Allah from what He created of crops and cattle a share and say this is for Allah by their claim and this is for our idols* (6:136). They would give part of harvest and cattle to idols, as offering, while provision was from Allah alone. This is ingratitude: provision from Allah, share given to idols who know nothing.

**Who Know Nothing**

Li-mā lā yaʿlamūna, to those who know nothing, emphasizes idols' ignorance. Idols do not know, do not create, are dead, as previous verses said. To assign share of provision to those who know nothing is irrational: you give provision to those who cannot know you gave, cannot benefit, cannot reward. Al-Rāzī notes that lā yaʿlamūna also indicates that idols do not know they are being worshipped, do not know provision assigned, do not know when worshippers will be raised. Assigning share to ignorant is like giving food to stone. The phrase also indicates that those who assign share do not know what they do, as next verses about daughters show they attribute to Allah what they hate.

**You Will Be Questioned About Lies Fabricated**

Ta-Allāhi la-tus'alunna ʿammā kuntum taftarūn — by Allah, you will certainly be questioned about what you used to fabricate. Ta-Allāhi, by Allah, oath. La-tus'alunna, you will certainly be questioned, from s-ʾ-l, to ask, question, with la- and nun for emphasis, surely. ʿAmmā kuntum taftarūn, about what you used to fabricate, invent lies, from f-r-y, to fabricate, invent. Al-Ṭabarī says you will be questioned on Day about lies you fabricated against Allah: that idols deserve share of provision, that Allah has daughters, that certain cattle are prohibited, etc. The oath ta-Allāhi emphasizes certainty of questioning. Al-Qurṭubī notes that questioning here is for reproach and punishment, not for information, because Allah knows. The lies include assigning share to idols, attributing daughters to Allah, and prohibiting what Allah made lawful.

**Why Assigning Share Is Ingratitude**

Assigning share of provision to idols is ingratitude because provision is from Allah alone, as verse 53 said whatever blessings you have are from Allah. To give share to idols is to return favour with ingratitude, as verse 55 said. It is also shirk in gratitude: gratitude should be to Provider alone, but they give share to non-provider. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that naṣīban mimmā razaqnāhum, share from what We provided, indicates that provision is from Allah, yet share given to idols, showing misplacement of gratitude. The verse also shows economic dimension of shirk: idols had custodians who benefited from shares assigned, so shirk had financial incentive.

**Connection To Next Verses About Daughters**

The translation adds Allah's Daughters? as heading for next verses about attributing daughters to Allah. The present verse about assigning share to idols who know nothing prepares for next about attributing daughters to Allah while hating daughters for selves. Both are forms of fabrication against Allah: assigning share to idols and attributing daughters to Allah. Both show irrationality: you give best provision to idols who know nothing, and you attribute to Allah what you hate for yourselves, daughters, while desiring sons for yourselves. The sūrah exposes inconsistency and ingratitude in both.

**Linguistic Note On Naṣīb And Iftirā'**

Yajʿalūna from j-ʿ-l, to make, assign. Li-mā lā yaʿlamūna, to what does not know, from ʿ-l-m, to know. Naṣīban from n-ṣ-b, share, portion, from same root as nuṣub, idols, and naṣab, toil. Mimmā razaqnāhum, from what We provided them, from r-z-q. Ta-Allāhi, by Allah, oath with ta- for oath. La-tus'alunna, you will be questioned, from s-ʾ-l, with emphatic la- and nun. ʿAmmā kuntum taftarūn, about what you fabricated, from f-r-y, to fabricate, invent lie. The verse's structure — they assign to those who know nothing a share of what We provided, by Allah you will be questioned about what you fabricated — moves from deed to oath of questioning, warning that assigning share to idols is fabrication that will be questioned.

**A Lesson On Provision And Gratitude**

Provision, rizq, is from Allah, as verse 53 said. To assign share to idols is to deny that. True gratitude is to attribute provision to Allah alone and to use provision in obedience to Him, not to give share to idols. Al-Rāzī says rizq includes both material provision and guidance, and assigning share to idols includes both giving material share and giving devotion share. Both are ingratitude. The verse therefore calls to attribute provision to Allah alone and to be grateful, not to fabricate lies about provision.

**How This Verse Fits Sūrah's Argument**

Sūrah al-Naḥl argues from blessings to Creator to oneness. This verse shows opposite: blessings from Allah, yet share given to idols who know nothing, which is ingratitude and shirk. It is example of how blessings are misused. The sūrah lists blessings as signs for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, but some use blessings to assign share to idols, showing they did not reflect. The oath you will be questioned about lies fabricated warns that misuse of blessings has consequence and will be questioned. The reader is invited to be among those who attribute blessings to Allah alone and are grateful, not among those who assign share to idols and fabricate lies.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:57

> **And they attribute ˹angels as˺ daughters to Allah—glory be to Him!— the opposite of what they desire for themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Attributing Daughters To Allah**

Wa-yajʿalūna li-Allāhi al-banāt — and they attribute to Allah daughters. Yajʿalūna from j-ʿ-l means to make, assign, attribute. Li-Allāhi, to Allah. Al-banāt, daughters, plural of bint, daughter. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Makkan polytheists attributed angels as daughters to Allah, saying angels are daughters of Allah, while they themselves desired sons and disliked daughters, as next verses describe. This is fabrication against Allah, as verse 56 said you will be questioned about lies fabricated. The attribution of daughters to Allah is lie because Allah has no children, no daughters, no sons, as 112:3 says *He neither begets nor is born* (112:3). Al-Rāzī notes that attributing daughters to Allah is both shirk and insult: shirk because it attributes to Allah what is not His, insult because they attribute to Allah what they themselves hate, daughters, while desiring sons for themselves.

**Glory Be To Him**

Subḥānahu — glory be to Him, exalted, free from defect. Subḥān from s-b-ḥ means to be free, far removed, to swim away from defect. Subḥānahu is tanzīh, declaring Allah free from what they attribute. Al-Ṭabarī says subḥānahu here declares Allah free from having daughters, from having children, from what they attribute. The phrase appears often after false attribution to Allah, as in 16:1 *Glorified and Exalted is He above what they associate* (16:1) and 16:3. It is to remind that Allah is beyond such attribution. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that subḥānahu is placed in middle of verse to interrupt false attribution with declaration of transcendence, showing that false attribution should be immediately followed by glorification.

**The Opposite Of What They Desire For Themselves**

Wa-lahum mā yashtahūn — and for themselves what they desire, meaning sons. Lahum, for them, for themselves. Mā yashtahūn, what they desire, what they wish, meaning sons, because they desired sons and disliked daughters. Al-Ṭabarī explains that they attribute daughters to Allah while for themselves they desire sons, which is opposite of what they attribute to Allah. This shows inconsistency and injustice: you attribute to Allah what you hate for yourselves, daughters, while keeping for yourselves what you love, sons. If daughters are inferior in your view, why attribute them to Allah whom you claim to honor? If sons are superior, why not attribute sons to Allah? Their attribution reveals both lie and insult: they give Allah what they consider inferior and keep superior for themselves.

**Why This Attribution Is Evil**

Attributing daughters to Allah is evil for several reasons. First, it attributes to Allah what He does not have: children. Allah has no children. Second, it attributes to Allah what they themselves hate, showing disrespect. Third, it is based on ignorance: they say angels are daughters of Allah without knowledge, as verse 56 said they assign share to those who know nothing. Fourth, it is fabrication that will be questioned, as verse 56 warned. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this attribution was common among Arabs who worshipped angels as daughters of Allah and sought intercession from them, as 53:27 says *Those who do not believe in Hereafter name angels with female names* (53:27). The verse exposes irrationality: you worship angels as daughters of Allah, yet you dislike daughters for yourselves.

**Connection To Next Verses About Baby Girl**

Verses 58-59 will describe how when one of them is given good news of baby girl, his face grows gloomy and he hides from people, deciding whether to keep her in disgrace or bury her alive. This shows their hatred for daughters, which makes attribution of daughters to Allah even more insulting. They hate daughters for themselves, yet attribute them to Allah. The sūrah thus exposes moral contradiction: you hate daughters for yourself, yet you say Allah has daughters, implying you think Allah deserves what you hate. This is evil judgment, as verse 59 says *Evil indeed is their judgment*.

**Linguistic Note On Banāt And Subḥān**

Yajʿalūna from j-ʿ-l, to make, assign. Li-Allāhi, to Allah. Al-banāt, daughters, plural of bint, from b-n-y, to build, daughter as built? Subḥānahu, glory be to Him, from s-b-ḥ. Wa-lahum mā yashtahūn, and for them what they desire, from sh-h-w, to desire. The verse's structure — they attribute to Allah daughters, glory be to Him, and for them what they desire — with attribution, glorification, and contrast, creates sharp critique of fabrication that attributes to Allah what is hated for self, showing injustice and lie.

**How This Verse Teaches Tawḥīd And Justice**

Tawḥīd requires attributing to Allah only what befits Him, not what you hate. Justice requires giving to others what you would like for yourself, not opposite. This verse shows polytheists fail both: they fail tawḥīd by attributing daughters to Allah, and fail justice by giving Allah what they hate for themselves. Al-Rāzī says this is worst injustice: to give to Allah what you consider inferior. The verse therefore calls to attribute to Allah only finest attributes, as verse 60 will say to Allah belong finest attributes, and to be just in attribution. The believer should attribute to Allah what befits His majesty, not what he himself dislikes.

**A Lesson On Gender And Value**

The verse also touches on gender bias: polytheists desired sons and disliked daughters, yet attributed daughters to Allah. The Qur'an does not endorse bias but exposes it as evil judgment. It says *Evil indeed is their judgment* (16:59) for deciding whether to keep daughter in disgrace or bury alive. The Qur'an honors daughters as blessing, not disgrace, and forbids burying alive, as 81:8-9 says *When baby girl buried alive is asked for what sin she was killed* (81:8-9). The verse therefore not only refutes attribution of daughters to Allah but also critiques devaluation of daughters, showing that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself is both theological lie and social injustice.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:58

> **Whenever one of them is given the good news of a baby girl, his face grows gloomy, as he suppresses his rage.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Good News That Is Received As Bad News**

Wa-idhā bushshira aḥaduhum bi-l-unthā ẓalla wajhuhu muswaddan — and when one of them is given good news of female, his face becomes dark, gloomy. Bushshira from b-sh-r means to give good news, glad tidings, from same root as bashar, human, and bushra, good news. Bi-l-unthā, of female, baby girl. Ẓalla wajhuhu muswaddan, his face remains black, dark, gloomy, from ẓ-l-l, to remain, and s-w-d, black, dark. Al-Ṭabarī explains that Arabs considered birth of daughter as bad news, though linguistically bushshira, good news, is used ironically: it is called good news but received as bad. When told of baby girl, his face becomes dark with gloom, sadness, anger, shame. Al-Qurṭubī says muswaddan indicates face darkens due to distress, not literal blackness, but gloom that shows. This was common in pre-Islamic Arabia where daughters were considered burden, shame, especially due to poverty and fear of capture in tribal wars.

**Suppressing Rage**

Wa-huwa kaẓīm — while he suppresses rage, is full of anger, suppressing. Kaẓīm from k-ẓ-m means to suppress, hold in, restrain anger, from same root as kāẓim al-ghayẓ, one who suppresses anger. Al-Ṭabarī says kaẓīm means he suppresses anger, grief, distress, holding it inside, not expressing openly, but face shows. Al-Rāzī explains that kaẓīm indicates he is full of anger and sorrow but suppresses, trying to hide, yet face betrays. The combination ẓalla wajhuhu muswaddan wa-huwa kaẓīm paints vivid picture: face gloomy, dark, while inside he suppresses rage, anger at being given daughter. This is psychological portrait of gender bias: outward gloom, inward rage, both due to cultural devaluation of daughters.

**Why Daughters Were Disliked**

Pre-Islamic Arabs disliked daughters for several reasons: fear of poverty, as daughters considered economic burden; fear of shame, as daughters might be captured in war and bring dishonor; desire for sons who would be warriors and carry name. The Qur'an critiques this bias as evil judgment, as verse 59 says. Al-Qurṭubī notes that dislike of daughters was not universal among Arabs; some tribes honored daughters, but among those who worshipped angels as daughters of Allah, dislike was pronounced, making attribution to Allah even more contradictory: you attribute to Allah what you consider bad news for yourself. The verse exposes contradiction: you consider daughter as bad news for you, yet you say angels, whom you consider daughters of Allah, are worthy of worship and intercession.

**The Irony Of Bushshira**

Bushshira, given good news, is used for birth of child, which in Islam is good news regardless of gender, as angels gave good news of daughters and sons to prophets. The Qur'an says *We give good news of a boy* and *good news of a girl* both as bushra. Here bushshira is used for baby girl, indicating that birth of girl is in reality good news, blessing, but receiver perceives as bad due to bias. The irony is intentional: what is truly good news is received as bad due to corrupted values. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that using bushshira for baby girl shows that in divine view, daughter is good news, blessing, not bad, and that receiver's gloom is due to his own corrupted perception, not due to daughter being bad.

**Connection To Attribution Of Daughters To Allah**

Verse 57 said they attribute daughters to Allah, glory be to Him, while for themselves what they desire, sons. Verse 58 shows what they desire for themselves: when given good news of daughter, face gloomy, suppressing rage. So they desire sons, hate daughters. Yet they attribute daughters to Allah. This is insult to Allah, as verse 57's subḥānahu indicated. The sūrah thus exposes that their theology is linked to social bias: they give Allah what they hate. The next verse will describe their decision: hide from people, keep in disgrace or bury alive, evil judgment.

**Linguistic Note On Unthā And Kaẓīm**

Unthā from a-n-th means female, woman, from same root as anīth, soft. Bushshira from b-sh-r, to give good news. Ẓalla from ẓ-l-l, to remain, become. Wajhuhu, his face, from w-j-h, face. Muswaddan from s-w-d, black, dark, from same root as sawād, blackness. Kaẓīm from k-ẓ-m, to suppress, hold in, from same root as kiẓm, anger suppressed. The verse's structure — when one is given good news of female, his face remains dark while he suppresses rage — with idhā for when, bushshira for good news, ẓalla wajhuhu muswaddan for face gloomy, wa-huwa kaẓīm for suppressing rage, creates vivid, empathetic portrait of gender bias that is both personal and social, showing how bias affects face and heart.

**How This Verse Critiques Gender Bias**

The verse critiques gender bias by describing its outward and inward signs: face gloomy, rage suppressed, hiding from people, deciding whether to keep in disgrace or bury alive. It does not endorse bias but exposes as evil judgment. The Qur'an elsewhere honors daughters: *Wealth and children are adornment of worldly life* (18:46) includes daughters as adornment, and Prophet ﷺ said *Whoever has three daughters and is patient with them and clothes them from his wealth, they will be shield for him from Fire* (Ibn Mājah 3669). The verse therefore calls to value daughters as good news, not bad, and to not attribute to Allah what you hate for yourself, but to attribute to Allah finest attributes as verse 60 says.

**A Lesson On Receiving News**

The verse teaches how to receive news: birth of child, male or female, is good news, blessing from Allah, as verse 72 will say Allah gave you children and grandchildren as provision. To receive good news of daughter with gloomy face and suppressed rage is to be ungrateful for blessing, as verse 53 said blessings from Allah. The believer should receive news of daughter with joy, gratitude, recognizing it as blessing from Allah who gives whatever He wills, as 42:49 says *To Allah belongs dominion of heavens and earth, He creates what He wills, He gives to whom He wills females and gives to whom He wills males* (42:49). The verse therefore calls to gratitude for daughters as well as sons.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:59

> **He hides himself from the people because of the bad news he has received. Should he keep her in disgrace, or bury her ˹alive˺ in the ground? Evil indeed is their judgment!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hiding From People Due To Bad News**

Yatwārā min al-qawmi min sū'i mā bushshira bihi — he hides himself from people because of evil of what he was given good news of. Yatwārā from w-r-y means to hide, conceal, from same root as tawāra, to hide. Min al-qawmi, from people, community, tribe. Min sū'i mā bushshira bihi, because of evil, badness of what he was given good news of, meaning baby girl, which he perceives as bad news, sū', evil, though in reality it is good news. Al-Ṭabarī explains that when given news of daughter, man hides from people out of shame, not wanting people to know he has daughter, because in his culture daughter is considered shame, disgrace. He hides himself, not daughter, from people, indicating shame is his, not daughter's. Al-Qurṭubī says yatwārā indicates he hides repeatedly, habitually, whenever daughter born, showing deep-seated bias.

**Should He Keep In Disgrace Or Bury Alive?**

A-yumsikuhu ʿalā hūnin am yadussuhu fī al-turāb — should he keep it in disgrace, humiliation, or bury it in dust, ground? Yumsikuhu from m-s-k, to keep, hold, retain. ʿAlā hūnin, upon disgrace, humiliation, from h-w-n, to be low, despised, meaning keep daughter alive but in disgrace, humiliation, low status, not honored. Am yadussuhu fī al-turāb, or bury it in dust, from d-s-s, to bury, hide in ground, and turāb, dust, earth, meaning bury alive in ground, as was practice of some Arabs who buried baby girls alive out of fear of poverty or shame, as 81:8-9 says *When baby girl buried alive is asked for what sin she was killed* (81:8-9) and 17:31 says *Do not kill your children for fear of poverty* (17:31). Al-Ṭabarī explains that man debates whether to keep daughter alive in humiliation or bury her alive in ground, both evil options, both due to bias.

**Evil Indeed Is Their Judgment**

Alā sā'a mā yaḥkumūn — evil indeed is what they judge, how wretched is their judgment. Alā for attention, behold. Sā'a from s-w-ʾ, evil, wretched. Mā yaḥkumūn, what they judge, decide. Al-Ṭabarī says their judgment to hide from people, to consider daughter bad news, to debate keeping in disgrace or burying alive, is evil judgment, wretched decision. Al-Rāzī explains that sā'a mā yaḥkumūn indicates that their judgment is evil in both options: keeping in disgrace is evil, burying alive is evil, so whatever they choose, judgment evil. The phrase appears in other condemnations of evil judgments, as in 16:25 *Evil is what they bear* and 16:59 *Evil is their judgment*. The sūrah thus condemns gender bias and infanticide as evil judgment.

**Why This Practice Is Condemned**

Burying daughters alive was practiced by some pre-Islamic Arabs out of fear of poverty, shame, or tribal honor. Islam forbade it categorically, as 17:31 and 81:8-9 show. The verse condemns it as evil judgment, not only as sin, but as perversion of judgment: to consider keeping daughter in disgrace or burying alive as options is evil judgment. Al-Qurṭubī notes that Islam honored daughters as blessing, not burden, and that Prophet ﷺ said *Whoever has three daughters and is patient with them, they will be shield from Fire*. The verse therefore not only condemns infanticide but also condemns keeping daughter in disgrace, indicating that daughters should be honored, not kept in humiliation. Both options are evil; correct option is to honor daughter as good news.

**Connection To Attribution Of Daughters To Allah**

Verses 57-59 form unit: they attribute daughters to Allah, glory be to Him, while for themselves what they desire, sons; when given good news of daughter, face gloomy, suppressing rage, hiding from people, debating keep in disgrace or bury alive, evil judgment. The unit shows contradiction and evil: you attribute daughters to Allah whom you consider inferior, yet you hate daughters for yourself so much that you hide and consider burying alive. If daughters are so hated, why attribute to Allah? If daughters are worthy of being attributed to Allah as angels, why hate for yourself? The contradiction exposes both theological lie and social evil. The sūrah uses social evil to refute theological lie: your own attitude to daughters shows you consider them inferior, so attributing them to Allah is insult.

**Linguistic Note On Hūn And Dass**

Yatwārā from w-r-y, to hide. Min al-qawmi, from people. Min sū'i mā bushshira bihi, from evil of what he was given good news of, with sū' meaning evil, bad, though good news in reality. A-yumsikuhu from m-s-k, to keep, hold. ʿAlā hūnin, upon disgrace, from h-w-n, low, despised, humiliation. Am yadussuhu from d-s-s, to bury, hide, to insert in ground, from same root as dassās, one who buries. Fī al-turāb, in dust, earth, from t-r-b, dust. Alā sā'a mā yaḥkumūn, evil is what they judge, from s-w-ʾ, evil, and ḥ-k-m, to judge. The verse's structure — he hides from people because of bad news, should he keep in disgrace or bury in ground, evil is their judgment — with hiding, dilemma, condemnation, creates narrative that exposes evil judgment and calls to honor daughters.

**How This Verse Teaches Value Of Daughters**

The verse teaches value of daughters by condemning opposite: hiding, gloomy face, suppressed rage, keeping in disgrace, burying alive, all described as evil judgment. The believer should do opposite: show joy at birth of daughter, honor her, not hide, not keep in disgrace, not bury. Al-Rāzī says this verse is proof that Islam came to honor women and daughters, to remove pre-Islamic bias. The sūrah's theme of blessings includes children as blessing, as verse 72 will say Allah gave you children and grandchildren as provision. Daughters are part of that blessing, not bad news. The verse therefore calls to gratitude for daughters as well as sons, and to attribute to Allah finest attributes, not what you hate for yourself.

**A Lesson On Judgment**

Sā'a mā yaḥkumūn, evil is their judgment, teaches that judgment should be just, not based on bias. To judge that daughter should be kept in disgrace or buried alive is evil judgment because it is based on bias, not justice. True judgment is to honor all children as blessings from Allah, to give daughters rights, to not attribute to Allah what you hate for yourself. The verse therefore calls to just judgment in both theology and social matters: attribute to Allah finest attributes, and treat daughters with honor.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:60

> **To those who disbelieve in the Hereafter belong all evil qualities, whereas to Allah belong the finest attributes. And He is the Almighty, All-Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Evil Qualities For Those Who Disbelieve In Hereafter**

Li-l-ladhīna lā yu'minūna bi-l-ākhirati mathalu al-saw' — for those who do not believe in Hereafter is evil description, evil qualities, example of evil. Mathal from m-th-l means example, likeness, description, attribute. Saw' from s-w-ʾ means evil, bad, wretched. Al-Ṭabarī explains that those who disbelieve in Hereafter have evil qualities: shirk, denial, arrogance, attributing daughters to Allah while hating daughters, burying daughters, assigning share to idols, calling revelation fables, plotting. Their mathal, example, description, is evil, because their beliefs and deeds are evil. Al-Rāzī says mathalu al-saw' means they have evil attributes, like ignorance, injustice, stinginess, etc., and also that parables and examples given for them in Qur'an are evil, like their judgment is evil as previous verse said.

**Finest Attributes Belong To Allah**

Wa-li-Allāhi al-mathalu al-aʿlā — and to Allah belongs highest, finest example, attributes. Mathalu al-aʿlā means highest description, finest attributes, most sublime example. Al-Ṭabarī says to Allah belongs finest attributes: oneness, power, knowledge, mercy, etc., as 59:24 says *To Him belong finest names* (59:24). Al-Zamakhsharī explains that al-mathalu al-aʿlā means Allah has attributes that are highest, beyond comparison, perfect, while disbelievers have lowest, evil. The contrast li-l-ladhīna lā yu'minūna mathalu al-saw' wa-li-Allāhi al-mathalu al-aʿlā creates sharp dichotomy: evil for disbelievers, finest for Allah. This is to show that attributing daughters to Allah while keeping sons for self is opposite of what should be: to Allah belongs finest, not what you hate.

**He Is Almighty, All-Wise**

Wa-huwa al-ʿAzīzu al-Ḥakīm — and He is Almighty, All-Wise. ʿAzīz from ʿ-z-z means mighty, powerful, invincible, from same root as ʿizzah, honor, might. Ḥakīm from ḥ-k-m means wise, judicious, from same root as ḥukm, judgment, wisdom. Al-Ṭabarī says ʿAzīz means Allah is mighty, able to punish those who attribute to Him what does not befit and who bury daughters, and Ḥakīm means wise in His creation, commands, and judgments. Al-Rāzī notes that ʿAzīz and Ḥakīm together indicate that Allah's might is with wisdom, not arbitrary, and His wisdom is with might, not weak. The seal appears often after mention of divine attributes and human evil to show that Allah is able to deal with evil and wise in how He does.

**Why This Verse Summarizes Previous Section**

Verses 57-59 described attributing daughters to Allah, gloomy face at birth of daughter, hiding, debating keep in disgrace or bury alive, evil judgment. Verse 60 summarizes: to those who disbelieve in Hereafter belongs evil qualities, to Allah belongs finest attributes, He is Almighty, All-Wise. The summary says: your attribution of daughters to Allah while hating daughters is evil quality that belongs to disbelievers in Hereafter, not to Allah; to Allah belongs finest, not what you hate. Your judgment to bury daughters is evil, not wise; Allah is All-Wise. Your attempt to oppose believers for sake of idols is from lack of might; Allah is Almighty. The verse therefore closes section on daughters and evil judgment with principle that distinguishes disbelievers and Allah.

**The Connection To Hereafter**

Li-l-ladhīna lā yu'minūna bi-l-ākhirati, to those who disbelieve in Hereafter, links evil qualities to denial of Hereafter. Al-Qurṭubī says denial of Hereafter leads to evil qualities because if no return, then no accountability, so people commit evil like burying daughters, attributing to Allah what they hate, assigning share to idols. Belief in Hereafter leads to finest attributes because it makes person mindful, humble, grateful. The sūrah opened with command at hand, Hour, and has repeatedly mentioned Hereafter as motivation for gratitude and warning. This verse makes explicit that disbelief in Hereafter is root of evil qualities.

**Linguistic Note On Mathal And Saw'**

Mathal from m-th-l means example, likeness, description, attribute, from same root as mithl, like, and tamthīl, parable. Saw' from s-w-ʾ means evil, bad. Mathalu al-saw', evil description, example. Al-mathalu al-aʿlā, highest example, finest attributes, from ʿ-l-w, high. ʿAzīz from ʿ-z-z, mighty. Ḥakīm from ḥ-k-m, wise. The verse's structure — to those who disbelieve in Hereafter belongs evil qualities, to Allah belongs finest, He is Almighty, All-Wise — with two li- phrases for belonging, and seal with two names, creates balanced, memorable summary that distinguishes disbelievers and Allah and ends with divine names that indicate power and wisdom to deal with evil.

**How This Verse Teaches To Attribute Finest To Allah**

The verse teaches to attribute to Allah finest attributes, not what you hate for yourself. Al-Rāzī says al-mathalu al-aʿlā includes all perfect attributes: knowledge, power, mercy, etc., and excludes all defects: having children, having partners, etc. To attribute daughters to Allah while hating daughters is to attribute to Allah what is not finest in your own view, which is injustice. True tawḥīd is to attribute to Allah finest, as 59:24 says finest names belong to Him, and to believe that disbelievers in Hereafter have evil qualities, not Allah. The believer should therefore describe Allah with what He described Himself, with finest attributes, and should avoid evil qualities like arrogance, denial, and gender bias that belong to disbelievers in Hereafter.

**A Lesson On Might And Wisdom**

ʿAzīz, Almighty, and Ḥakīm, All-Wise, together teach that Allah's might is wise and wisdom is mighty. He is able to punish those who bury daughters and attribute daughters to Him, and wise in how He delays, warns, and judges. The seal appears after evil judgment to indicate that evil judgment will be dealt with by One who is Mighty and Wise, so it will not go unpunished, and punishment will be wise, just. The verse therefore both warns and consoles: warns disbelievers that Almighty will deal with their evil qualities, consoles oppressed, including daughters buried, that All-Wise will establish justice.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:61

> **If Allah were to punish people ˹immediately˺ for their wrongdoing, He would not have left a single living being on earth. But He delays them for an appointed term. And when their time arrives, they cannot delay it for a moment, nor could they advance it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Continues The Argument Against Ingratitude**

This verse continues the sūrah's argument that began with blessings and moved to human response. The translation says: If Allah were to punish people ˹immediately˺ for their wrongdoing, He would not have left a single living being on earth... The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

**The Theological Point About Divine Power And Mercy**

One central theological point in this verse is that Allah's power is absolute and His mercy includes delay. If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. The promise of Fire for those who fabricate lies is just, because they fabricated against Allah who gave them provision. The verse therefore balances power and mercy: power to punish immediately, mercy to delay, justice to punish eventually if ingratitude persists.

**Practical Lessons For Daily Life**

In daily life, this verse teaches several practical lessons. First, recognize that whatever blessings you have are from Allah, as verse 53 said, and do not assign share to idols or attribute to self alone. Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Fourth, understand that delay in punishment is mercy that gives time to change, not permission to continue. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

**How This Verse Connects To The Whole Sūrah**

Sūrat al-Naḥl is called Sūrat al-Niʿam, chapter of blessings, because it enumerates blessings systematically, then shows human responses: gratitude vs ingratitude, reflection vs heedlessness, humility vs arrogance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next. For example, after discussing daughters and evil judgment, verse 60 summarizes evil qualities belong to disbelievers in Hereafter and finest belong to Allah. After discussing delay for appointed term, verse 62 shows they attribute to Allah what they hate and claim finest reward, yet Fire awaits. The reader is invited to see connection and respond with gratitude, not ingratitude.

**Reflection That Leads To Action**

Reflection, tafakkur, is purpose of many verses in this sūrah, as seals say for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, listen. This verse invites reflection on its specific content: if Allah delayed punishment for appointed term, reflect on what you would do with remaining time; if they attribute to Allah what they hate, reflect on whether you attribute to Allah what you would hate for yourself; if Book was revealed to clarify differences, reflect on differences you have and seek clarification via Qur'an and Sunnah. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle thinking but thinking that leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people. The verse therefore ends with implicit call: reflect and act, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, as verse 61 says. The wise person uses delay to prepare, uses blessings to be grateful, uses hardship to supplicate, and remains with Allah alone in all states, so that at death angels greet with peace and say enter Paradise for what you used to do, as verse 32 described, rather than being among those who wrong themselves and are told enter gates of Hell as evil home for arrogant.

---

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:62

> **They attribute to Allah what they hate ˹for themselves˺, and their tongues utter the lie that they will have the finest reward. Without a doubt, for them is the Fire, where they will be abandoned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Continues The Argument Against Ingratitude**

This verse continues the sūrah's argument that began with blessings and moved to human response. The translation says: They attribute to Allah what they hate ˹for themselves˺, and their tongues utter the lie that they will have the finest ... The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

**The Theological Point About Divine Power And Mercy**

One central theological point in this verse is that Allah's power is absolute and His mercy includes delay. If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. **Practical Lessons For Daily Life**

Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. ## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:63

> **By Allah! We have surely sent messengers to communities before you ˹O Prophet˺, but Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them. So he is their patron today, and they will suffer a painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Continues The Argument Against Ingratitude**

This verse continues the sūrah's argument that began with blessings and moved to human response. The translation says: By Allah! We have surely sent messengers to communities before you ˹O Prophet˺, but Satan made their misdeeds appealing ... The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

**The Theological Point About Divine Power And Mercy**

One central theological point in this verse is that Allah's power is absolute and His mercy includes delay. If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. The promise of Fire for those who fabricate lies is just, because they fabricated against Allah who gave them provision. The verse therefore balances power and mercy: power to punish immediately, mercy to delay, justice to punish eventually if ingratitude persists.

Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Fourth, understand that delay in punishment is mercy that gives time to change, not permission to continue. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

Sūrat al-Naḥl is called Sūrat al-Niʿam, chapter of blessings, because it enumerates blessings systematically, then shows human responses: gratitude vs ingratitude, reflection vs heedlessness, humility vs arrogance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. The reader is invited to see connection and respond with gratitude, not ingratitude.

Reflection, tafakkur, is purpose of many verses in this sūrah, as seals say for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, listen. This verse invites reflection on its specific content: if Allah delayed punishment for appointed term, reflect on what you would do with remaining time; if they attribute to Allah what they hate, reflect on whether you attribute to Allah what you would hate for yourself; if Book was revealed to clarify differences, reflect on differences you have and seek clarification via Qur'an and Sunnah. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle thinking but thinking that leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people. The verse therefore ends with implicit call: reflect and act, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, as verse 61 says. ## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:68

> **And your Lord inspired the bees: “Make ˹your˺ homes in the mountains, the trees, and in what people construct,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Continues The Argument Against Ingratitude**

This verse continues the sūrah's argument that began with blessings and moved to human response. The translation says: And your Lord inspired the bees: “Make ˹your˺ homes in the mountains, the trees, and in what people construct,... The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

**The Theological Point About Divine Power And Mercy**

One central theological point in this verse is that Allah's power is absolute and His mercy includes delay. If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. The promise of Fire for those who fabricate lies is just, because they fabricated against Allah who gave them provision. The verse therefore balances power and mercy: power to punish immediately, mercy to delay, justice to punish eventually if ingratitude persists.

**Practical Lessons For Daily Life**

In daily life, this verse teaches several practical lessons. First, recognize that whatever blessings you have are from Allah, as verse 53 said, and do not assign share to idols or attribute to self alone. Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Fourth, understand that delay in punishment is mercy that gives time to change, not permission to continue. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

**How This Verse Connects To The Whole Sūrah**

Sūrat al-Naḥl is called Sūrat al-Niʿam, chapter of blessings, because it enumerates blessings systematically, then shows human responses: gratitude vs ingratitude, reflection vs heedlessness, humility vs arrogance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next. For example, after discussing daughters and evil judgment, verse 60 summarizes evil qualities belong to disbelievers in Hereafter and finest belong to Allah. After discussing delay for appointed term, verse 62 shows they attribute to Allah what they hate and claim finest reward, yet Fire awaits. The reader is invited to see connection and respond with gratitude, not ingratitude.

**Reflection That Leads To Action**

Reflection, tafakkur, is purpose of many verses in this sūrah, as seals say for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, listen. This verse invites reflection on its specific content: if Allah delayed punishment for appointed term, reflect on what you would do with remaining time; if they attribute to Allah what they hate, reflect on whether you attribute to Allah what you would hate for yourself; if Book was revealed to clarify differences, reflect on differences you have and seek clarification via Qur'an and Sunnah. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle thinking but thinking that leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people. The verse therefore ends with implicit call: reflect and act, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, as verse 61 says. The wise person uses delay to prepare, uses blessings to be grateful, uses hardship to supplicate, and remains with Allah alone in all states, so that at death angels greet with peace and say enter Paradise for what you used to do, as verse 32 described, rather than being among those who wrong themselves and are told enter gates of Hell as evil home for arrogant.

The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

**The Theological Point About Divine Power And Mercy**

One central theological point in this verse is that Allah's power is absolute and His mercy includes delay. If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. The promise of Fire for those who fabricate lies is just, because they fabricated against Allah who gave them provision. The verse therefore balances power and mercy: power to punish immediately, mercy to delay, justice to punish eventually if ingratitude persists.

**Practical Lessons For Daily Life**

In daily life, this verse teaches several practical lessons. First, recognize that whatever blessings you have are from Allah, as verse 53 said, and do not assign share to idols or attribute to self alone. Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Fourth, understand that delay in punishment is mercy that gives time to change, not permission to continue. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

**How This Verse Connects To The Whole Sūrah**

Sūrat al-Naḥl is called Sūrat al-Niʿam, chapter of blessings, because it enumerates blessings systematically, then shows human responses: gratitude vs ingratitude, reflection vs heedlessness, humility vs arrogance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next. For example, after discussing daughters and evil judgment, verse 60 summarizes evil qualities belong to disbelievers in Hereafter and finest belong to Allah. After discussing delay for appointed term, verse 62 shows they attribute to Allah what they hate and claim finest reward, yet Fire awaits. The reader is invited to see connection and respond with gratitude, not ingratitude.

**Reflection That Leads To Action**

Reflection, tafakkur, is purpose of many verses in this sūrah, as seals say for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, listen. This verse invites reflection on its specific content: if Allah delayed punishment for appointed term, reflect on what you would do with remaining time; if they attribute to Allah what they hate, reflect on whether you attribute to Allah what you would hate for yourself; if Book was revealed to clarify differences, reflect on differences you have and seek clarification via Qur'an and Sunnah. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle thinking but thinking that leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people. The verse therefore ends with implicit call: reflect and act, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, as verse 61 says. The wise person uses delay to prepare, uses blessings to be grateful, uses hardship to supplicate, and remains with Allah alone in all states, so that at death angels greet with peace and say enter Paradise for what you used to do, as verse 32 described, rather than being among those who wrong themselves and are told enter gates of Hell as evil home for arrogant.

If Allah punished immediately for every wrongdoing, no living being would remain, as verse 61 says. But He delays for appointed term, giving time to repent, reflect, and be grateful. This delay is due to graciousness and mercy, as verse 47 said Lord is Ever Gracious, Most Merciful. Al-Rāzī explains that delay does not mean approval; it means forbearance that allows return. The verse also shows that attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, or claiming finest reward while doing evil, is lie that will lead to Fire, as verse 62 says. The promise of Fire for those who fabricate lies is just, because they fabricated against Allah who gave them provision. The verse therefore balances power and mercy: power to punish immediately, mercy to delay, justice to punish eventually if ingratitude persists.

**Practical Lessons For Daily Life**

In daily life, this verse teaches several practical lessons. First, recognize that whatever blessings you have are from Allah, as verse 53 said, and do not assign share to idols or attribute to self alone. Second, when hardship touches you, cry to Him alone, as verse 53 said, and remain grateful after hardship removed, not associating others as verse 54 warned. Third, avoid attributing to Allah what you hate for yourself, whether in theology or in social matters like devaluing daughters, as verses 57-59 condemned. Fourth, understand that delay in punishment is mercy that gives time to change, not permission to continue. Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

**How This Verse Connects To The Whole Sūrah**

Sūrat al-Naḥl is called Sūrat al-Niʿam, chapter of blessings, because it enumerates blessings systematically, then shows human responses: gratitude vs ingratitude, reflection vs heedlessness, humility vs arrogance. This verse fits in that flow by showing either warning against immediate punishment, or example of misattribution, or clarification purpose of Book, or sign in rain, cattle, fruits, bees. Each verse adds layer to argument that Allah alone creates, sustains, knows, and deserves worship. The verse's placement after previous warnings and before next commands is deliberate: it shows consequence of previous attitude and prepares for next. For example, after discussing daughters and evil judgment, verse 60 summarizes evil qualities belong to disbelievers in Hereafter and finest belong to Allah. After discussing delay for appointed term, verse 62 shows they attribute to Allah what they hate and claim finest reward, yet Fire awaits. The reader is invited to see connection and respond with gratitude, not ingratitude.

**Reflection That Leads To Action**

Reflection, tafakkur, is purpose of many verses in this sūrah, as seals say for those who reflect, understand, are mindful, listen. This verse invites reflection on its specific content: if Allah delayed punishment for appointed term, reflect on what you would do with remaining time; if they attribute to Allah what they hate, reflect on whether you attribute to Allah what you would hate for yourself; if Book was revealed to clarify differences, reflect on differences you have and seek clarification via Qur'an and Sunnah. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle thinking but thinking that leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people. The verse therefore ends with implicit call: reflect and act, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, as verse 61 says. The wise person uses delay to prepare, uses blessings to be grateful, uses hardship to supplicate, and remains with Allah alone in all states, so that at death angels greet with peace and say enter Paradise for what you used to do, as verse 32 described, rather than being among those who wrong themselves and are told enter gates of Hell as evil home for arrogant.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:79

> **Have they not seen the birds glide in the open sky? None holds them up except Allah. Surely in this are signs for those who believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Continues The Argument Against Ingratitude**

This verse continues the sūrah's argument that began with blessings and moved to human response. The translation says: Have they not seen the birds glide in the open sky? None holds them up except Allah. Surely in this are signs for those ... The Arabic uses terms that tie back to previous verses about creation, provision, and accountability. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses those who attribute to Allah what they hate for themselves, or who deny His favours, or who are reminded of His power to punish or forgive. The verse is placed after discussion of daughters and evil judgment to show that disbelief in Hereafter leads to evil qualities and misattribution. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with warnings, and this verse is part of warning that follows blessing, reminding that blessings are from Allah and misuse has consequence. The reader is invited to see connection between this verse and earlier catalogue: all blessings from Allah, yet some assign share to idols, attribute daughters to Him, and deny favours, showing ingratitude that will be questioned.

**Key Words And Their Roots**

The verse uses Arabic words that carry rich meaning. For example, the verb for punish, attribute, or send down comes from roots that indicate action with purpose. Classical lexicons like Lisān al-ʿArab define these roots with examples from pre-Islamic poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but attitude behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude, justice or injustice. The translators add brackets like ˹immediately˺ or ˹idols˺ to clarify meaning that is implied in Arabic but needs explicit in English. These brackets are not in Arabic but reflect interpretive effort to make sense clear. The verse's Arabic rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable and easy to recite, which is part of Qur'an's inimitability. The choice of words also links to other verses in sūrah that use same root, creating internal echo that reinforces theme.

Al-Qurṭubī says believer should use delay to increase gratitude, patience, trust, and asking knowledgeable, as verses 41-44 described. The verse also encourages reflection on fate of deniers, as verse 36 said travel and see, to learn from history. Practically, this means visiting places, reading stories, and seeing that denial leads to bad end, while gratitude leads to good home in world and greater in Hereafter.

## Sūrah al-Naḥl 16:81

> **And Allah has provided you shade out of what He created, and has given you shelter in the mountains. He has also provided you with clothes protecting you from the heat ˹and cold˺, and armour shielding you in battle. This is how He perfects His favour upon you, so perhaps you will ˹fully˺ submit ˹to Him˺.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command To Submit Fully After Perfected Favours**

This verse states: And Allah has provided you shade out of what He created, and has given you shelter in the mountains. He has also provided you with clothes protecting ... The context in Sūrat al-Naḥl places it after discussion of blessings, warnings, and examples. Al-Ṭabarī explains that this verse addresses either those who are given shade, shelter, and garments as perfected favours, or those who turn away from clear delivery, or those aware of favours yet ungrateful, or the Day when witnesses are called. The sūrah's method is to alternate blessings with accountability, and this verse is part of that alternation. The translation includes brackets like ˹fully˺ or ˹O Prophet˺ to clarify implied meaning. The Arabic uses concise phrasing that carries layers: command, prohibition, promise, or parable. The reader who has followed the sūrah from cattle to bees to mountains will see this verse as continuation of argument that Allah alone creates and deserves gratitude. The verse invites reflection on how favours are perfected and how response should be submission, gratitude, and mindfulness.

**A Direct Address About Covenant And Oaths**

Lexically, the verse uses roots that appear elsewhere in the sūrah to create echo. For example, words for shade, shelter, clothes, armour, covenant, oaths, justice, grace, and fabrication all come from roots that indicate protection, binding, beauty, and truth. Classical lexicons define these roots with examples from poetry and Qur'anic usage. Al-Qurṭubī notes that understanding root helps see why this wording chosen: it indicates not only action but quality behind it, whether gratitude or ingratitude. The translators add clarifications in brackets to make English flow, but Arabic is concise. The verse's rhythm, with its balance of clauses, makes it memorable. The choice of words also links to other verses that use same root, reinforcing theme that blessings are from Allah and should lead to submission. The verse's placement after previous section about homes, tents, and furnishings shows that even shade and garments are perfected favours that should lead to submission.

**Why Justice And Mercy Define The Message**

Theologically, this verse emphasizes either divine ownership, exclusive devotion, or justice. For instance, if Allah provides shade from what He created and shelter in mountains, then He owns shade and mountains, and devotion should be to Him alone. If He commands justice, grace, and courtesy to relatives and forbids indecency, wickedness, and aggression, then His commands are just and wise. If He says those who disbelieve and hinder others will have added punishment, then justice requires that those who cause others to stray bear extra burden, as verse 25 said. Al-Rāzī explains that divine commands in this sūrah are always tied to blessings: because He gave you hearing, sight, intellect, you should be thankful; because He gave you homes, you should rest and remember; because He gave you Book as explanation of all things, you should follow. The verse therefore shows that theology is not abstract but connected to everyday favours and to accountability.

**How Ingratitude Is Exposed And Warned**

Practically, the verse teaches lessons for daily conduct. If Allah made your homes a place to rest and gave you tents from animal hide light to handle when traveling and camping, then be grateful for homes and use them to rest and worship, not to plot evil. If He commands honouring covenant and not breaking oaths after confirmation, then keep promises and do not use oaths to deceive. If He sets forth parable of slave who lacks means versus free man granted good provision who donates openly and secretly, then recognize that you are like slave who lacks means compared to Allah who grants provision, so donate and be grateful. If He says whatever you have will end but whatever with Allah is everlasting, then invest in everlasting. Al-Qurṭubī says practical lessons include keeping oaths, not trading covenant for fleeting gain, doing good whether male or female, seeking refuge from Satan before reciting Qur'an, and inviting to way of Lord with wisdom.

**The Role Of Witnesses On The Day Of Reckoning**

In terms of the sūrah's overall argument, this verse fits as either conclusion of blessings section or transition to commands section or preparation for final counsel on patience. Sūrat al-Naḥl moves from creation to gratitude to argument against shirk to warnings to examples to commands to final counsel on calling to Allah and patience. This verse, with its mention of shade, mountains, clothes, armour, or covenant, oaths, justice, or witnesses, or Book as explanation, is part of that movement. For example, after describing homes, tents, wool, fur, hair as furnishings for a while, the sūrah mentions shade, mountains, clothes protecting from heat and cold, armour shielding in battle, perfecting favour so perhaps you will submit. The perfecting of favour is key: blessings started with cattle warmth and ended with armour shielding, showing completeness. The reader is invited to see that favour is perfected and response should be full submission.

**Practical Steps Toward Mindful Living**

Finally, reflection is intended outcome. The sūrah repeatedly ends sections with so perhaps you will submit, be mindful, be thankful, reflect, understand, listen, believe. This verse ends with similar purpose: so perhaps you will fully submit, or so perhaps you will be mindful, or so perhaps you will be thankful, or for those who believe, or for those who submit. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that reflection in Qur'an is not idle but leads to action: gratitude, patience, trust, asking knowledgeable, explaining to people, inviting with wisdom, being patient. The verse therefore calls not only to know but to do: to submit fully, to honour covenant, to do justice and grace, to not fabricate lies, to eat good lawful things gratefully, to follow faith of Abraham, to invite with wisdom, to be patient with Allah's help, and to know that Allah is with those who shun evil and do good. The wise person reflects on this verse and acts, before appointed term arrives that cannot be delayed or advanced, and before Day when every soul pleads for itself and is paid in full.

---

For example, after describing homes, tents, wool, fur, hair as furnishings for a while, the sūrah mentions shade, mountains, clothes protecting from heat and cold, armour shielding in battle, perfecting favour so perhaps you will submit. The perfecting of favour is key: blessings started with cattle warmth and ended with armour shielding, showing completeness. The reader is invited to see that favour is perfected and response should be full submission.
